# Part III, The Fire Sermon: the debate record

Ethan, 20 September 2026, on the live site:

> really interesting but you have a major language issue in the work, which is that all of the commentary is written in the voice of an LLM "Where the poem is standing" and "The poem's argument is a weather report: rain that is unwelcome, rivers that are dirty, a sea that drowns, and then twenty-nine lines of rock before three words of rain." set up Eliot scholar agents and Reader agents to debate every section to make sure it is clear,, good in its wording, and real

This thread took the commentary hung on lines 173 to 311, the site's count, which is Part III, The Fire Sermon: the nineteen glosses, the eleven ways in, the twenty-eight pathway stops that fall in the part, and the three records on the drafts page. It also read, as proposals only, the sixteen library records for the part's sources and the twelve notes the Voices lens gives the part's speakers, since those files belong to the thread on the front matter and notes; the proposals are in shared-proposals-part3.md beside this file, with the recurring headings.

## How the debate ran

Two sides read every piece. The scholar, briefed on the 1971 facsimile, Ricks and McCue, Southam, Rainey, Grover Smith, Kenner, Gardner, Gordon and Crawford, asked whether each piece is true, whether it says what is known and how it is known, whether a reading is passed off as fact, and what is wrong or missing; it checked what it could reach online (the 1922 Boni and Liveright text on archive.org, Gutenberg, Wikisource, Perseus, Froude's Elizabeth, Bullen's Day, Warren's Buddhism in Translations, the London County Council's pamphlet of 1920, the Prayer Book psalter). The reader, an intelligent person who came to read the poem and is not a specialist, asked whether each piece is clear, whether it helps with the line, whether a person would say it, and whether it is showing off. The pieces were split into four batches, with a scholar and a reader for each: A, the glosses from line 176 to 218; B, the glosses from 222 to 307; C, the ways, the pathway stops and the drafts records; D, the library records and the voice notes.

Round 1: each side reported on every piece without seeing the other's report. Round 2: the editor rewrote each piece, taking the scholar's corrections of fact and the reader's cuts and wording, wrote down each decision, and sent the rewrite to both sides, who answered Accept or Object, item by item. Round 3: the editor folded the objections in, sent the final text to both sides for a last look (the scholar for errors of fact the rewording might have introduced, the reader for the sentences the editor had altered), and took their last corrections. On substance the scholar had the last word; on wording the reader. Three rounds were the limit. What was not settled is listed below.

The standard both sides were given is printed at the end of this file. The round files as they were written are in the directory part3-rounds beside this file.

Fixed fields (ids, line numbers, anchors, kinds, sources, paths) were not touched; the import script's check was run on the edited files.

### What the scholars corrected

- leman: Eliot's line follows the Prayer Book psalter ("By the waters of Babylon we sat down and wept"), not the King James "rivers"; the quotation field now carries the Prayer Book verse.
- at-my-back: the 1922 notes really do print 196 for Day and 197 for Marvell, the wrong way round; confirmed on two copies of the first edition.
- fishing-canal: Ferdinand's father is not drowned, only believed so; "wrack" to "wreck" in the quotation; the Fisher King fishes again at 424–425, not 424.
- tereu: Lyly's songs were first printed in 1632, not with the play in 1584, and whether Lyly wrote them has long been doubted; Eliot names no source for "jug jug" and "tereu", so Lyly is the editors' candidate.
- eugenides: no boat trains left Cannon Street after 1914; Smyrna was a dried-fruit port, not London's currant supplier; "usual place" and "daily" were unsupported; Weston counts Syrian merchants among the carriers of the mystery cults, not "first through" them; the burning of Smyrna (September 1922) postdates the poem and is gone from the gloss and the Cities stop.
- violet-hour: the Part V lines are 373 and 380 in the site's count (Eliot's 372 and 379).
- tiresias: the note's claim dated to the notes of December 1922, a year after the scene was typed; the 1956 "bogus scholarship" remark added; Pound's "make up yr. mind" placed beside the typist's "one half-formed thought may pass", which the printed "allows" bears out, and "Perhaps be damned" beside "Perhaps a cheap house agent's clerk".
- typist: the stays are on the divan, not out of the window; the quatrains hold from 231 to 246 and loosen before and after, so "the scene keeps its quatrains" was wrong.
- lovely-woman: Goldsmith's lines have four beats and Eliot's five, so "in the same meter" was wrong; the Vicar found Olivia at an inn, cast off by the squire, weeks before the song.
- strand: it is the music that goes along the Strand, not a walker; St Magnus's body was complete by 1676 and the steeple added in 1705 ("rebuilt 1671–87" was wrong); the Phillimore commission reported in April 1920 and the Council's pamphlet of October 1920 finds the interior "disappointing", which is what Eliot's note answers; the fishmen "lounge", they do not "drink"; Billingsgate closed in 1982.
- thames-daughters: Greenwich Reach and the Isle of Dogs placed; the barges' red-brown sails and the sprit as the "heavy spar" confirmed.
- elizabeth-leicester: the quotation field carried Eliot's note, not Froude; it now carries Froude's words as the Everyman page has them; the letter is De Quadra's of 30 June 1561; Amy Robsart died in September 1560.
- highbury: La Pia's identification is the early commentators', and nothing certain is known of her.
- margate: Eliot was at Margate from late October to mid-November 1921, not "October"; the shelter is the listing's identification (2009), not Eliot's, who wrote only "a shelter on the front"; "some fifty lines" is Eliot's own figure, in the letter to Schiff of 4 November; "Nothing with nothing" as Eliot's own confession is Lyndall Gordon's reading, not a fact.
- carthage: "Eliot read it at Harvard in Warren" is an assumption, not a fact; the series is the Harvard Oriental Series, as Eliot's note says.
- ways: "See who speaks" claimed Part III has the most quotation marks; it has fewer than Parts I or II. "Every bird" said every song is a complaint, which the hermit-thrush's is not, and had the cock before the thrush. "Tiresias in Ovid" sent the reader to the library for nineteen lines it does not hold (it has 324–331). "Margate" said Eliot wrote "much of this section" there; his own figure is some fifty lines.
- pathway stops: "Smyrna, which burned in September 1922" cut; "Elizabeth ... talking nonsense" made the note's own words, "began to talk nonsense"; "where the poem was written" at Leman corrected to "where the poem was finished" (Parts IV and V).
- drafts: the passage addressed to London stood before the typist scene, not after it; the dots at 248 fall where the clerk's exit stood; Pound's marks on the Thames-daughters were "OK" and "echt".
- library: Verlaine's line 7 reads "sous la tente", not "sa tente"; Sappho's two lines come from two sources, Demetrius and the Etymologicum Magnum, not Demetrius alone; Ferdinand's speech is quoted once and adapted once, not quoted twice; in Day "the spring" is the water Diana bathes in; the earliest surviving edition of Day is the quarto of 1641; the Rhine-daughters' refrain is not printed anywhere in Eliot's two-line form; Ovid's Book VI record had no passage, and lines 667–674 with Miller's translation are supplied.

### What the readers threw out

Every note carried at least one of the marks Ethan named. The readers cut, and the editor followed, among others:

- the antitheses: "Spenser's river is a procession; Eliot's is the morning after"; "the poem's most private moment and its most public"; "skip the seduction and keep the dying";
- the bow-tying last sentences: "The nightingale's song in this poem is never lovely. It is evidence."; "The fire that burns here is lust. The fire at line 428 refines."; "The mandoline is real; so is the pub"; "given to a building on a street of fish"; "closes the sixteenth century"; "The song runs down to 'la la' and stops";
- the counting flourishes: "Four lines, three birds' worth of noise, then the name"; "a sentence, a word four times, a prayer, a prayer with its end torn off, one word alone"; "two ascetics, one word between them";
- the metaphors for the poem: "a water-rhythm"; "the river's own noise takes over"; "the rhymes are doing the judging"; "This is the poem as music";
- the tour-guide verbs and second person: "Watch the river run through the whole section and then burn"; "Read lines 249–256 once each way"; "Decide whether"; "a season you can feel on your skin"; "so that you can test the claim";
- the superlatives: "the most violent cut in the poem"; "the most argued-over scene in the poem"; "the first unqualified praise of anything in the poem"; "Readers have argued for a century"; "the only mercy in the poem";
- the tour-guide title "Where the poem was written" (now "Margate Sands");
- and facts that were true but did not help with the line: the doctor's name at Leman (kept on the Eliot pathway), the Albemarle Hotel, the steeple's date, the Lyly authorship dispute (kept in the library record), Byron's imitation of Sappho, the day of the month of Verlaine's first printing, the phantom 1607 edition of Day.

The readers also broke up the stacked clauses: most notes were three or four semicolon-and-colon sentences and are now plain ones.

## What was not settled

1. Where Pound's "make up yr. mind you Tiresias" stands in the typescript. The batch A scholar places it beside the typist's line "Across her brain one half-formed thought may pass" (facsimile p. 47, as cited by Zeligs and by Vendler), and the printed line 251, "allows one half-formed thought to pass", bears that out; the batch C scholar's memory put it beside the loiterers' lines. The gloss and the drafts record follow the batch A placing. The facsimile page should be checked.
2. The wording of Eliot's letter to Schiff of 4 November 1921. The batch B scholar cites the Letters, as quoted by Kent Maps Online and the facsimile's introduction, for "a rough draft of part of part III"; the Guardian of 11 July 2009 prints "part III". The gloss quotes "part of part III". The Letters, volume 1, should settle it.
3. The Margate leaf's first version, with "the pressure of dirty hand" (facsimile p. 53), is reported at second hand only and was left out of every text; and Rainey's statement that the twelve-line Margate leaf was unrevised sits oddly beside the reported revision. Not used.
4. Rainey's dating of lines 173–182 to Paris, January 1922, is printed as "On Rainey's dating"; the batch C scholar's memory of the leaves supports it; the facsimile was not seen.
5. That Eliot "shortened" the first Thames-daughter's speech rests on three memories of the facsimile that agree; no figures are given.
6. Goldsmith's song: the site prints "soothe" and "is—to die"; the Gutenberg text has "sooth" and "bosom—is to die". Editions differ. The gloss and the library record carry the same text, and the site thread should settle both together or leave both.
7. The one eye explained by the profile faces on playing cards: kept as "sometimes explained", with no critic named; the attribution was from memory.
8. "Leman" as a lover: "critics have noted"; the batch A scholar recalls Southam and Ricks and McCue, unverified.
9. Whether Eliot met the Fire Sermon at Harvard, and in Warren: "generally assumed"; the one account of the Harvard course (Kearns, from Eliot's course notes) has the class reading the Pali with Warren recommended, and is unverified.
10. A 1643 issue of Day's Parliament of Bees is listed in the Early English Books microfilms and was not examined; "the earliest edition that survives is the quarto of 1641" is the wording.
11. Pound's "OK" and "echt" on the Thames-daughters are not placed by line; memory.
12. The Nayland Rock shelter's listing: 2009, confirmed from English Heritage's news item as cited; the listing text itself was behind a verification wall.


## The notes, one by one

Each entry gives the text as exported, the two round-one reports as written, the editor's decisions and the two sides' answers in rounds two and three, and the text as it now stands.

## The glosses, 1 to 10 (batch A)

### sweet-thames (lines 176–184)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: Spenser's wedding river
quote: Sweete Themmes runne softly, till I end my Song.
body: The refrain of Spenser's <i>Prothalamion</i> (1596), a "spousal verse" for a double wedding in which nymphs gather flowers on the Thames bank and two swans float down to Essex House. Spenser's river is a procession; Eliot's is the morning after. "The nymphs are departed" and so have "their friends, the loitering heirs of city directors," the young men of the summer, leaving no bottles, papers or cigarette ends because the season is over. October: "the last fingers of leaf / Clutch and sink into the wet bank." Part of the section was drafted at Margate in the autumn of 1921, and it is the only part of the poem with a season you can feel on your skin.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.

Errors:
- "Part of the section was drafted at Margate in the autumn of 1921" is true of Part III (Rainey: the Margate drafts are "O City, City", "The river sweats", "Highbury bore me", "On Margate Sands", lines 259-311; Eliot to Schiff, "some fifty lines") but placed here it says this passage was, and it was not: on Rainey's dating the ten opening lines were the last thing written, in Paris, 5-10 January 1922, after Pound had cut the Fresca opening (Revisiting, pp. 31-32, via ebin.pub; not verified against the facsimile).
- "October:" The poem names no month. Margate was late October to 12 November (Rainey p. 29 for the departure; arrival about 22 October from memory of Letters vol. 1, unverified), and these lines were not written there. Cut.
- Spenser details are right: "Spousall Verse" for the double marriage of the Earl of Worcester's daughters; nymphs gather flowers; the two swans (the brides) come down to "a stately place" where the poet once had the favour of "that great lord" (Leicester House, by 1596 Essex House), and "two gentle knights" meet them (en.wikisource.org/wiki/Prothalamion, via The Works of Edmund Spenser).

Readings passed off as fact:
- "Spenser's river is a procession; Eliot's is the morning after." Antithesis; annotator's.
- "the young men of the summer, leaving no bottles ... because the season is over": the usual reading (Southam, from memory), stated as fact.
- "it is the only part of the poem with a season you can feel on your skin": uncheckable, second person.

Missing: that the refrain returns at 183-184; the Paris dating, since the body raises the drafts at all.

Cut: the antithesis; "October:"; the last sentence entire.

Proposed body:
The refrain of Spenser's <i>Prothalamion</i> (1596), a "spousal verse" for the double wedding of the Earl of Worcester's daughters: the poet walks by the Thames, nymphs gather flowers for the brides, and two swans swim down to Essex House to meet the bridegrooms. Eliot keeps the refrain (again at 183–184) and empties the river. "The nymphs" and "their friends, the loitering heirs of city directors" are usually taken as the couples of the summer, gone with the season, and the litter of summer nights gone with them. On Rainey's dating of the drafts these opening lines were the last written for the poem, in Paris in January 1922, after Pound had cut the long passage in couplets about Fresca that had stood at the head of Part III.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Rewrite.

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- "Spenser's river is a procession; Eliot's is the morning after." Balanced antithesis (one of the owner's own examples).
- "a season you can feel on your skin." Second-person flourish (also one of the owner's examples).
- "it is the only part of the poem with a season" Superlative nobody could check, and I do not believe it: Part I has April, lilacs, winter and a summer shower.
- "October:" Fragment with a colon. The poem says leaves are falling; it does not say October.

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- The two swans floating down to Essex House do nothing for line 176.
- "Part of the section was drafted at Margate in the autumn of 1921" belongs at line 300 ("On Margate Sands"), not here.
- "leaving no bottles ... because the season is over" tells me what lines 177 to 179 already say.

**Keep**
- That the line is the refrain of the Prothalamion (1596), a wedding poem ("spousal verse" is Spenser's own phrase and can stay).
- That Spenser's nymphs gather flowers on the Thames bank, so that "The nymphs are departed" means those nymphs.
- That "their friends, the loitering heirs of city directors" are the young men of the summer. This is the one thing in the note I could not have worked out alone.

**Proposed body**
Title unchanged.

The refrain of Spenser's <i>Prothalamion</i> (1596), a "spousal verse" for a double wedding, in which nymphs gather flowers on the Thames bank on a summer day. Eliot's nymphs and "their friends, the loitering heirs of city directors," are the girls and young men of a summer now over, and the litter that was its "testimony" has gone with them.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** Spenser's poem summarised as both sides had it; the antithesis, "October:" (the poem names no month) and "a season you can feel on your skin" cut. The draft history (Margate; Rainey's Paris dating of 173–182) is moved out of this gloss to the drafts record "fresca", where the section's opening is the subject; the reader is right that it does not help at 176.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "Eliot keeps the refrain (again at 183–184); his nymphs "are departed"." A semicolon stacking a fragment, and the quotation tells me nothing I have not just read; the nymphs as the girls of the summer, which both sides had in round 1, have dropped out, so "their friends" now hangs on nothing. Replace the last two sentences with: "Eliot repeats the refrain at 183–184. His nymphs and "their friends, the loitering heirs of city directors" are usually taken as the girls and young men of the summer, gone with the season, and the litter of summer nights with them."

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's last two sentences taken; "repeats the refrain at 183–184" made exact (183 repeats it, 184 varies it, as the Spenser way says).

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** Accept. Line 183 repeats 176 and 184 varies it; the sentence is exact and plain.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Spenser's wedding river
quote: Sweete Themmes runne softly, till I end my Song.
body: The refrain of Spenser's <i>Prothalamion</i> (1596), a "spousal verse" for a double wedding, in which the poet walks by the Thames and sees nymphs gathering flowers for two swans that stand for the brides. Eliot repeats the refrain at 183 and varies it at 184. His nymphs and "their friends, the loitering heirs of city directors" are usually taken as the girls and young men of the summer, gone with the season, and the litter of summer nights with them.

### leman (line 182)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: Babylon, Geneva, and a lover
quote: By the rivers of Babylon, there we sat down, yea, we wept, when we remembered Zion.
body: Psalm 137, the exiles' psalm, with one word changed. Leman is Lac Léman, Lake Geneva, on whose shore at Lausanne Eliot spent November and December 1921 under the care of Dr Roger Vittoz, writing Parts IV and V. "Leman" is also an old English word for a lover or mistress. The line is the poem's most private moment and its most public: a man on sick leave from a bank, weeping by a Swiss lake, in the words of a people carried off to Babylon.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.

Errors:
- "with one word changed": wrong. Eliot's line follows the Prayer Book psalter, "By the waters of Babylon we sat down and wept" (Coverdale; the 1662 and 1928 books are the same here, episcopalnet.org/1928bcp/Psalter/Ps137.html), not the King James "By the rivers of Babylon, there we sat down, yea, we wept" that the quote field gives. Against the Prayer Book two words change, Leman for Babylon and "I" for "we"; against the King James, four or five. The quote field should carry the Prayer Book wording or the body must say which version.
- "November and December 1921": Rainey p. 23 gives 24 November 1921 to 2 January 1922, Hotel Ste Luce, Lausanne. Say "late November 1921 to 2 January 1922".
- "writing Parts IV and V": Rainey pp. 31-32: the autograph fair copies of IV and V, plus a seventeen-line passage meant for III, were made there. Acceptable as "Parts IV and V were written there".
- The body implies the line was written by the lake. On Rainey's dating the section's opening ten lines, this among them, were written in Paris in the days after Eliot left Lausanne (pp. 31-32; unverified against the facsimile). State as his.

Readings passed off as fact:
- "The line is the poem's most private moment and its most public": antithesis for its own sake.
- "a man on sick leave from a bank, weeping by a Swiss lake": a scene inferred from the biography, told as if witnessed.
- "'Leman' is also an old English word for a lover or mistress": true of the word (OED, leman: lover, mistress); that Eliot meant it is a reading (Ricks and McCue note the sense, from memory).

Missing: which psalter; that he was on leave from Lloyds Bank (granted 12 October 1921, three months; from memory of Valerie Eliot's introduction, unverified) is worth one clause since it explains the stay.

Cut: the last sentence.

Proposed body:
Psalm 137 in the Prayer Book wording, "By the waters of Babylon we sat down and wept" (the King James has "rivers"), with Leman for Babylon and "I" for "we". Leman is Lac Léman, Lake Geneva. Eliot was at Lausanne, on its shore, from late November 1921 to 2 January 1922, on sick leave from Lloyds Bank and under the care of Dr Roger Vittoz; Parts IV and V were written there. On Rainey's dating of the drafts this line, with the rest of the section's opening, was written in Paris in the days after he left. "Leman" is also an old word for a lover or mistress, a second sense critics have noted.

Title: "Psalm 137 and Lake Geneva" (the present one is a list for effect).

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Rewrite.

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- "The line is the poem's most private moment and its most public:" Balanced antithesis plus superlative (one of the owner's examples).
- "a man on sick leave from a bank, weeping by a Swiss lake, in the words of a people carried off to Babylon." Rhythmic list of three tying a bow, and a reading (that the weeper is Eliot) put as a fact.

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- "with one word changed": the quote printed beside it shows three changes (rivers/waters, Babylon/Leman, we/I). I cannot tell whether the claim or the quote is wrong. The Prayer Book wording ("By the waters of Babylon we sat down and wept") looks nearer to Eliot's line; the scholar should decide which to print.
- Dr Roger Vittoz and "writing Parts IV and V" are facts about Lausanne, not about this line.

**Keep**
- Psalm 137, the exiles' psalm.
- Leman is Lake Geneva, and Eliot was at Lausanne on its shore in November and December 1921, on sick leave from the bank. Without this the line is just an odd place name.
- "Leman" is an old English word for a lover or mistress. Short, checkable, and it changes how I hear the line.

**Proposed body**
Plainer title: Psalm 137 at Lake Geneva

Psalm 137, the psalm of the exiles in Babylon, with Babylon changed to Leman. Lac Léman is Lake Geneva; Eliot spent November and December 1921 at Lausanne on its shore, on sick leave from Lloyds Bank, and worked on the poem there. "Leman" is also an old English word for a lover or mistress.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** the scholar's correction stands: Eliot's line follows the Prayer Book psalter, not the King James, so the quote field changes to the Prayer Book wording and the body says which. Lausanne dates as the scholar gave them. Rainey's dating of this line to Paris, after Lausanne, is left out: nothing in the rewrite implies the line was written by the lake, and the reader does not need the point; it goes in the record. "Leman" as lover kept, marked as a second sense critics note. The antithesis and the "weeping by a Swiss lake" sentence cut.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "Eliot was at Lausanne, on its shore, from late November 1921 to early January 1922, on sick leave from Lloyds Bank and under the care of Dr Roger Vittoz; Parts IV and V were written there." The doctor's name does not help with the line, and the semicolon tacks a second fact on. Replace with: "Eliot was at Lausanne, on its shore, from late November 1921 to early January 1922, on sick leave from Lloyds Bank and under a doctor's care. Parts IV and V were written there." If the scholar wants the name, keep it, but still break the sentence.

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's cut of the doctor's name taken (it stays on the Eliot pathway at 182, where the biography is the point); the sentence broken.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Psalm 137 and Lake Geneva
quote: By the waters of Babylon we sat down and wept: when we remembered thee, O Sion.
body: Psalm 137 in the Prayer Book wording, "By the waters of Babylon we sat down and wept" (the King James Bible has "rivers"), with Leman for Babylon and "I" for "we". Leman is Lac Léman, Lake Geneva. Eliot was at Lausanne, on its shore, from late November 1921 to early January 1922, on sick leave from Lloyds Bank and under a doctor's care. Parts IV and V were written there. "Leman" is also an old word for a lover or mistress, a second sense critics have noted.

### at-my-back (lines 185–186)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: Time's chariot, twice
quote: But at my back I always hear<br>Time’s wingèd chariot hurrying near;<br>And yonder all before us lie<br>Deserts of vast eternity.
body: Marvell, "To His Coy Mistress" (published 1681). Eliot's note attaches it to line 196 (numbered 197 in the 1922 notes, a slip), where the phrase comes round again and turns into Sweeney's motor horns; here, first time through, what the speaker hears at his back is not a chariot but "the rattle of the bones, and chuckle spread from ear to ear," a skull's grin. Marvell's poem is a seduction: hurry, because we die. Eliot's borrowings from it, both times, skip the seduction and keep the dying.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs hedging.

Errors:
- None of fact. The numbering claim is confirmed: the 1922 notes carry the Day and Marvell notes with each other's numbers, 196 Day and 197 Marvell (both archive.org copies above). "A slip" is fair; "swapped" is exacter. Marvell "published 1681" (Miscellaneous Poems) is right; the quote is the standard modernised text.

Readings passed off as fact:
- "a skull's grin": reading of "chuckle spread from ear to ear", unowned.
- "Marvell's poem is a seduction: hurry, because we die." The "X is a Y:" pattern; the content is fine.
- "Eliot's borrowings from it, both times, skip the seduction and keep the dying." Antithesis named in the standard.
- "turns into Sweeney's motor horns": metaphor for "is followed by".

Missing: nothing a reader of this line needs. (Eliot's TLS essay on Marvell, March 1921, is in the library note and need not be repeated.)

Cut: the last two sentences.

Proposed body:
Marvell, "To His Coy Mistress" (published 1681). Eliot's note attaches the source to line 196, where the phrase comes a second time and what is heard is "horns and motors"; in the 1922 notes the Marvell and Day notes carry each other's numbers, 196 Day and 197 Marvell. Here, the first time, what is heard is "the rattle of the bones", and the bones return at 194–195. In Marvell the sound at the speaker's back is the argument for love now, before the grave.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Rewrite.

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- "Marvell's poem is a seduction: hurry, because we die." The "X is a Y:" opening with a brisk fragment after the colon.
- "skip the seduction and keep the dying." Balanced antithesis as a bow (one of the owner's examples).
- "comes round again and turns into Sweeney's motor horns; here, first time through," Tour-guide narration; the sentence runs on through two semicolons and I read it twice to find out where Eliot's note actually sits.
- "a skull's grin" is fine as an explanation of "chuckle spread from ear to ear"; it is the only metaphor here that explains rather than decorates.

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- "both times, skip the seduction": at line 196 the horns bring Sweeney to Mrs Porter, which looks to me like the seduction in a low form. I cannot tell whether to believe the claim.
- "turns into Sweeney's motor horns" assumes I already know who Sweeney is; he is glossed at 198, so a plain cross-reference would do.

**Keep**
- Marvell, "To His Coy Mistress," 1681, and in one plain clause what the poem is (a man urging a woman to yield because time is short); the quote does the rest.
- That Eliot uses the line twice, here and at 196, and that his note is at the second.
- The 1922 numbering slip, in a few words, because the site prints Eliot's note with the wrong number beside it and I would otherwise think the site had mis-set it.
- The skull's grin.

**Proposed body**
Title unchanged.

Marvell, "To His Coy Mistress" (published 1681), which urges a woman to yield because time is short. Eliot uses the line twice. Here what is heard at the speaker's back is the rattle of bones and a chuckle "spread from ear to ear," a skull's grin; at line 196 it is "the sound of horns and motors." Eliot's note is placed at the second use (numbered 197 in the 1922 notes, one off).

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** the numbering slip kept in a few words (confirmed against the 1922 pages). "A skull's grin" dropped: the reader liked it, the scholar called it an unowned reading, and the quotation carries the sense without it. The seduction/dying antithesis cut.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "in which the speaker urges his mistress to yield" and then "Here what is heard at the speaker's back": two poems' speakers under one name in consecutive sentences. And "the Marvell and Day notes carry each other's numbers, 197 and 196" makes the reader work out which is which. Replace with: "Marvell, "To His Coy Mistress" (published 1681), in which the lover urges his mistress to yield because time is short and the grave is near. Eliot uses the line twice. Here what is heard "at my back" is "the rattle of the bones, and chuckle spread from ear to ear"; at line 196 it is "the sound of horns and motors". Eliot's note gives the source at the second use, line 196. In the 1922 notes it is numbered 197 and the Day note 196, the wrong way round."

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's text taken whole; the numbering checked again against the 1922 pages (196 Day, 197 Marvell).

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Time's chariot, twice
quote: But at my back I always hear<br>Time’s wingèd chariot hurrying near;<br>And yonder all before us lie<br>Deserts of vast eternity.
body: Marvell, "To His Coy Mistress" (published 1681), in which the lover urges his mistress to yield because time is short and the grave is near. Eliot uses the line twice. Here what is heard "at my back" is "the rattle of the bones, and chuckle spread from ear to ear"; at line 196 it is "the sound of horns and motors". Eliot's note gives the source at the second use, line 196. In the 1922 notes it is numbered 197 and the Day note 196, the wrong way round.

### fishing-canal (lines 189–195)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: Ferdinand on the bank, and the Fisher King
quote: Sitting on a bank,<br>Weeping again the king my father’s wrack,<br>This music crept by me upon the waters,<br>Allaying both their fury and my passion<br>With its sweet air.
body: Ferdinand in <i>The Tempest</i>, Act I scene ii, just before Ariel sings "Full fathom five." Eliot's fisherman is both Ferdinand, mourning a drowned king, and Weston's Fisher King, whose wound has dried up his country; and he is doing it "round behind the gashouse," by a canal, in winter, which is not where either of them belongs. The poem adds a brother to Shakespeare's father: "the king my brother's wreck / And on the king my father's death before him." Then the rat again (line 115 sent you here), and the bones in the "little low dry garret," and the white naked bodies on the ground, which are the war dead, or the swimmers of last summer, or both.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs hedging.

Errors:
- None of fact. Ferdinand's speech does come just before "Full fathom five" (I.ii). The quote field reads "wrack" where the library record reads "wreck" (Folio "wracke"; Eliot's line "wreck"); make the two agree.
- The rat and bones link is Eliot's own: his note to line 115 reads "Cf. Part III, l. 195" (1922 text, both archive.org copies). The body treats it as the annotator's connection.

Readings passed off as fact:
- "Eliot's fisherman is both Ferdinand ... and Weston's Fisher King": the second is the standard reading (from the headnote on Weston and line 424), not something Eliot's note says.
- "which is not where either of them belongs": flourish.
- "which are the war dead, or the swimmers of last summer, or both": unowned readings.
- "(line 115 sent you here)": second person.

Missing: Eliot's cross-reference at 115; that the speech returns at 257.

Cut: "which is not where either of them belongs"; "(line 115 sent you here)"; the last clause of readings.

Proposed body:
Ferdinand in <i>The Tempest</i>, I.ii, just before Ariel sings "Full fathom five": "Sitting on a bank, / Weeping again the king my father's wreck". Eliot's note gives the source at line 192, and the speech returns at 257. Eliot's fisherman adds a brother to Shakespeare's father and sits by a canal behind a gashouse in winter; he is usually read as also the Fisher King of Weston's <i>From Ritual to Romance</i>, named in Eliot's headnote, who fishes again at 424. The rat and the bones are Eliot's own cross-reference: his note to "rats' alley" at line 115 reads "Cf. Part III, l. 195".

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Rewrite.

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- "which is not where either of them belongs." Annotator's wit tying off the sentence.
- "Then the rat again (line 115 sent you here)," Tour-guide second person; nothing sent me anywhere.
- "which are the war dead, or the swimmers of last summer, or both." Rhythmic "or ... or both" ending; a reading dressed as fact.
- "Eliot's fisherman is both Ferdinand ... and Weston's Fisher King" Reading put as fact. Ferdinand is in the line; the Fisher King is in Weston and in Eliot's prefatory note.

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- Whose reading is "the swimmers of last summer"? I cannot tell whether to believe it. The war dead I have met before in this poem (line 115); the swimmers are new and unsourced.
- "mourning a drowned king": Ferdinand's father is not drowned, he only thinks so. Costs nothing to say so.
- The printed quote includes "This music crept by me upon the waters," which is line 257, and the note does not say so. I wanted that.

**Keep**
- Ferdinand, Tempest I ii, on the shore weeping for his father, just before Ariel's "Full fathom five" (which I have met at lines 48 and 125).
- "The poem adds a brother to Shakespeare's father." Plain and exact.
- The Fisher King, as a reading and with a one-clause account of who he is (wounded king, barren land), because Eliot's note on sources points to Weston and the reader needs the connection made once.
- The cross-reference to line 115 and the war dead as the usual reading of the white bodies.

**Proposed body**
Title unchanged.

Ferdinand in <i>The Tempest</i>, Act I scene ii, sitting on the shore weeping for the father he believes drowned, just before Ariel sings "Full fathom five"; the same speech gives line 257. Eliot adds a brother: "the king my brother's wreck / And on the king my father's death before him." Because Eliot's notes name Weston's <i>From Ritual to Romance</i> as the poem's source, the man fishing is usually also taken as the Fisher King of the Grail legend, whose wound leaves his land barren; here he fishes a canal behind a gasworks in winter. The rat and the bones recall line 115, "rats' alley / Where the dead men lost their bones," and the "white bodies naked on the low damp ground" are generally read as the war dead.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** Ferdinand's father not drowned but believed so (reader); the Fisher King given as the usual reading (both); Eliot's own cross-reference from line 115 added (scholar); the war dead given as a reading, the "swimmers of last summer" dropped as unowned. The quote field's "wrack" becomes "wreck", to match the library record and the modern text (Folio "wracke").

**Scholar, round 2:** Object: one line number, my own slip in Round 1. In the site's count "I sat upon the shore" is 424 and "Fishing, with the arid plain behind me" is 425 (counted through the Gutenberg text: 434 lines, "Shantih" at 434); Eliot's Weston note is numbered 424 in his count, which runs one behind here. Replace "and who fishes again at 424" with "and who fishes again at 424–425". The rest: accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "He is usually read as also the Fisher King of Weston's <i>From Ritual to Romance</i>, named in Eliot's headnote, whose wound leaves his land barren and who fishes again at 424." Four hangers on one "He", and "named in Eliot's headnote" could be the book or the king. Replace with: "Because Eliot's headnote names Weston's <i>From Ritual to Romance</i> as the poem's source, the man fishing is usually taken also as the Fisher King of the Grail legend, whose wound leaves his land barren. He fishes again at 424." Also "The rat and the bones are Eliot's own cross-reference: his note to "rats' alley" at line 115 reads "Cf. Part III, l. 195"." The rat and bones are not a cross-reference; the note is. Replace with: "Eliot himself links the rat and the bones to line 115: his note to "rats' alley" there reads "Cf. Part III, l. 195"."

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the scholar's "424–425"; the reader's two rewordings, with the headnote's claim stated as the headnote states it (title and plan, not "source").

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** Accept. The headnote's claim is stated as Eliot states it, and "424–425" is the scholar's.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Ferdinand on the bank, and the Fisher King
quote: Sitting on a bank,<br>Weeping again the king my father’s wreck,<br>This music crept by me upon the waters,<br>Allaying both their fury and my passion<br>With its sweet air.
body: Ferdinand in <i>The Tempest</i>, Act I scene ii, sitting on the shore and weeping for the father he believes drowned, just before Ariel sings "Full fathom five". Eliot's note gives the source at line 192, and the same speech returns at 257. Eliot adds a brother to Shakespeare's father and puts the fisherman by a canal behind a gashouse in winter. Because Eliot's headnote says the poem's title and plan came from Weston's <i>From Ritual to Romance</i>, the man fishing is usually taken also as the Fisher King of the Grail legend, whose wound leaves his land barren. He fishes again at 424–425. Eliot himself links the rat and the bones to line 115: his note to "rats' alley" there reads "Cf. Part III, l. 195". The "White bodies naked on the low damp ground" have been read as the war dead.

### mrs-porter (lines 198–201)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: Actaeon, Diana, Sweeney, Mrs Porter
quote: When of the sudden, listening, you shall hear,<br>A noise of horns and hunting, which shall bring<br>Actaeon to Diana in the spring,<br>Where all shall see her naked skin . . .
body: John Day's <i>The Parliament of Bees</i> (printed 1641), quoted in Eliot's note. Actaeon, out hunting, sees the goddess Diana bathing and is turned into a stag and torn apart by his own hounds. In Eliot the horns are motor horns, the hunter is Sweeney, the ape-necked man of his 1920 poems, the goddess is Mrs Porter, and the bathing is a foot-wash in soda water. The three-line song is real: Eliot's note says it "was reported to me from Sydney, Australia," and it was a soldiers' song of the Gallipoli campaign, said to be about a Cairo brothel-keeper, in versions considerably less printable than this one. Which makes the next line, in French, the most violent cut in the poem.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs hedging.

Errors:
- "printed 1641": right. Day's lines as quoted match the 1922 note.
- "the ape-necked man of his 1920 poems": "Apeneck Sweeney" opens "Sweeney Among the Nightingales", first printed 1918, collected 1919 and 1920. Say "his poems of 1918–20".
- "a soldiers' song of the Gallipoli campaign, said to be about a Cairo brothel-keeper": the usual account (Southam, from memory) is a song of Australian troops in the First World War, Mrs Porter reputedly a Cairo brothel-keeper; Gallipoli is where it is usually placed; the sung versions vary. Keep "said to be" on all of it.
- "Which makes the next line, in French, the most violent cut in the poem." Uncheckable superlative.

Readings passed off as fact:
- "the goddess is Mrs Porter, and the bathing is a foot-wash in soda water": the plain parallel; fine as description.
- "the most violent cut in the poem": cut.

Missing: nothing.

Cut: the last sentence.

Proposed body:
John Day's <i>The Parliament of Bees</i> (printed 1641), quoted in Eliot's note: a spring hunt brings Actaeon to Diana bathing. In Ovid the hunter who sees the goddess naked is turned into a stag and torn apart by his own hounds. In Eliot the horns are motor horns, the hunter is Sweeney, the "Apeneck Sweeney" of his poems of 1918–20, and the bathing is done in soda water. The three lines of song are from a bawdy ballad of Australian troops in the First World War, in which Mrs Porter is said to have kept a brothel in Cairo; the soldiers' versions were less printable. Eliot's note says only that it "was reported to me from Sydney, Australia".

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Trim.

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- "Which makes the next line, in French, the most violent cut in the poem." Fragment beginning "Which", superlative nobody could check (one of the owner's examples), and "cut" is film talk.
- "The three-line song is real:" Colon fragment. Real as opposed to what? Say what it was.
- "in versions considerably less printable than this one." Arch.
- "the horns are motor horns, the hunter is Sweeney, ..., the goddess is Mrs Porter, and the bathing is a foot-wash" is a list of four, but it is the mapping I needed, so it stays if said flatly.

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- "the ape-necked man of his 1920 poems": "Apeneck Sweeney" is Eliot's own phrase and could be quoted as such; paraphrasing it loses the point that the wit is his.
- Nothing else; this note is mostly clear.

**Keep**
- Day, Parliament of Bees, 1641, quoted in Eliot's note.
- The Actaeon story in two sentences, since Day only alludes to it.
- The mapping of Day's horns, hunter, goddess and bathing onto Eliot's lines.
- The song's history: reported from Sydney (Eliot's note), a Gallipoli soldiers' song, Mrs Porter said to be a Cairo brothel-keeper, the usual versions obscene.

**Proposed body**
Plainer title: Actaeon and Mrs Porter

John Day's <i>The Parliament of Bees</i> (printed 1641), quoted in Eliot's note. Actaeon, out hunting, came upon the goddess Diana bathing; she turned him into a stag and his own hounds tore him apart. In Eliot the horns are motor horns, the hunter is Sweeney, "Apeneck Sweeney" of his 1920 poems, and Diana bathing is Mrs Porter and her daughter washing their feet in soda water. The song was a real one. Eliot's note says it "was reported to me from Sydney, Australia"; it was a soldiers' song of the Gallipoli campaign, Mrs Porter is said to have kept a brothel in Cairo, and the usual versions were cruder than this.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** "Apeneck Sweeney" quoted as Eliot's (reader); the song's history hedged as the scholar asked; "the most violent cut in the poem" cut.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "In Eliot the horns are motor horns, the hunter is Sweeney, the "Apeneck Sweeney" of his poems of 1918–20, and the bathing is done in soda water." The title promises Mrs Porter and the body never says she stands where Diana stood. Replace with: "In Eliot the horns are motor horns, the hunter is Sweeney, the "Apeneck Sweeney" of his poems of 1918–20, and in Diana's place Mrs Porter and her daughter wash their feet in soda water." Also "The three lines of song are from a bawdy ballad of Australian troops in the First World War, in which Mrs Porter is said to have kept a brothel in Cairo; the soldiers' versions were less printable." "In which ... is said to" reads as if the ballad says it. Replace with: "The three lines of song are from a bawdy ballad sung by Australian troops in the First World War. Its Mrs Porter is said to have kept a brothel in Cairo, and the soldiers' versions were cruder than this."

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's two rewordings taken.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Actaeon and Mrs Porter
quote: When of the sudden, listening, you shall hear,<br>A noise of horns and hunting, which shall bring<br>Actaeon to Diana in the spring,<br>Where all shall see her naked skin . . .
body: John Day's <i>The Parliament of Bees</i> (printed 1641), quoted in Eliot's note: a noise of horns and hunting brings Actaeon to Diana bathing. In Ovid the hunter who sees the goddess naked is turned into a stag and torn apart by his own hounds. In Eliot the horns are motor horns, the hunter is Sweeney, the "Apeneck Sweeney" of his poems of 1918–20, and in Diana's place Mrs Porter and her daughter wash their feet in soda water. The three lines of song are from a bawdy ballad sung by Australian troops in the First World War. Its Mrs Porter is said to have kept a brothel in Cairo, and the soldiers' versions were cruder than this. Eliot's note says only that it "was reported to me from Sydney, Australia".

### verlaine (line 202)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: The children's voices in the dome
quote: Parsifal a vaincu les Filles, leur gentil<br>Babil et la luxure amusante — et sa pente<br>Vers la Chair de garçon vierge que cela tente<br>D’aimer les seins légers et ce gentil babil; . . .<br>— Et, ô ces voix d’enfants chantant dans la coupole!
body: The last line of Verlaine's sonnet "Parsifal" (1886), written after Wagner's opera, in which the pure knight resists the Flower-maidens and Kundry, heals the wounded king Amfortas and adores the Grail while boys' voices sing from the dome of the temple. In Wagner, Parsifal's feet are washed before the ceremony. Eliot puts the choirboys directly after Mrs Porter and her daughter washing their feet in soda water. The Grail chapel and the brothel are one line apart, and the line between them is in another language, as if the poem could not say it in English.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs hedging.

Errors:
- None. The quote field matches the Vanier text (fr.wikisource, Oeuvres completes II, p. 52) line for line, including "— Et, ô ces voix d'enfants chantant dans la coupole !". For the library thread: the sources record's full passage has "rentre sous sa tente" where that text reads "sous la tente". "1886": the sonnet appeared in the Revue wagnérienne, January 1886 (from memory, unverified), then in Amour (1888). "In Wagner, Parsifal's feet are washed before the ceremony": Act III, by Kundry; right.

Readings passed off as fact:
- "The Grail chapel and the brothel are one line apart, and the line between them is in another language, as if the poem could not say it in English." Annotator's epigram; and "brothel" is inferred from the ballad, not in the poem.
- The feet-washing parallel is a critics' observation (Southam, Grover Smith, from memory); give it as one.

Missing: nothing.

Cut: the last sentence.

Proposed body:
The last line of Verlaine's sonnet "Parsifal" (1886), on Wagner's opera: the knight has resisted the Flower-maidens and Kundry, healed the wounded king and adores the Grail, and the sonnet ends on the sound of the boys' choir in the dome of the Grail temple. Eliot's note gives the source. The line follows Mrs Porter and her daughter washing their feet; in Wagner's third act Kundry washes Parsifal's feet before he goes in to the Grail, a parallel critics have often drawn.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Trim.

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- "The Grail chapel and the brothel are one line apart, and the line between them is in another language, as if the poem could not say it in English." Bow-tying last sentence; the poem personified as unable to speak. It also does not parse: if the two are one line apart there is no line between them, and the French line is the Grail chapel.

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- "written after Wagner's opera": after in time, or based on it? Both are true; "on Wagner's opera" says it.
- Nothing else; the summary of the sonnet is needed because the quote gives only the first quatrain and the last line.

**Keep**
- Last line of Verlaine's "Parsifal," 1886, on Wagner's opera; the summary of the sonnet (Flower-maidens, Kundry, Amfortas, the Grail, boys' voices in the dome).
- "In Wagner, Parsifal's feet are washed before the ceremony." This is the fact that connects the choirboys to the soda water, and I would not have known it.
- "Eliot puts the choirboys directly after Mrs Porter and her daughter washing their feet." That is the whole note, said plainly.

**Proposed body**
Title unchanged.

The last line of Verlaine's sonnet "Parsifal" (1886), on Wagner's opera: the pure knight resists the Flower-maidens and Kundry, heals the wounded king Amfortas, and adores the Grail while boys' voices sing from the dome of the temple. In Wagner, Parsifal's feet are washed before he goes in to the Grail. Eliot sets the choirboys directly after Mrs Porter and her daughter washing their feet in soda water.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** the scholar's draft, which the reader's matches in substance; the feet-washing parallel given as critics'.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Accept.

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** accepted by both; unchanged from round 2.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: The children's voices in the dome
quote: Parsifal a vaincu les Filles, leur gentil<br>Babil et la luxure amusante — et sa pente<br>Vers la Chair de garçon vierge que cela tente<br>D’aimer les seins légers et ce gentil babil; . . .<br>— Et, ô ces voix d’enfants chantant dans la coupole!
body: The last line of Verlaine's sonnet "Parsifal" (1886), on Wagner's opera: the knight has resisted the Flower-maidens and Kundry, healed the wounded king Amfortas and adores the Grail, and the sonnet ends on the sound of the boys' choir in the dome of the Grail temple. Eliot's note gives the source. The line follows Mrs Porter and her daughter washing their feet; in Wagner's third act Kundry washes Parsifal's feet before he goes in to the Grail, a parallel critics have often drawn.

### tereu (line 206)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: The bird names her rapist
quote: What bird so sings, yet so does wail?<br>O ’tis the ravish’d nightingale.<br>Jug, jug, jug, jug, tereu! she cries,<br>And still her woes at midnight rise.
body: John Lyly's song from <i>Campaspe</i> (1584), the best-known statement of the Elizabethan convention that the nightingale sings "jug jug" and "tereu," which is the vocative of Tereus: the bird calling the name of the man who cut out her tongue. Four lines, three birds' worth of noise, then the name. "So rudely forc'd" is line 100 with its spelling roughened. The nightingale's song in this poem is never lovely. It is evidence.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.

Errors:
- "John Lyly's song from Campaspe (1584)": the play was printed 1584, but its songs were first printed in Blount's Six Court Comedies of 1632 and their authorship has been questioned (Wikipedia, Campaspe (play), citing G. K. Hunter's edition; the site's own library record says the same). The song's text as quoted is right (Oxford Book of English Verse no. 86, bartleby.com).
- "the best-known statement of the Elizabethan convention": uncheckable; the convention is older than Lyly (Gascoigne's "Complaint of Philomene", 1576, has "Tereu"; from memory, unverified).
- "'So rudely forc'd' is line 100 with its spelling roughened": metaphor. Line 100 reads "So rudely forced" (Gutenberg; 1922 text); Eliot's own note to 100 reads "Cf. Part III, l. 204".

Readings passed off as fact:
- "Four lines, three birds' worth of noise, then the name." Counting for effect.
- "The nightingale's song in this poem is never lovely. It is evidence." The "X is not Y. It is Z." close named in the standard.

Missing: Eliot's cross-reference from 100; that the story is Ovid's (Met. VI) and is told at 99–103.

Cut: the two sentences above; "best-known".

Proposed body:
The song printed with Lyly's <i>Campaspe</i> (the play 1584; its songs first printed 1632, and whether Lyly wrote them is disputed) gives the Elizabethan convention in full: the nightingale sings "jug jug" and cries "tereu", the vocative of Tereus, the name of the man who raped her and cut out her tongue. The story is Ovid's (<i>Metamorphoses</i> VI) and is told at lines 99–103. Line 205 repeats line 100, "So rudely forced", in an older spelling; Eliot's note to line 100 reads "Cf. Part III, l. 204".

Title: "Tereu, the nightingale's cry" (the present one is a headline).

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Rewrite.

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- "Four lines, three birds' worth of noise, then the name." Counting flourish (one of the owner's examples). Which three birds?
- "The nightingale's song in this poem is never lovely. It is evidence." The "X is not Y. It is Z." bow (one of the owner's examples).
- "the best-known statement of the Elizabethan convention" Superlative nobody could check.
- "with its spelling roughened" Metaphor in place of the fact, which is that "forced" at line 100 is here "forc'd".

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- "the man who cut out her tongue" assumes I remember Philomela from Part II. A line reference to 99 would do it.
- Which bird is "Twit twit twit" is never said; if the note counts birds it should name them, and if it does not need to, it should not count.

**Keep**
- Lyly's song from Campaspe, 1584, as the source of "jug jug" and "tereu."
- Tereu is the vocative of Tereus: the nightingale is calling her rapist's name. This is the fact the line turns on.
- "So rudely forc'd" repeats line 100.

**Proposed body**
Title unchanged.

John Lyly's song from <i>Campaspe</i> (1584), which gives the Elizabethan convention that the nightingale sings "jug jug" and "tereu." Tereu is the vocative of Tereus, so the bird is calling the name of the king who raped her and cut out her tongue (lines 99 to 103). "So rudely forc'd" repeats line 100, "So rudely forced."

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** the scholar's correction on the songs' printing and authorship; the reader's cross-reference to 99–103; Eliot's own note from 100 added; the counting and the "It is evidence" close cut; title plainer.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "(the play 1584; its songs first printed 1632, and whether Lyly wrote them is disputed)". A reader at "Tereu" does not need the authorship question, and "The song printed with Lyly's <i>Campaspe</i>" already leaves it open; ask that the dispute go. Replace with: "The song printed with Lyly's <i>Campaspe</i> (the play 1584, its songs 1632) gives ...". Also "Line 205 repeats line 100, "So rudely forced", in an older spelling; Eliot's note to line 100 reads "Cf. Part III, l. 204"." The 205 against the 204 leaves the reader checking which is right. Replace with: "Line 205 repeats line 100, "So rudely forced", in an older spelling, and Eliot's own note at line 100 points to this passage ("Cf. Part III, l. 204")."

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the authorship dispute cut here at the reader's asking (it stays in the library record for Lyly, proposed to the site thread); the reader's last sentence taken.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Tereu, the nightingale's cry
quote: What bird so sings, yet so does wail?<br>O ’tis the ravish’d nightingale.<br>Jug, jug, jug, jug, tereu! she cries,<br>And still her woes at midnight rise.
body: The song printed with Lyly's <i>Campaspe</i> (the play 1584, its songs 1632) gives the Elizabethan convention that the nightingale sings "jug jug" and cries "tereu", the vocative of Tereus, the name of the king who raped her and cut out her tongue. The story is Ovid's (<i>Metamorphoses</i> VI) and is told at lines 99–103. Line 205 repeats line 100, "So rudely forced", in an older spelling, and Eliot's own note at line 100 points to this passage ("Cf. Part III, l. 204").

### eugenides (lines 209–214)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: The one-eyed merchant, in person
body: Eugenides means "well-born." Smyrna, now İzmir, was the great port of Asia Minor and shipped the currants that London's bakers used; a pocketful is a sample. "C.i.f. London: documents at sight" is the trade shorthand for cost, insurance and freight paid to London, with the shipping documents handed over when the sight draft is paid, language Eliot handled daily at Lloyds. (His own note expands the abbreviation slightly wrongly, as "carriage and insurance free," a banker's slip that the editors of the poem have enjoyed ever since.) "Demotic French" is the Levant's trading language. The Cannon Street Hotel stood in front of Cannon Street station, the terminus for the boat trains to the Continent, and was the usual place for City men to meet foreign clients; a weekend at the Metropole, the big hotel on the Brighton seafront, is a proposition, and has generally been read as a homosexual one.

He is the one-eyed merchant of Madame Sosostris's pack (one-eyed, presumably, because a card shows a face in profile), and, Eliot's note says, he "melts into the Phoenician Sailor." The Phoenicians were the traders of the ancient Mediterranean; Weston argued that the Grail mysteries travelled Europe's trade routes with Syrian merchants. In September 1922, between the poem's completion and its publication, Smyrna burned.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.

Errors:
- "Cannon Street station, the terminus for the boat trains to the Continent": not in 1921–22. The Continental boat trains were withdrawn from Cannon Street on 15 November 1914 and rerouted to Victoria, and the South Eastern and Chatham's Continental services were all at Victoria from 8 January 1920 (en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannon_Street_station; kentrail.uk/london_victoria_7.htm). Southam's "terminus for the Continental boat-trains" (from memory) is the source of the slip. Write "had been ... until 1914".
- "the usual place for City men to meet foreign clients": unsupported. Known: the City Terminus Hotel, opened 1867 as the station's frontage, renamed Cannon Street Hotel 1879, mostly closed 1931 with its public rooms kept for meetings (Wikipedia, as above). "Much used for business meetings" is what can be said.
- "Smyrna ... shipped the currants that London's bakers used": overstated. The dried-currant trade was Greek (Zante, Patras; Britain the chief importer); Smyrna's staples were figs and sultanas (en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zante_currant; europenowjournal.org on the Greek currant trade). Say "a centre of the dried-fruit trade".
- "His own note expands the abbreviation slightly wrongly, as 'carriage and insurance free,' a banker's slip that the editors of the poem have enjoyed ever since": the note's wording is confirmed (1922 text); "cost, insurance and freight" is the trade term. "Enjoyed ever since" is decoration; "banker's slip" is a guess at its cause.
- "language Eliot handled daily at Lloyds": "daily" unsupported; he was in the Colonial and Foreign Department from 1917 (from memory of Gordon and Crawford, unverified).
- "the big hotel on the Brighton seafront": usually so taken (Hotel Metropole, Brighton, 1890), but there was a Hotel Métropole in London and one at Folkestone; hedge.
- "Weston argued that the Grail mysteries travelled Europe's trade routes with Syrian merchants": Weston, ch. XII, following Cumont, says the Attis and Mithra cults reached the farthest borders of the Roman Empire by three channels, "First, commercial, through the medium of Syrian merchants", then slaves and officials, then soldiers (Gutenberg #4090). Right in substance; cite the chapter.
- "In September 1922 ... Smyrna burned": the fire of 13–22 September 1922 (from memory); the Criterion printing was 16 October 1922. Right.
- "Eugenides means 'well-born'": Greek eugenēs, well-born, with a patronymic ending; fine.

Readings passed off as fact:
- "a pocketful is a sample": reasonable; say "presumably".
- "one-eyed, presumably, because a card shows a face in profile": a suggestion (Southam's, from memory); give it as one.
- "has generally been read as a homosexual one": "often" is the defensible word.

Missing: Eliot's note is at 218; that the identification with the one-eyed merchant is Eliot's own (the body has this).

Cut: "a banker's slip that the editors of the poem have enjoyed ever since"; "that London's bakers used"; "the usual place for City men to meet foreign clients".

Proposed body:
Eugenides is a Greek surname, from <i>eugenēs</i>, well-born. Smyrna, now İzmir, was the chief port of Asia Minor and a centre of the dried-fruit trade; the pocketful of currants is presumably his samples. "C.i.f. London: documents at sight" is trade shorthand: the price covers cost, insurance and freight to London, and the shipping documents are handed over against payment of the sight draft. Eliot's note expands it as "carriage and insurance free", which is not the trade usage; the terms were those of his work at Lloyds Bank. French was the trading language of the Levant, and "demotic" marks his as the common kind. The Cannon Street Hotel formed the front of Cannon Street station, the City terminus that had been the London end of the Continental boat trains until 1914, and was much used for business meetings. The Metropole is usually taken as the Hotel Metropole on the Brighton seafront, and the weekend as a proposition, often read as a homosexual one.

Eliot's note to line 218 makes him the "one-eyed merchant, seller of currants" of Madame Sosostris's pack, who "melts into the Phoenician Sailor"; the one eye has been explained by the profile faces of playing cards. Weston (<i>From Ritual to Romance</i>, ch. XII) says the mystery cults behind the Grail reached the west of the Roman Empire first through Syrian merchants. Smyrna burned in September 1922, between the poem's completion and its publication.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Trim.

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- "a banker's slip that the editors of the poem have enjoyed ever since." Arch, and nobody could check it.
- "language Eliot handled daily at Lloyds" "Daily" is colour I cannot check; that he worked with such documents at Lloyds is the fact.
- "In September 1922, between the poem's completion and its publication, Smyrna burned." Dramatic last sentence built for effect; a bow.
- "The one-eyed merchant, in person" (title) "In person" is a wink.

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- ""Demotic French" is the Levant's trading language." Demotic means colloquial, of the common people; the gloss slides from the word to a claim about the Levant that I cannot check. Say both plainly if both are wanted.
- "the usual place for City men to meet foreign clients": cannot check "the usual."
- "the terminus for the boat trains to the Continent": cannot check; keep only if the scholar can.
- Smyrna burning does not help me with the line; Eliot wrote it before. If it belongs anywhere it is with the 1920 photograph.
- Two paragraphs is long, but the six lines are full of trade jargon and the length is mostly earned.

**Keep**
- Eugenides means "well-born."
- Smyrna (İzmir), the currant port; the pocketful is a sample.
- The expansion of "C.i.f. London: documents at sight," and that Eliot's own note expands it wrongly, because I will read his note and be puzzled. The bare fact is enough.
- The Cannon Street Hotel by the station, a meeting place for City men and foreign clients; the Metropole at Brighton; the weekend as a proposition "generally read as a homosexual one" (this is the right way to put a reading).
- Eliot's note linking him to the one-eyed merchant of the cards and the Phoenician Sailor; the profile guess, hedged as it is; Weston on Syrian merchants and the trade routes, in one clause.

**Proposed body**
Plainer title: The one-eyed merchant

Eugenides means "well-born." Smyrna, now İzmir, was the port of Asia Minor that shipped currants to London; the pocketful is a sample. "C.i.f. London: documents at sight" is trade shorthand: cost, insurance and freight paid to London, and the shipping documents handed over when the sight draft is paid. Eliot, who handled such documents at Lloyds Bank, nevertheless expands the abbreviation in his note as "carriage and insurance free," which is not what c.i.f. stands for. "Demotic French" is the colloquial French of Levantine trade. The Cannon Street Hotel stood in front of Cannon Street station, where the Continental boat trains came in, and City men met foreign clients there. A weekend at the Metropole, the large hotel on the Brighton seafront, is a proposition, and it has generally been read as a homosexual one.

Eliot's note identifies him with the one-eyed merchant of Madame Sosostris's cards (one-eyed, presumably, because the card shows a face in profile), who "melts into the Phoenician Sailor." The Phoenicians were the traders of the ancient Mediterranean, and Weston argued that the Grail mysteries travelled Europe's trade routes with Syrian merchants.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** the scholar's corrections taken (no boat trains at Cannon Street after 1914; Smyrna a dried-fruit port rather than London's currant supplier; "usual place" and "daily" unsupported; Weston cited by chapter; Metropole hedged). The burning of Smyrna is cut, on the reader's point that it postdates the poem and does not bear on the line; the photograph's caption keeps its date. The profile explanation of the one eye kept, hedged.

**Scholar, round 2:** Object: the Weston sentence, again my own Round 1 wording, misleads. Weston (ch. XII, following Cumont) says the cults travelled by three channels, "First, commercial, through the medium of Syrian merchants", then slaves and officials, and "Thirdly, and most important", the soldiers; "first through Syrian merchants" reads as if merchants were the main carriers. Replace the last sentence with: "Weston (<i>From Ritual to Romance</i>, chapter XII) counts Syrian merchants, with slaves, officials and soldiers, among the carriers of the mystery cults behind the Grail to the western Roman Empire." The rest: accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object, four sentences.
1. "Smyrna, now İzmir, was the chief port of Asia Minor and a centre of the dried-fruit trade; the pocketful of currants is presumably his samples." Semicolon, and "pocketful ... is ... samples" does not agree. Replace with: "Smyrna, now İzmir, was the chief port of Asia Minor and a centre of the dried-fruit trade. The currants in his pocket are presumably samples."
2. ""C.i.f. London: documents at sight" is trade shorthand: the price covers cost, insurance and freight to London, and the shipping documents are handed over when the sight draft is paid. Eliot's note expands it as "carriage and insurance free", which is not the trade usage; the terms were those of his work at Lloyds Bank." Two colons in one sentence, and the Lloyds fact is tacked on after the slip, where a reader wonders what it is doing; it belongs with the jargon it explains. Replace with: ""C.i.f. London: documents at sight" is trade shorthand of the kind Eliot handled at Lloyds Bank. The price covers cost, insurance and freight to London, and the shipping documents are handed over when the sight draft is paid. Eliot's note expands it as "carriage and insurance free", which is not the trade usage."
3. "French was the trading language of the Levant, and "demotic" marks his as the common, colloquial kind." "Marks his as" took me three readings. Replace with: "French was the trading language of the Levant, and Mr. Eugenides speaks the "demotic", or colloquial, kind."
4. "who "melts into the Phoenician Sailor"; the one eye has been explained by the profile faces on playing cards." Semicolon, and "explained by the faces" reads as if the faces do the explaining. Replace with: "who "melts into the Phoenician Sailor". His one eye is sometimes explained by the playing cards that show a face in profile."
The rest, including the Weston sentence, reads plainly.

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's four rewordings and the scholar's Weston sentence, all taken.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: The one-eyed merchant
body: Eugenides is a Greek surname, from <i>eugenēs</i>, well-born. Smyrna, now İzmir, was the chief port of Asia Minor and a centre of the dried-fruit trade. The currants in his pocket are presumably samples. "C.i.f. London: documents at sight" is trade shorthand of the kind Eliot handled at Lloyds Bank. The price covers cost, insurance and freight to London, and the shipping documents are handed over when the sight draft is paid. Eliot's note expands it as "carriage and insurance free", which is not the trade usage. French was the trading language of the Levant, and Mr. Eugenides speaks the "demotic", or colloquial, kind. The Cannon Street Hotel formed the front of Cannon Street station in the City and was much used for business meetings. The Metropole is usually taken as the Hotel Metropole on the Brighton seafront, and the weekend as a proposition, often read as a homosexual one.

Eliot's note to line 218 makes him the "one-eyed merchant, seller of currants" of Madame Sosostris's pack, who "melts into the Phoenician Sailor". His one eye is sometimes explained by the playing cards that show a face in profile. Weston (<i>From Ritual to Romance</i>, chapter XII) counts Syrian merchants, with slaves, officials and soldiers, among the carriers of the mystery cults behind the Grail to the western Roman Empire.

### violet-hour (lines 215–223)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: Evening brings everything home
quote: Ἔσπερε πάντα φέρων ὄσα φαίνολις ἐσκέδασ’ Αὔως,<br>φέρεις ὄιν, φέρεις αἶγα, φέρεις ἄπυ μάτερι παῖδα.
body: Sappho's fragment on Hesperus, behind "the evening hour that strives / Homeward, and brings the sailor home from sea." Eliot's note apologises that his lines "may not appear as exact as Sappho's" and explains that he had in mind the longshore fisherman coming in at nightfall, which is to say his own boyhood summers at Gloucester, Massachusetts. Stevenson's "Requiem" ("Home is the sailor, home from sea") is in there too. The violet hour is the poem's hour: twilight, the office emptying, "the human engine" idling "like a taxi throbbing waiting." It comes back as "violet air" and "violet light" in the collapsing cities of Part V.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs hedging.

Errors:
- None of fact. The Greek is the Lobel–Page text of fr. 104a (Loeb; dcc.dickinson.edu/sappho/frag-104-117); Demetrius, On Style, preserves it. Eliot's note is at 221 and the quoted phrase is accurate. Stevenson's line is "Home is the sailor, home from sea" (Underwoods, 1887, no. XXI). "Violet air" is 372 and "violet light" 379.

Readings passed off as fact:
- "which is to say his own boyhood summers at Gloucester, Massachusetts": inference. The dory is the Gloucester fishing boat and the Eliots summered at Gloucester (from memory of Crawford and Gordon), but the note says nothing of Gloucester.
- "Stevenson's 'Requiem' ... is in there too": a parallel critics cite (Southam, from memory); give it as one.
- "The violet hour is the poem's hour": uncheckable.
- "Eliot's note apologises": "concedes" is what the note does.

Missing: nothing.

Cut: "The violet hour is the poem's hour"; "the collapsing cities of Part V" (give the line numbers instead).

Proposed body:
Sappho's two lines on the evening star, which brings back all that dawn scattered, are behind "the evening hour that strives / Homeward, and brings the sailor home from sea". Eliot's note concedes that his lines "may not appear as exact as Sappho's" and says he had in mind the "longshore" or "dory" fisherman coming in at nightfall; the dory is the Gloucester fishing boat, and Eliot's family summered at Gloucester, Massachusetts. Stevenson's "Requiem" ("Home is the sailor, home from sea") is also usually cited. "Violet" returns in Part V, "the violet air" (372) and "the violet light" (379).

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Rewrite.

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- "The violet hour is the poem's hour:" Superlative nobody could check (one of the owner's examples), followed by a colon and a list.
- "which is to say his own boyhood summers at Gloucester, Massachusetts." A biographical leap put as identity.
- "is in there too." Breezy.
- "the collapsing cities of Part V" Fine as a pointer, but the phrase is doing atmosphere.

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- "Eliot's note apologises": it does not apologise, it grants a point and explains.
- Whether the Gloucester connection is a fact or the annotator's inference I cannot tell. Put it as a fact about Eliot (he spent boyhood summers there, where the dory fishermen were) and let me make the link.
- Whose is the Stevenson echo? "Critics also hear" would settle it.

**Keep**
- Sappho's fragment to Hesperus, the evening star, behind "brings the sailor home from sea"; the translation beside it does the work.
- Eliot's note on the longshore or dory fisherman, quoted.
- Stevenson's "Requiem," as an echo critics hear.
- That the violet hour is dusk and the end of the office day, since "the eyes and back / Turn upward from the desk" is what the note is explaining.
- That violet returns in Part V, in half a sentence.

**Proposed body**
Plainer title: Sappho's evening star

Sappho's fragment to Hesperus, the evening star, behind "the evening hour that strives / Homeward, and brings the sailor home from sea." Eliot's note grants that his lines "may not appear as exact as Sappho's" and says he had in mind the "longshore" or "dory" fisherman who comes in at nightfall, as the fishermen did at Gloucester, Massachusetts, where he spent his boyhood summers. Critics also hear Stevenson's "Requiem" ("Home is the sailor, home from sea"). The violet hour is dusk, the end of the working day, when "the eyes and back / Turn upward from the desk"; the colour returns in Part V as "violet air" and "violet light."

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** Gloucester given as fact about the Eliots, the link left to the reader (both sides); Stevenson as a parallel usually cited; "the poem's hour" cut; the Part V lines given by number in the site's count (373 and 380, not Eliot's 372 and 379).

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept (373 and 380 confirmed by count).

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "coming in at nightfall; the dory is the small boat of the New England fishermen, and the Eliots summered at Gloucester, Massachusetts." Gloucester arrives with nothing tying it to the dory. Replace with the scholar's own link: "coming in at nightfall. The dory is the fishing boat of Gloucester, Massachusetts, where the Eliots spent their summers." Also the last sentence joins two unrelated points with a semicolon; end it at "from the desk" and add: "The word returns in Part V, in "the violet air" (373) and "the violet light" (380)."

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader wanted the dory tied to Gloucester; the tie is made without calling the dory Gloucester's own boat, which it was not only. The reader's split of the last sentence taken.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** Accept. The dory is now tied to Gloucester in one plain sentence, and "one of their ports" claims no more than it should.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Sappho's evening star
quote: Ἔσπερε πάντα φέρων ὄσα φαίνολις ἐσκέδασ’ Αὔως,<br>φέρεις ὄιν, φέρεις αἶγα, φέρεις ἄπυ μάτερι παῖδα.
body: Sappho's two lines on the evening star, which brings back all that dawn scattered, are behind "the evening hour that strives / Homeward, and brings the sailor home from sea". Eliot's note concedes that his lines "may not appear as exact as Sappho's" and says he had in mind the "longshore" or "dory" fisherman coming in at nightfall. The dory is the small boat of the New England fishing fleets, and Gloucester, Massachusetts, where the Eliots spent their summers, was one of their ports. Stevenson's "Requiem" ("Home is the sailor, home from sea") is also usually cited. The violet hour is dusk, the end of the working day, when "the eyes and back / Turn upward from the desk". The word returns in Part V, in "the violet air" (373) and "the violet light" (380).

### tiresias (lines 218–219)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: The man who had been a woman
body: Ovid, <i>Metamorphoses</i> III, which Eliot's long note quotes in Latin: Tiresias struck two coupling snakes with his stick and was turned into a woman for seven years; striking them again, he became a man. Jupiter and Juno, arguing over which sex has more pleasure in love, called him in as the one witness who knew. He sided with Jupiter (women do). Juno blinded him; Jupiter, unable to undo another god's act, gave him foresight instead. He is the blind prophet of Thebes in <i>Oedipus</i>, and the dead seer Odysseus consults in the underworld: "I who have sat by Thebes below the wall / And walked among the lowest of the dead."
