Eliot's note makes him "the most important personage in the poem, uniting all the rest . . . the two sexes meet in Tiresias. What Tiresias <i>sees</i>, in fact, is the substance of the poem." The note is the closest thing the poem has to a stated design, and the drafts show that the design came late: Tiresias was there in the typescript, but the claim for him was written for the notes. Take it seriously and the poem has a single consciousness; take it as one more voice and it doesn't. Pound, in the margin of the typescript, was impatient with the seer's hedging: "make up yr. mind you Tiresias if you know know damn well or else you dont."

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.

Errors:
- "the drafts show that the design came late": the drafts contain no notes and cannot show when a design came. What is known: Tiresias is in the typescript of the typist scene, typed on Rainey's dating in November 1921 (p. 29); the notes were written in 1922 for the Boni & Liveright book (December 1922), the first printing to carry them; in "The Frontiers of Criticism" (1956) Eliot said the notes were expanded to provide "a few more pages of printed matter" and called the result "bogus scholarship" (wording confirmed via en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Frontiers_of_Criticism and kcctreasures.com). Whether the note states a design or supplies one afterwards is a critical dispute (Kenner sceptical, from memory of The Invisible Poet; unverified).
- Pound's marginalium is quoted entire; the rights rule allows a phrase or two. It stands beside the draft's "Across her brain one half-formed thought may pass" (1922: "allows ... to pass"), objecting to the "may" (facsimile, typist scene, from memory; Vendler, Yale Review, transcribes "Make up yr. mind / you Tiresias / if you know / know damn well / or else / you / don't"). Quote "make up yr. mind you Tiresias" and say what it was beside.
- Ovid is summarised correctly (Met. III.316–338): seven autumns as a woman, the change back in the eighth; Jupiter's claim that the woman's pleasure is greater; Juno's blinding; Jupiter's foresight since no god may undo another's act. "(women do)" makes Jupiter's claim the annotator's.

Readings passed off as fact:
- "The note is the closest thing the poem has to a stated design": an evaluation; acceptable if owned or softened.
- "Take it seriously and the poem has a single consciousness; take it as one more voice and it doesn't.": second person, epigram; replace with the two positions, named as positions.

Missing: that the note's claim is Eliot's and what he later said of the notes; that Tiresias was in the typescript.

Cut: "the drafts show that the design came late"; the "Take it seriously" sentence; the full marginalium.

Proposed body:
Ovid, <i>Metamorphoses</i> III, which Eliot's note quotes in Latin. Tiresias struck two coupling snakes with his staff and became a woman for seven years; in the eighth year he struck them again and was a man. Jupiter and Juno, disputing whether men or women have the greater pleasure in love, asked him as the one who had been both; he said women, as Jupiter had claimed. Juno blinded him; Jupiter, who could not undo another god's act, gave him foresight. He is the blind prophet of Thebes in Sophocles, and the dead seer Odysseus consults in <i>Odyssey</i> XI: "I who have sat by Thebes below the wall / And walked among the lowest of the dead."

Eliot's note calls him "the most important personage in the poem, uniting all the rest", and says "What Tiresias <i>sees</i>, in fact, is the substance of the poem." That is Eliot's claim, made in notes written in 1922 for the book edition; the typist passage, with Tiresias in it, had been typed in November 1921. In 1956 he said the notes had been expanded to fill out the volume and called them "bogus scholarship". Critics divide: some read the poem as what Tiresias sees, others take him as one voice among many and the note as an afterthought. Pound, in the margin of the typescript beside a hedging "may" in the typist scene, wrote "make up yr. mind you Tiresias".

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Trim (first paragraph stands with small changes; second paragraph recast).

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- "Take it seriously and the poem has a single consciousness; take it as one more voice and it doesn't." The "decide whether" instruction dressed as an antithesis.
- "The note is the closest thing the poem has to a stated design" Annotator's verdict as fact.
- "the drafts show that the design came late: Tiresias was there in the typescript, but the claim for him was written for the notes." Sounds like a discovery; what it says is that the note was written after the poem, which is true of every note.
- "He sided with Jupiter (women do)." Brisk parenthesis; a small cuteness.
- "was impatient with the seer's hedging" "The seer's hedging" refers to draft wording I have not seen; unexplained.

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- Pound's marginal comment is quoted as a full sentence; the site's rule says a phrase or two of the marginalia, so describe it and quote the phrase.
- I wanted the three things the standard asks to be kept apart, and here they are mixed: what Eliot's note says, what critics made of it, and what the lines do (Tiresias speaks only here, at 228 to 230, and at 243 to 246).

**Keep**
- The Ovid story in full, since Eliot's note gives it in untranslated Latin: the snakes, seven years as a woman, the judgement between Jupiter and Juno, blindness from Juno, foresight from Jupiter who could not undo another god's act.
- Sophocles' Tiresias at Thebes and Homer's in the underworld, tied to lines 245 to 246.
- Eliot's note quoted: "the most important personage in the poem" and "What Tiresias sees, in fact, is the substance of the poem."
- That the note came after the poem and that critics divide on how much weight it bears.
- Pound's "make up yr. mind," as a phrase.

**Proposed body**
Title unchanged.

Ovid, <i>Metamorphoses</i> III, which Eliot's note quotes in Latin. Tiresias struck two coupling snakes with his stick and was turned into a woman; seven years later he struck them again and became a man. When Jupiter and Juno argued over which sex has the greater pleasure in love, they called him to judge, as the one who had been both, and he agreed with Jupiter that women do. Juno blinded him; Jupiter, who could not undo what another god had done, gave him foresight instead. He is also the blind prophet of Thebes in Sophocles' <i>Oedipus</i> and the dead seer Odysseus consults in the underworld in the <i>Odyssey</i>: "I who have sat by Thebes below the wall / And walked among the lowest of the dead."

Eliot's note calls him "the most important personage in the poem, uniting all the rest," and says "What Tiresias <i>sees</i>, in fact, is the substance of the poem." The notes were written after the poem was finished. Critics have divided over whether to read the poem as Tiresias's single vision, as the note proposes, or to treat him as one voice among many; in the poem itself he speaks only here, at lines 228 to 230, and at 243 to 246. In the margin of the typescript Pound objected to hedging in the draft's wording: Tiresias should "make up yr. mind" whether he knew or not.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** Ovid as both sides had it, without "(women do)"; the note's claim kept as Eliot's, with the date of the notes and his 1956 remark (scholar); "the drafts show" and the "Take it seriously" antithesis cut; the two critical positions given as positions; where Tiresias names himself given plainly; Pound's marginalium reduced to a phrase and not placed against a particular line, since the two scholars' memories of where it stands differ.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** First paragraph accepted. Object to two sentences in the second.
1. "That is Eliot's claim, made in the notes he wrote for the book edition of December 1922; the typescript with Tiresias in it had been made a year earlier." "Made" for a typescript, "with Tiresias in it", and the point of the two dates left for the reader to infer. Replace with: "The claim is Eliot's, made in the notes he wrote for the book edition of December 1922, a year after the scene was typed."
2. "Pound, in the margin of the typescript of the scene, told Eliot that Tiresias should "make up yr. mind" whether he knew or not." Knew what? With the placement gone, "whether he knew or not" hangs. Replace with: "Pound, in the margin of the typescript of the scene, objected to a hedging word in what Tiresias says and told Eliot that Tiresias should "make up yr. mind" whether he knew or not."

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's first rewording taken. For the second, the reader asked what Tiresias was to make up his mind about. Round 3: the scholar placed Pound's two comments, "Perhaps be damned" beside "Perhaps a cheap house agent's clerk" and "make up yr. mind" beside the typist's "one half-formed thought may pass" (facsimile p. 47, as cited by Zeligs and Vendler), which the printed line 251, "allows one half-formed thought to pass", bears out; the scholar's sentence taken. The batch C scholar's memory put the second comment beside the loiterers' lines; the drafts record is made to agree with this placing, and the difference is recorded.

**Scholar, round 3:** Correct the last sentence. Pound's "make up yr. mind you Tiresias" stands against the typist's line, "Across her brain one half-formed thought may pass", with "may" struck through (facsimile p. 47); the clerk's "Perhaps a cheap house agent's clerk" drew a different comment, "Perhaps be damned" (Zeligs, Vassar thesis 2013, citing the facsimile and Lyndall Gordon; Vendler, Yale Review, ties the comment to Eliot's "perhaps" and "may"; this matches my memory of the facsimile). As phrased, the sentence attaches the comment to the wrong line and makes "whether he knew him or not" about the clerk. Replace with: "Pound, in the margin of the typescript, objected to the scene's hedging: beside "Perhaps a cheap house agent's clerk" he wrote "Perhaps be damned", and beside the typist's "one half-formed thought may pass" he told Eliot that Tiresias should "make up yr. mind" whether he knew or not."

**Reader, round 3:** Accept. "Whether he knew him or not" now has its referent, and the quoted hedge is a phrase, within the rights rule.

Nothing else open. The ten final texts are clear, plain and, where they offer a reading, say so.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: The man who had been a woman
body: Ovid, <i>Metamorphoses</i> III, which Eliot's note quotes in Latin. Tiresias struck two coupling snakes with his staff and was turned into a woman; in the eighth year he struck them again and became a man. When Jupiter and Juno argued over which sex has the greater pleasure in love they asked him, as the one who had been both, and he sided with Jupiter, who said women do. Juno blinded him; Jupiter, who could not undo another god's act, gave him foresight instead. He is also the blind prophet of Thebes in Sophocles' <i>Oedipus</i>, and the dead seer Odysseus consults in <i>Odyssey</i> XI: "I who have sat by Thebes below the wall / And walked among the lowest of the dead."

Eliot's note calls him "the most important personage in the poem, uniting all the rest", and says "What Tiresias <i>sees</i>, in fact, is the substance of the poem." The claim is Eliot's, made in the notes he wrote for the book edition of December 1922, a year after the scene was typed. In 1956 he said the notes had been expanded to fill out the volume and called them "bogus scholarship". Critics divide: some read the whole poem as what Tiresias sees, others take him as one voice among many and the note as an afterthought. In the poem he names himself three times, at 218, 228 and 243, all in this scene. Pound, in the margin of the typescript, objected to the scene's hedging: beside "Perhaps a cheap house agent's clerk" he wrote "Perhaps be damned", and beside the typist's "one half-formed thought may pass" he told Eliot that Tiresias should "make up yr. mind" whether he knew or not.

## The glosses, 11 to 19 (batch B)

### typist (lines 222–248)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: The typist and the clerk
body: The most argued-over scene in the poem. A typist in a bedsit, her underwear ("combinations," one-piece, and "stays") drying out of the window, her dinner from tins, her divan a bed by night; a "small house agent's clerk," pimpled ("carbuncular"), with the confidence of new money, "a silk hat on a Bradford millionaire" (the Yorkshire wool towns made fortunes in the war); a seduction that is really an assault met with indifference. Tiresias watches, "foresuffered all," and blesses the clerk's exit with a rhyme.

It is written in rhymed quatrains, abab, and the rhymes are doing the judging: "guesses / caresses," "tired / undesired," "at once / no response." The typescript had more of them, and Pound cut hard. His marginal verdict on the passage: "verse not interesting enough as verse to warrant so much of it." Against Eliot's tentative "perhaps" in one line he wrote "Perhaps be damned." What survived is the poem's cruellest writing, and the cruelty is aimed as much at the eighteenth-century manner it imitates as at the two people on the divan. Whether Tiresias is compassionate or a voyeur is a question the poem asks about you.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite

**Errors**
- "her underwear ("combinations," one-piece, and "stays") drying out of the window". Wrong: only the combinations are out of the window (224–225); the stays are on the divan with the stockings, slippers and camisoles (226–227). Source: the 1922 text, lines 224–227 (poem-part3.txt; https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/1321/pg1321.txt).
- "It is written in rhymed quatrains, abab". Overstated. Lines 218–221, 224–227, 231–246 and 253–256 rhyme abab; 222–223 and 228 are unrhymed, 229–230 is a couplet, and 247–252 run kiss / unlit / glass / lover / pass / over, which is no quatrain scheme. The regular quatrains belong to the typescript, and Pound's cuts broke them. Source: the poem text; the 1971 facsimile for the typescript (from memory, unverified; Wikipedia's Waste Land article says the same).
- "verse not interesting enough as verse to warrant so much of it" quotes a whole Pound comment. The site's rule for Pound's marginalia is a phrase or two: "not interesting enough as verse" is enough. The wording is right (facsimile, from memory; confirmed by the Wikipedia article on the poem). "Perhaps be damned" is right and is a phrase (the annotated page is reproduced at https://ecehm.tumblr.com/post/165927846321/).
- "pimpled ("carbuncular")": a carbuncle is a boil; "pimply" is the usual gloss (Southam). Not wrong.
- "the Yorkshire wool towns made fortunes in the war": Bradford's wool trade specifically; Southam's gloss (from memory). Not wrong, but say Bradford.

**Readings passed off as fact**
- "The most argued-over scene in the poem." Uncheckable.
- "the rhymes are doing the judging". Annotator's metaphor.
- "What survived is the poem's cruellest writing, and the cruelty is aimed as much at the eighteenth-century manner it imitates as at the two people on the divan." Uncheckable superlative, and the reading (the quatrains as pastiche of the eighteenth-century heroic quatrain) is one critics have offered; it is not a fact, and the manner is as much Eliot's own quatrain poems of 1917–19.
- "blesses the clerk's exit with a rhyme". Decoration.
- "Whether Tiresias is compassionate or a voyeur is a question the poem asks about you." Second person; the two readings can be offered as readings.

**Missing**
- Eliot's note at 218, the test case: what Tiresias sees "is the substance of the poem". A reader of this scene needs to be pointed at it, kept distinct from what the lines do.
- What "combinations", "stays" and "house agent" mean.
- Pound's other intervention here, which explains the poem's certainty at 251: the typescript let a thought "may pass" across her brain; Pound objected that Tiresias must make up his mind whether he knows; the poem now says her brain "allows" it. Facsimile, from memory, unverified (the wording of Pound's comment is confirmed by the Wikipedia article on the poem).

**Cut**
"The most argued-over scene in the poem." / "and the rhymes are doing the judging" / "and blesses the clerk's exit with a rhyme" / "What survived is the poem's cruellest writing, and the cruelty is aimed ... on the divan." / "Whether Tiresias is compassionate or a voyeur is a question the poem asks about you."

**Proposed body**
Title — The typist and the clerk

The typist's room is a bedsit: food from tins, the divan her bed at night, her "combinations" (a one-piece undergarment) drying out of the window, her "stays" (corset) piled on the divan with the stockings and camisoles. Her visitor is a "small house agent's clerk," an estate agent's clerk, pimply ("carbuncular"), with the assurance of new money; Bradford, the Yorkshire wool town, stood for fortunes made in the war. Eliot's note at line 218 says that what Tiresias sees "is the substance of the poem"; here he says he has "foresuffered all" and foretells the rest.

In the typescript the scene ran in regular quatrains rhyming abab. Pound cut several stanzas, writing beside the passage that the verse was "not interesting enough as verse" for so much of it, and "Perhaps be damned" against a line that began with "perhaps." What survives keeps the quatrains from line 231 to 246 and loosens them before and after. The typescript had a thought that "may pass" across her brain; Pound wrote that Tiresias must make up his mind whether he knows, and the poem now says her brain "allows" it. Critics have read Tiresias's watching as compassion and as voyeurism. The lines say "assaults," "no defence," "indifference," and give her the last word: "Well now that's done: and I'm glad it's over."

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Rewrite.

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- "The most argued-over scene in the poem." — superlative nobody could check, as an opening fragment.
- "A typist in a bedsit, her underwear ... ; a 'small house agent's clerk,' ... ; a seduction that is really an assault met with indifference." — one sentence of clauses stacked on semicolons; the last clause is a reading passed off as summary.
- "blesses the clerk's exit with a rhyme" — annotator's wit; I could not find the rhyme it means.
- "the rhymes are doing the judging" — metaphor describing the poem instead of explaining it.
- "Against Eliot's tentative 'perhaps' in one line he wrote 'Perhaps be damned.'" — anecdote for colour; does nothing for the line.
- "What survived is the poem's cruellest writing" — superlative nobody could check.
- "the cruelty is aimed as much at the eighteenth-century manner it imitates as at the two people on the divan" — balanced antithesis; a reading dressed as fact.
- "Whether Tiresias is compassionate or a voyeur is a question the poem asks about you." — second-person flourish; bow-tying last sentence.

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- "house agent" is not glossed (an estate agent); a non-British reader needs it.
- The Bradford parenthesis says the wool towns made fortunes but not that a silk hat is a gentleman's top hat, which is the point of the simile.
- Nothing on "Thebes below the wall" or "the lowest of the dead" (245–246), inside this note's range; I wanted both.
- "eighteenth-century manner": what manner? I cannot tell what is meant or whether to believe it.
- "rhymed quatrains, abab": true of 231–246; 247–252 are looser. Fine as a description, but "mostly" would be honest.
- Two Pound quotations where one, or none, would do.

**Keep**
- The word glosses: combinations (one-piece underwear), stays (corset), carbuncular (pimpled), dinner from tins, the divan a bed by night.
- Bradford as the Yorkshire wool town that made wartime fortunes.
- That the passage is in rhymed quatrains, abab.
- "foresuffered": Tiresias has been through it before.
- One Pound phrase, if the scholar wants any: "verse not interesting enough as verse to warrant so much of it", to say the draft ran longer and was cut.
- That Tiresias's attitude is disputed, stated as a dispute and not put to me.

**Proposed body**
A typist in a rented room. "Combinations" are one-piece underwear and "stays" a corset; her supper comes out of tins; the divan is her bed at night. Her visitor is a clerk in a house agent's (estate agent's) office, "carbuncular" meaning pimpled. Bradford, the Yorkshire wool town, made new millionaires in the war, and a silk hat is a gentleman's top hat. The passage is mostly in rhymed quatrains, abab. "Foresuffered" means Tiresias has been through all this before. Thebes is the city where he prophesied, and he is among the dead in Hades when Odysseus consults him (<i>Odyssey</i> XI). The draft ran longer; Pound wrote beside it "verse not interesting enough as verse to warrant so much of it," and Eliot cut it. Whether Tiresias's watching is pity or voyeurism has been read both ways.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** the stays moved from the window to the divan (scholar; the poem's own placing); the word glosses the reader asked for; the quatrain claim corrected to what the text does; Eliot's note at 218 pointed to and kept distinct from what the lines say; one Pound phrase, not two; the "may pass / allows" detail left out of the gloss, since the two scholars place Pound's comment differently and neither has the facsimile; all superlatives, "the rhymes are doing the judging", "blesses ... with a rhyme" and the second-person close cut. The two readings of Tiresias given as readings.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept. (Pound's "make up yr mind you Tiresias" is confirmed at second hand; which typescript line it stood against I still cannot verify, so leaving the "may pass / allows" detail out is right.)

**Reader, round 2:** Object: three sentences in the first paragraph.

"The typist's room is a bedsit: food from tins, the divan her bed at night, her "combinations" (a one-piece undergarment) drying out of the window, her "stays" (a corset) piled on the divan with the stockings and camisoles." — a colon and a list of four; "bedsit" is British. Put instead: "The typist lives in one rented room. Her food comes out of tins, the divan is her bed at night, her "combinations" (a one-piece undergarment) are drying out of the window, and her "stays" (a corset) are piled on the divan with the stockings and camisoles."

"Her visitor is a "small house agent's clerk", an estate agent's clerk, pimply ("carbuncular"), with the assurance of new money: Bradford, the Yorkshire wool town, stood for fortunes made in the war, and a silk hat is a gentleman's top hat." — "with the assurance of new money" muddles the simile: he is "one of the low", and it is his assurance, not his money, that is compared to the hat. Put instead: "Her visitor is a clerk in a house agent's (an estate agent's) office; "carbuncular" means pimply. Bradford, the Yorkshire wool town, stood for fortunes made in the war, and a silk hat is a gentleman's top hat."

"and the lines themselves say "assaults", "no defence" and "indifference"." — the poem quoted back at a reader who has it in front of them. Cut; end the sentence at "foretells the rest".

The second paragraph is accepted as it stands. Thebes and "the lowest of the dead" (245–246) are still unglossed; that is the scholar's call, not mine.

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's first and third changes taken whole. For the second, the reader's two sentences left Bradford and the silk hat hanging on nothing, so they are tied to the simile they gloss, by line number, without explaining it.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept (the simile is at 233–234; the quatrains 231–246; Eliot's note at 218 quoted exactly).

**Reader, round 3:** Accept. "In the simile at 233–234, Bradford is the Yorkshire wool town that stood for fortunes made in the war, and a silk hat is a gentleman's top hat." — tied to the line by number, glosses the two words, explains nothing that the reader can do alone.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: The typist and the clerk
body: The typist lives in one rented room. Her food comes out of tins, the divan is her bed at night, her "combinations" (a one-piece undergarment) are drying out of the window, and her "stays" (a corset) are piled on the divan with the stockings and camisoles. Her visitor is a clerk in a house agent's (an estate agent's) office; "carbuncular" means pimply. In the simile at 233–234, Bradford is the Yorkshire wool town that stood for fortunes made in the war, and a silk hat is a gentleman's top hat. Eliot's note at line 218 says that what Tiresias sees "is the substance of the poem"; here Tiresias says he has "foresuffered all" and foretells the rest.

In the typescript the scene ran in regular quatrains rhyming abab, and Pound cut several stanzas, writing beside it that the verse was "not interesting enough as verse" for so much of it. What survives keeps the quatrains from 231 to 246 and loosens them before and after. Critics have read Tiresias's watching as compassion and as voyeurism.

### lovely-woman (lines 253–256)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: Goldsmith's song, and a gramophone
quote: When lovely woman stoops to folly,<br>And finds too late that men betray,<br>What charm can soothe her melancholy,<br>What art can wash her guilt away?<br><br>The only art her guilt to cover,<br>To hide her shame from every eye,<br>To give repentance to her lover,<br>And wring his bosom, is—to die.
body: Olivia's song in <i>The Vicar of Wakefield</i> (1766), chapter 24, sung after her seduction. In Goldsmith the fallen woman's only art is to die. In Eliot she "smoothes her hair with automatic hand, / And puts a record on the gramophone." The gramophone is the poem's newest object, and "automatic" is its most modern word: the eighteenth-century ballad meter runs on into a room where nobody feels anything and a machine does the singing. Whether you hear that as contempt for her or relief for her decides how you read the poem.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction

**Errors**
- "the eighteenth-century ballad meter runs on into a room". Wrong. Goldsmith's song is in four-beat lines (tetrameter, abab); Eliot's lines 253–256 are pentameter, abab. Eliot takes Goldsmith's first line and adds "and" to fill his own line; the metre does not run on. Source: the song in the Gutenberg text (https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/2667/pg2667.txt, chapter XXIV) against the poem. The site's source record goldsmith-vicar says "in the same meter", which is wrong for the same reason; for shared-proposals-part3.md.
- "sung after her seduction". Vague. Chapter XXIV, "Fresh calamities": some days after her father has brought her home, the family breakfasts on the honeysuckle bank where she first met the squire; her mother asks her for "that little melancholy air your pappa was so fond of"; as she ends the last stanza Mr Thornhill's carriage appears. Source: same Gutenberg text.
- Quote field: the Gutenberg text reads "What charm can sooth her melancholy" and "And wring his bosom—is to die." The site has "soothe" and "is—to die." Editions differ; match one and say which. Minor.
- "The gramophone is the poem's newest object, and "automatic" is its most modern word". Uncheckable, and probably false: the taxi at 215–217 and the "motors" at 197 are as new as a gramophone (the name dates from 1887), and "C.i.f." at 211 is trade jargon of the day.

**Readings passed off as fact**
- "a room where nobody feels anything and a machine does the singing". A reading.
- "Whether you hear that as contempt for her or relief for her decides how you read the poem." Second person; the two readings belong in the note as readings.

**Missing**
Nothing a reader of the line needs beyond the correction of the metre.

**Cut**
"The gramophone is the poem's newest object ... does the singing." / "Whether you hear that ... how you read the poem."

**Proposed body**
Title — Goldsmith's song

Olivia Primrose's song in <i>The Vicar of Wakefield</i> (1766), chapter 24, sung to her family at her mother's asking some days after her father has brought her home from the squire who seduced her; they are sitting where she first met him, and his carriage appears as she finishes. Eliot takes Goldsmith's first line and adds "and" to carry it into his own longer line; Goldsmith's lines have four beats, Eliot's five. In the song the only art left to the fallen woman is to die. Eliot's typist "smoothes her hair with automatic hand, / And puts a record on the gramophone." The scene has been read as contempt for her and, by others, as a kind of mercy: she is not required to die.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Trim.

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- "The gramophone is the poem's newest object, and 'automatic' is its most modern word" — two superlatives nobody could check.
- ": the eighteenth-century ballad meter runs on into a room where nobody feels anything and a machine does the singing" — colon-led metaphor describing the poem; "a machine does the singing" is the annotator's wit.
- "Whether you hear that as contempt for her or relief for her decides how you read the poem." — second-person flourish; bow-tying last sentence.
- Title "Goldsmith's song, and a gramophone" — the tacked-on ", and a [thing]" flourish; the same tic in "The Queen and Lord Robert, in a barge" and "Augustine and the Buddha, burning".

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- "relief for her": relief felt by whom? Unclear.
- The note does not say the plain thing the quote shows: line 253 is Goldsmith's first line taken whole, and the stanza then goes Eliot's own way.
- "In Goldsmith the fallen woman's only art is to die. In Eliot she ..." is a balanced antithesis, but it is the point of the echo; keep it in plainer words.

**Keep**
- Olivia's song, The Vicar of Wakefield (1766), chapter 24, sung after her seduction.
- The contrast: in Goldsmith she can only die; here she puts on a record.
- That the tone is disputed, said as a dispute.

**Proposed body**
Plainer title — Goldsmith's song
Olivia's song in <i>The Vicar of Wakefield</i> (1766), chapter 24, sung after her seduction. Eliot takes Goldsmith's first line whole and finishes the stanza his own way. In Goldsmith the betrayed woman has nothing left but to die; the typist smooths her hair and puts a record on. The contrast has been read as contempt for her and as sympathy with her.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** the scholar's correction of the metre claim (Goldsmith four beats, Eliot five; the first line taken whole with "and" added); the chapter's setting in one clause; the superlatives and the "machine does the singing" figure cut; the two readings given as readings. The quote field is left as it is: Gutenberg's text reads "sooth" and "bosom—is to die", the site "soothe" and "is—to die"; editions differ, and the library record (another thread's) has the same wording as the gloss, so the two should be settled together (noted in the shared proposals).

**Scholar, round 2:** Object: "some days after her father has brought her home from the squire who seduced her". The Vicar did not fetch her from the squire: he found her at an inn (the Harrow), where the landlady was turning her out after the squire had cast her off (ch. 21), and brought her home; and in ch. 23 more than a week passes before the song, plus George's three-day errand (Gutenberg #2667, chs. 21–24). Replace the first sentence with: "Olivia Primrose's song in <i>The Vicar of Wakefield</i> (1766), chapter 24, sung to her family some weeks after her father has found her at an inn, cast off by the squire who seduced her, and brought her home."

**Reader, round 2:** Object: the close.

"Eliot's typist "smoothes her hair with automatic hand, / And puts a record on the gramophone." The scene has been read as contempt for her and as a kind of mercy: she is not required to die." — two lines of the poem quoted back, then a colon that ties the bow. Put instead: "In the song the only art left to the fallen woman is to die; Eliot's typist puts a record on the gramophone. The scene has been read as contempt for her and as a kind of mercy." (replacing the last three sentences).

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the scholar's first sentence (the Vicar found her at an inn; weeks, not days); the reader's close.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Goldsmith's song
quote: When lovely woman stoops to folly,<br>And finds too late that men betray,<br>What charm can soothe her melancholy,<br>What art can wash her guilt away?<br><br>The only art her guilt to cover,<br>To hide her shame from every eye,<br>To give repentance to her lover,<br>And wring his bosom, is—to die.
body: Olivia Primrose's song in <i>The Vicar of Wakefield</i> (1766), chapter 24, sung to her family some weeks after her father has found her at an inn, cast off by the squire who seduced her, and brought her home. Eliot takes Goldsmith's first line and adds "and" to carry it into his own longer line; Goldsmith's lines have four beats, Eliot's five. In the song the only art left to the fallen woman is to die; Eliot's typist puts a record on the gramophone. The scene has been read as contempt for her and as a kind of mercy.

### strand (lines 257–265)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: From the Strand to Lower Thames Street
body: Ferdinand's line from <i>The Tempest</i> again (Eliot's note: "as above"), and then a walk: along the Strand, up Queen Victoria Street, which runs from Blackfriars to the Bank, and down to Lower Thames Street, where Billingsgate fish market stood and the "fishmen" drank at noon. The mandoline is real; so is the pub. And so is St Magnus the Martyr, Wren's church at the foot of the old London Bridge, rebuilt 1671–87, with a white and gold interior of Ionic columns. In 1920 a commission chaired by Lord Phillimore proposed pulling down nineteen City churches, St Magnus among them; the London County Council's report against the scheme, <i>The Proposed Demolition of Nineteen City Churches</i>, is the pamphlet Eliot's note cites, and the church is still there. "Inexplicable splendour" is the first unqualified praise of anything in the poem, and it is given to a building on a street of fish.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction

**Errors**
- "and then a walk". The poem says the music "crept by me upon the waters / And along the Strand, up Queen Victoria Street": it is the music that travels. A walker is a reading.
- "rebuilt 1671–87". Not wrong (Wikipedia gives 1671–87 overall), but the pamphlet Eliot cites dates it: "rebuilt by Wren in 1676, the steeple being added in 1705". Source: LCC report, St Magnus entry (https://archive.org/details/cu31924015376233).
- "In 1920 a commission chaired by Lord Phillimore proposed pulling down nineteen City churches". Right in substance; the dates are: commission appointed by the Bishop of London 26 March 1919; report dated 14 April 1920; nineteen churches to be removed, seven towers (St Magnus's among them) to be kept. Source: the LCC report's preface. Phillimore as chairman: Wikipedia's St Magnus article (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Magnus-the-Martyr) and memory.
- "the London County Council's report against the scheme, The Proposed Demolition of Nineteen City Churches, is the pamphlet Eliot's note cites". Right: report by the clerk and the architect of the Council, dated 12 October 1920, P. S. King & Son. Missing from the gloss is what it says: St Magnus is "one of the most beautiful of all Wren's works", but the interior "although well proportioned, exhibits faults of design ... and is disappointing". Eliot's note ("to my mind one of the finest among Wren's interiors") answers the pamphlet it cites. Source: same report, p. 18–19.
- "with a white and gold interior of Ionic columns". Ionic columns: confirmed by the LCC report ("slender Ionic columns on octagonal bases"). "White and gold" is Eliot's; the interior was redone by Martin Travers in 1924 in a baroque manner (Wikipedia), so what Eliot saw in 1921 is not what stands now. One clause.
- "where Billingsgate fish market stood". It stood on Lower Thames Street until 1982 (from memory; general knowledge).
- Eliot's own view at the time of writing is on record and pre-1930: his London Letter in <i>The Dial</i>, June 1921 (dated May), says the threatened churches "give to the business quarter of London a beauty which its hideous banks and commercial houses have not quite defaced" and mentions the LCC pamphlet. Source: https://theworld.com/~raparker/exploring/tseliot/works/london-letters/london-letter-1921-06.html.
- Queen Victoria Street from Blackfriars to the Bank: right (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Victoria_Street,_London).

**Readings passed off as fact**
- "a walk".
- "The mandoline is real; so is the pub." Unsupported; nothing is known of either.
- ""Inexplicable splendour" is the first unqualified praise of anything in the poem, and it is given to a building on a street of fish." Uncheckable superlative and a flourish.

**Missing**
The LCC pamphlet's verdict on the interior, which Eliot's note contradicts; Eliot's Dial letter.

**Cut**
"The mandoline is real; so is the pub." / ""Inexplicable splendour" is the first unqualified praise ... a street of fish."

**Proposed body**
Title — From the Strand to Lower Thames Street

Ferdinand's line from <i>The Tempest</i> again (Eliot's note: "as above"), and then the music's course through the City: along the Strand, up Queen Victoria Street, which runs from Blackfriars to the Bank, and down to Lower Thames Street, where Billingsgate fish market stood until 1982 and the "fishmen" drink at noon. St Magnus the Martyr is Wren's church at the north end of the old London Bridge, rebuilt after the Great Fire, finished in 1676 with the steeple added in 1705; its nave is divided from the aisles by Ionic columns, which is what "Ionian" is usually taken to mean. In April 1920 a commission appointed by the Bishop of London and chaired by Lord Phillimore recommended removing nineteen City churches, St Magnus among them. The London County Council's reply is the pamphlet Eliot's note cites, <i>The Proposed Demolition of Nineteen City Churches</i> (October 1920): it calls St Magnus "one of the most beautiful of all Wren's works" but finds the interior "disappointing," which Eliot's note answers with "one of the finest among Wren's interiors." He had already written, in <i>The Dial</i> in June 1921, that the threatened churches gave the City "a beauty which its hideous banks and commercial houses have not quite defaced." The church stands; its interior was redecorated by Martin Travers in 1924.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Rewrite.

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- "and then a walk: along the Strand, up Queen Victoria Street, ... and down to Lower Thames Street" — tour-guide framing on a colon.
- "The mandoline is real; so is the pub. And so is St Magnus the Martyr" — rhythmic triple; "real" is a claim nobody could check.
- "'Inexplicable splendour' is the first unqualified praise of anything in the poem, and it is given to a building on a street of fish." — counting superlative nobody could check; "a street of fish" is the annotator's wit; bow-tying last sentence.

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- "Ferdinand's line from The Tempest again": "again" assumes I know the same speech is behind 191–192; nothing says so.
- The poem says "Ionian", the note says "Ionic columns", and neither is connected to the other; one clause would do it.
- "which runs from Blackfriars to the Bank": I needed the direction only (east into the City, down to the river).
- "The mandoline is real; so is the pub": on what evidence? A reading dressed as fact.
- The 1920 report is the one thing I wanted here, because Eliot's note cites the pamphlet without saying what it was; the note gives it, but under the flourishes.

**Keep**
- The Tempest, I, ii, Ferdinand's own line; "as above" points to Eliot's note on 192.
- The three streets as a route east into the City; Billingsgate fish market on Lower Thames Street, hence "fishmen".
- St Magnus the Martyr: Wren, Lower Thames Street, foot of the old London Bridge, rebuilt 1671–87, white and gold, Ionic columns.
- The Phillimore commission's 1920 proposal to demolish nineteen City churches, St Magnus among them; the LCC report against it is Eliot's pamphlet; the church survived.

**Proposed body**
Ferdinand's line in <i>The Tempest</i>, I, ii, as he follows Ariel's music; the same speech is behind lines 191–192, which is what Eliot's "as above" refers to. The Strand, Queen Victoria Street and Lower Thames Street run east into the City and down to the river. Billingsgate fish market stood on Lower Thames Street, hence the "fishmen." St Magnus the Martyr is Wren's church on the same street, at the foot of the old London Bridge, rebuilt 1671–87; "Ionian" is the Ionic order of its columns, and the interior is white and gold. In 1920 a commission under Lord Phillimore proposed demolishing nineteen City churches, St Magnus among them. The London County Council's report against the scheme is the pamphlet Eliot's note cites. The church is still there.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** it is the music that travels along the Strand, not a walker (scholar); St Magnus's dates from the pamphlet Eliot cites; the Phillimore commission and the LCC reply as the scholar found them, with the pamphlet's verdict on the interior, which Eliot's note answers; "Ionian" tied to the Ionic columns (reader); Billingsgate's closing date; the redecoration of 1924 in a clause. Eliot's Dial letter of 1921 left out as more than the line needs. "The mandoline is real; so is the pub" and the "street of fish" close cut.

**Scholar, round 2:** Object, one word: "the "fishmen" drink at noon". The poem's word is "lounge" ("Where fishmen lounge at noon"); the drinking is inferred from the public bar. Replace with: "where Billingsgate fish market stood until 1982 and the "fishmen" lounge at noon." Everything else confirmed (church dates and the pamphlet's verdict from the LCC report; the commission's dates from its preface; Phillimore from the St Magnus article).

**Reader, round 2:** Object: the opening, one fact, and the pamphlet sentence.

"Ferdinand's line from <i>The Tempest</i> again (Eliot's note: "as above"), and then the music's course through the City: along the Strand, up Queen Victoria Street, which runs from Blackfriars to the Bank, and down to Lower Thames Street, where Billingsgate fish market stood until 1982 and the "fishmen" drink at noon." — a fragment with no verb, the "and then ... :" walk-through, and "again" still unexplained; the Strand is not in the City. Put instead: "Ferdinand's line in <i>The Tempest</i>, I, ii, from the speech already echoed at lines 191–192, which is what Eliot's "as above" refers to. The music goes along the Strand, up Queen Victoria Street, which runs from Blackfriars to the Bank, and down to Lower Thames Street, where Billingsgate fish market stood until 1982, hence the "fishmen"."

"with the steeple added in 1705" — the line is about the interior; the steeple does not help with it. Cut, so the sentence ends "rebuilt after the Great Fire and finished in 1676."

"The London County Council's reply is the pamphlet Eliot's note cites, <i>The Proposed Demolition of Nineteen City Churches</i> (October 1920): it calls St Magnus "one of the most beautiful of all Wren's works" but finds the interior "disappointing", which Eliot's note answers with "one of the finest among Wren's interiors"." — one sentence carrying a title, a date, two quotations and a verdict on Eliot's motive. Put instead: "The London County Council's reply, <i>The Proposed Demolition of Nineteen City Churches</i> (October 1920), is the pamphlet Eliot's note cites. It calls St Magnus "one of the most beautiful of all Wren's works" but finds the interior "disappointing"; Eliot's "to my mind one of the finest among Wren's interiors" takes the other side."

The rest is accepted.

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's opening, which drops "drink at noon" (so the scholar's "lounge" point is met), the steeple cut, and the pamphlet sentence split; all taken. Round 3: with the steeple gone, "finished in 1676" was no longer true as phrased (the scholar), so "the body of the church complete by 1676".

**Scholar, round 3:** One correction. With the steeple clause cut, "rebuilt after the Great Fire and finished in 1676" is no longer true as phrased: the body was complete by 1676 and the steeple added in 1705 (LCC report, St Magnus entry; Wikipedia). Replace "rebuilt after the Great Fire and finished in 1676" with "rebuilt after the Great Fire, the body of the church complete by 1676". The rest stands: "as above" does refer to the note at 192 (Tempest I, ii), and lines 191–192 echo the same speech; Eliot's note is quoted exactly.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: From the Strand to Lower Thames Street
body: Ferdinand's line in <i>The Tempest</i>, I, ii, from the speech already echoed at lines 191–192, which is what Eliot's "as above" refers to. The music goes along the Strand, up Queen Victoria Street, which runs from Blackfriars to the Bank, and down to Lower Thames Street, where Billingsgate fish market stood until 1982, hence the "fishmen". St Magnus the Martyr is Wren's church at the north end of the old London Bridge, rebuilt after the Great Fire, the body of the church complete by 1676; "Ionian" is usually taken to mean the Ionic columns that divide its nave from the aisles. In April 1920 a commission appointed by the Bishop of London and chaired by Lord Phillimore recommended removing nineteen City churches, St Magnus among them. The London County Council's reply, <i>The Proposed Demolition of Nineteen City Churches</i> (October 1920), is the pamphlet Eliot's note cites. It calls St Magnus "one of the most beautiful of all Wren's works" but finds the interior "disappointing"; Eliot's "to my mind one of the finest among Wren's interiors" takes the other side. The church stands; its interior was redecorated in 1924.

### thames-daughters (lines 266–278)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: The Rhine-daughters on the Thames
body: "Weialala leia / Wallala leialala" is the refrain of the three Rhine-daughters in Wagner's <i>Götterdämmerung</i>, Act III scene i, as they swim in the river lamenting the gold that was stolen from them at the beginning of the <i>Ring</i>. Eliot's note says the Song of the Thames-daughters begins here, and that from line 292 to 306 they speak in turn. First the river as it was in 1921: sweating oil and tar, worked by sailing barges with red sails drifting down Greenwich Reach past the Isle of Dogs, the loop of docks on the river's north bank. The lines shorten to two and three words, a water-rhythm, and the river's own noise takes over.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs hedging

**Errors**
- No error of fact. Act III scene 1 of <i>Götterdämmerung</i> opens with the three Rhine-daughters swimming and singing of the lost gold before Siegfried appears; Alberich stole it in the first scene of <i>Das Rheingold</i>. Source: the libretto (https://www.murashev.com/opera/Götterdämmerung_libretto_German).
- One caution on the refrain: the libretto texts online, which follow the vocal score, print the refrain as "Weialala, weialala heia leia wallala la / heia la la lei la la la la la la lei". Eliot's "Weialala leia / Wallala leialala" is the form in the printed poem of the Ring (from memory, unverified here). The site's source record prints Eliot's form as Wagner's; worth a check before the quote field is called accurate.
- "the loop of docks on the river's north bank". The Isle of Dogs is a peninsula in a meander on the north bank, opposite Greenwich, holding the West India and Millwall docks; Greenwich Reach is the stretch between Limehouse Reach and Blackwall Reach, with the Isle of Dogs on its north side and Greenwich on the south. Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Dogs; https://thames.me.uk/s00035.htm. "Loop" is imprecise, not wrong.
- "First the river as it was in 1921". A fair description of 266–276, but "as it was in 1921" is the annotator's dating; say "the working river of Eliot's day".

**Readings passed off as fact**
- "The lines shorten to two and three words, a water-rhythm, and the river's own noise takes over." Named in the standard; a metaphor for the poem, not an explanation of it.

**Missing**
Nothing.

**Cut**
The last sentence.

**Proposed body**
Title — The Rhine-daughters on the Thames

"Weialala leia / Wallala leialala" is the refrain of the three Rhine-daughters at the opening of Act III of Wagner's <i>Götterdämmerung</i>, as they swim and sing of the gold stolen from them at the start of the <i>Ring</i>. Eliot's note says the Song of the Thames-daughters begins here and that from line 292 to 306 they speak in turn. Lines 266–276 give the working river of Eliot's day: oil and tar, sailing barges with red sails drifting with the tide down Greenwich Reach, the stretch opposite Greenwich, past the Isle of Dogs, the peninsula of docks on the north bank.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Trim.

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- "First the river as it was in 1921: sweating oil and tar, worked by sailing barges with red sails drifting down Greenwich Reach past the Isle of Dogs" — colon-led paraphrase of the lines I have just read.
- "The lines shorten to two and three words, a water-rhythm, and the river's own noise takes over." — counting the words; two metaphors describing the poem instead of explaining it.

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- "the loop of docks on the river's north bank": is the loop the docks or the river? The Isle of Dogs is the peninsula inside a loop of the river.
- The second sentence restates Eliot's note, which is printed beside it.
- "as it was in 1921": why that year? Say "when the poem was written" or drop it.
- I wanted a word on why the sails are red (Thames sailing barges, rust-red dressed sails) and on the "heavy spar" (the sprit). Both are mine to propose and the scholar's to confirm.

**Keep**
- The refrain is the Rhine-daughters' in Götterdämmerung, III, i; they swim and lament their stolen gold.
- Greenwich Reach and the Isle of Dogs placed on the map.

**Proposed body**
The refrain is sung by the three Rhine-daughters in Wagner's <i>Götterdämmerung</i>, Act III scene i, as they swim and lament the gold stolen from them at the start of the <i>Ring</i>; Eliot's note makes the three speeches from line 292 their Thames counterparts. The barges are Thames sailing barges, whose sails were dressed red-brown; the "heavy spar" is the sprit that carries the mainsail. Greenwich Reach is the stretch of river at Greenwich, and the Isle of Dogs the peninsula opposite, inside a loop of the river, then given over to docks.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** Wagner as both had it; Eliot's note kept in one sentence because the gloss hangs on the line the note is about; the river of "Eliot's day" rather than "1921" (scholar); the Isle of Dogs and Greenwich Reach placed exactly (scholar); the reader's two facts, the red-brown dressed sails and the sprit as the "heavy spar", added for the scholar to confirm; "a water-rhythm" and "the river's own noise" cut.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept. The reader's two facts confirmed: Thames sailing barges' flax sails were dressed with red ochre and oil, which gave them their rust-red colour, and the rig is a spritsail, the mainsail loose-footed and set on the sprit, a heavy spar (Wikipedia, Thames sailing barge: "red ochre, cod oil, urine and seawater"; the example barge's sprit 59 ft long). Since red-sailed Thames barges were spritsail-rigged, "the "heavy spar" the sprit" needs no hedge.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: the last sentence.

"Lines 266–276 give the working river of Eliot's day: oil and tar, and sailing barges, their sails dressed red-brown and the "heavy spar" the sprit that carries the mainsail, drifting with the tide down Greenwich Reach, the stretch opposite Greenwich, past the Isle of Dogs, the peninsula of docks on the north bank." — appositions inside appositions; "drifting" has lost its barges by the time it arrives; "oil and tar" is the poem quoted back. Put instead: "Lines 266–276 give the working river of Eliot's day. The barges are Thames sailing barges, whose sails were dressed red-brown; the "heavy spar" is the sprit that carries the mainsail. Greenwich Reach is the stretch of river opposite Greenwich, and the Isle of Dogs is the peninsula of docks on its north bank."

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's last three sentences, with the scholar's confirmation of the sails and the sprit.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept (Greenwich Reach has Greenwich on its south bank and the Isle of Dogs on its north).

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: The Rhine-daughters on the Thames
body: "Weialala leia / Wallala leialala" is the refrain of the three Rhine-daughters at the opening of Act III of Wagner's <i>Götterdämmerung</i>, as they swim and sing of the gold stolen from them at the start of the <i>Ring</i>. Eliot's note says the Song of the Thames-daughters begins here and that from line 292 to 306 they speak in turn. Lines 266–276 give the working river of Eliot's day. The barges are Thames sailing barges, whose sails were dressed red-brown; the "heavy spar" is the sprit that carries the mainsail. Greenwich Reach is the stretch of river opposite Greenwich, and the Isle of Dogs is the peninsula of docks on its north bank.

### elizabeth-leicester (lines 279–291)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: The Queen and Lord Robert, in a barge
quote: In the afternoon we were in a barge, watching the games on the river. (The queen) was alone with Lord Robert and myself on the poop, when they began to talk nonsense, and went so far that Lord Robert at last said, as I was on the spot there was no reason why they should not be married if the queen pleased.
body: Eliot's note quotes Froude's <i>History of England</i>, which quotes a letter of 1561 from Álvaro de la Quadra, the Spanish ambassador, to Philip II, about Elizabeth I flirting with Robert Dudley, later Earl of Leicester, on the Thames. The stern "formed / A gilded shell," the red and gold, the peal of bells from the "White towers" (the Tower of London's keep), the south-west wind: it is Cleopatra's barge again, real this time, and going nowhere, since the queen never married. The second Rhine-daughters' refrain closes the sixteenth century, and the three modern daughters begin.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs hedging

**Errors**
- "Eliot's note quotes Froude's History of England, which quotes a letter of 1561 from Álvaro de la Quadra, the Spanish ambassador, to Philip II". Right. Froude heads it "De Quadra to Philip, London, June 30" [1561], in ch. iv, p. 244 of the Everyman <i>Reign of Elizabeth</i> vol. I that Eliot cites (https://archive.org/details/reignofelizabeth01frouuoft). The Simancas calendar has the same letter, Quadra to the King, 30 June 1561, and dates the water party to St John's day, 24 June, at a feast given by Lord Robert (https://www.british-history.ac.uk/cal-state-papers/simancas/vol1/pp205-209). De Quadra was Bishop of Aquila, Philip's ambassador in London 1559–63 (from memory).
- Quote field: Froude's text reads "She was alone with the Lord Robert and myself on the poop". The "(The queen)" and the dropped "the" are Eliot's. The quote as given is Eliot's note, not Froude; either say so or restore Froude's words.
- "the peal of bells from the "White towers" (the Tower of London's keep)". The keep is one White Tower; the poem has "White towers", plural, and Eliot does not identify them. The Tower of London is the usual guess; I do not recall Ricks and McCue settling it (unverified). Hedge.
- "Robert Dudley, later Earl of Leicester": right; created earl in 1564 (from memory).
- "since the queen never married": right, but the reason the marriage was impossible is nearer: Dudley's wife Amy Robsart had died in September 1560 in a fall that left him suspected (from memory; the site's source note says the same).
- None of the barge's furniture (gilded stern, red and gold, south-west wind, bells, towers) is in the letter as Froude gives it. Source: the Froude page.

**Readings passed off as fact**
- "it is Cleopatra's barge again, real this time, and going nowhere". The link to line 77 ("The Chair she sat in, like a burnished throne", Enobarbus on Cleopatra's barge, whose "poop was beaten gold") is a critics' reading and can be given as one; "real this time" is loose, since Cleopatra's barge is Plutarch's and Shakespeare's and Elizabeth's is de Quadra's report; "going nowhere" is the annotator's.
- "The second Rhine-daughters' refrain closes the sixteenth century". Fine as structure if said plainly.

**Missing**
The date of the outing; that the pageantry is Eliot's, not the letter's; why the marriage could not happen.

**Cut**
"it is Cleopatra's barge again, real this time, and going nowhere" as worded.

**Proposed body**
Title — The Queen and Lord Robert, in a barge

Eliot's note quotes Froude's <i>History of England</i> (the Everyman volume <i>Elizabeth</i>, I, ch. iv), which translates a letter of 30 June 1561 from Álvaro de la Quadra, Bishop of Aquila and Philip II's ambassador in London, reporting a water party on St John's day at which Elizabeth and Robert Dudley, made Earl of Leicester three years later, joked in his hearing about marrying. Froude's text has "She was alone with the Lord Robert and myself on the poop"; the parenthesis "(The queen)" is Eliot's. Dudley's wife had died the year before in a fall that left him suspected, and the marriage never took place. The gilded stern, the red and gold, the south-west wind and the peal of bells are not in the letter; "White towers" is not identified, and is usually taken as the Tower of London. Critics have set the passage beside the "burnished throne" of line 77, Shakespeare's Cleopatra in her barge. After the refrain the three Thames-daughters of Eliot's note speak in turn, lines 292–305.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Rewrite.

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- "The stern 'formed / A gilded shell,' the red and gold, the peal of bells from the 'White towers' (the Tower of London's keep), the south-west wind: it is Cleopatra's barge again, real this time, and going nowhere, since the queen never married." — a list on a colon, then the annotator's epigram ("real this time, and going nowhere").
- "The second Rhine-daughters' refrain closes the sixteenth century, and the three modern daughters begin." — structural bow; "closes the sixteenth century" describes the poem in a figure rather than explaining anything.
- Title "The Queen and Lord Robert, in a barge" — the tacked-on ", in a barge".

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- "'White towers' (the Tower of London's keep)": the keep is one tower, the White Tower; the poem says towers. Given as fact, and I cannot tell whether to believe it. Say whose reading it is, or "usually taken as".
- "Cleopatra's barge again": I had to work out that "again" means line 77 and Eliot's note there. Say so.
- "flirting": the letter says they "began to talk nonsense" and got as far as marriage; "flirting" is looser than the note it glosses.
- "a letter of 1561": no source for the date beyond Froude; fine if the scholar confirms.
- The last sentence repeats Eliot's note on 266 and the thames-daughters gloss.

**Keep**
- Froude's History of England; De Quadra, the Spanish ambassador, writing to Philip II; Robert Dudley, later Earl of Leicester.
- That the marriage never happened.
- The Cleopatra's barge echo, tied to line 77, offered as a reading.

**Proposed body**
Plainer title — Elizabeth and Dudley
Eliot's note quotes Froude's <i>History of England</i>, which quotes a dispatch to Philip II from Álvaro de la Quadra, the Spanish ambassador, describing the queen on the river with Robert Dudley, whom she later made Earl of Leicester. The marriage never happened; Elizabeth never married. The gilded stern and the red and gold recall Enobarbus's description of Cleopatra's barge in <i>Antony and Cleopatra</i>, which Eliot's note on line 77 cites. The "White towers" have been taken as the Tower of London.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** the letter dated and its occasion given (scholar, from Froude and the Simancas calendar); the quote field restored to Froude's words, which the 1911 Everyman page confirms ("She was alone with the Lord Robert and myself on the poop"; the parenthesis "(The queen)" is Eliot's); Amy Robsart's death in a clause; the barge's furniture marked as Eliot's, not the letter's; "White towers" hedged; Cleopatra's barge given as a critics' comparison tied to line 77; the "closes the sixteenth century" sentence cut; the title plainer.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept. The quote now matches the Everyman page word for word (OCR p. 244: "She was alone with the Lord Robert and myself on the poop"). "A water party" is fair: the letter's words are "in the afternoon we were in a barge, watching the games on the river", at a party given by Dudley on St John's day, 24 June (Froude's preceding paragraph; the Simancas calendar, 30 June 1561). If the editor wants the letter's own terms: "reporting an afternoon in a barge watching games on the river, at a party given by Dudley on St John's day".

**Reader, round 2:** Object: the first sentence, and one phrase.

"Eliot's note quotes Froude's <i>History of England</i> (the volume on Elizabeth, chapter iv), which translates a letter of 30 June 1561 from Álvaro de la Quadra, Philip II's ambassador in London, reporting a water party at which Elizabeth and Robert Dudley, made Earl of Leicester three years later, joked in his hearing about marrying. Dudley's wife had died the year before in a fall that left him under suspicion, and the marriage never took place." — fifty words and three appositions before the verb. Put instead: "Eliot's note quotes Froude's <i>History of England</i> (the volume on Elizabeth, chapter iv). Froude translates a letter of 30 June 1561 from Álvaro de la Quadra, Philip II's ambassador in London, describing a water party at which Elizabeth and Robert Dudley joked in his hearing about marrying. Dudley was made Earl of Leicester three years later. His wife had died the year before the outing, in a fall that left him under suspicion, and the marriage never took place."

"the "White towers" are not identified, and are usually taken as the Tower of London" — not identified by whom? Put instead: "Eliot does not say what the "White towers" are; they are usually taken as the Tower of London."

The rest, and the new quote, accepted.

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's split of the first sentence and the "White towers" sentence; the scholar's quote confirmed against the Everyman page; "a water party" kept, which the scholar allows.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept (Leicester 1564, Amy Robsart September 1560, line 77 "burnished throne" all right as now phrased).

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Elizabeth and Leicester on the river
quote: In the afternoon we were in a barge, watching the games on the river. She was alone with the Lord Robert and myself on the poop, when they began to talk nonsense, and went so far that Lord Robert at last said, as I was on the spot there was no reason why they should not be married if the queen pleased.
body: Eliot's note quotes Froude's <i>History of England</i> (the volume on Elizabeth, chapter iv). Froude translates a letter of 30 June 1561 from Álvaro de la Quadra, Philip II's ambassador in London, describing a water party at which Elizabeth and Robert Dudley joked in his hearing about marrying. Dudley was made Earl of Leicester three years later. His wife had died the year before the outing, in a fall that left him under suspicion, and the marriage never took place. The gilded stern, the red and gold, the south-west wind and the peal of bells are not in the letter. Eliot does not say what the "White towers" are; they are usually taken as the Tower of London. Critics have set the passage beside the "burnished throne" of line 77, Shakespeare's Cleopatra in her barge.

### highbury (lines 293–295)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: La Pia's line, on a north London girl
quote: “Ricorditi di me, che son la Pia;<br>Siena mi fé, disfecemi Maremma:<br>salsi colui che ’nnanellata pria<br>disposando m’avea con la sua gemma.”
body: <i>Purgatorio</i> V, 133–136. La Pia dei Tolomei of Siena, murdered, it was said, by her husband in his castle in the Maremma marshes, asks Dante for remembrance in a single sentence. Eliot's first Thames-daughter has her grammar exactly: born in Highbury (a north London suburb of trams and dusty trees), "undone" at Richmond and Kew, the pleasure reaches upriver where Londoners went boating, "Supine on the floor of a narrow canoe." Four lines carry the whole of a cheap seduction and the whole of Dante's line, and the girl does not even get a name.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs hedging

**Errors**
- Quote and translation: right against the Petrocchi text (Wikisource prints "m'aveva" for "m'avea"; both are current). Lines 133–136 are right: the canto has 136 lines and her speech is 130–136. Source: https://it.wikisource.org/wiki/Divina_Commedia/Purgatorio/Canto_V. Eliot's note gives "Siena mi fe', disfecemi Maremma."
- "murdered, it was said, by her husband in his castle in the Maremma marshes". Right as tradition: Dante gives no name but hers; the early commentators made her Pia de' Tolomei of Siena, wife of Nello dei Pannocchieschi, lord of Castel di Pietra in the Maremma, who had her killed there; motive and manner vary by commentator; nothing certain is known. From memory of the commentaries (Benvenuto, the Ottimo), unverified; the site's source record says the same.
- "born in Highbury (a north London suburb of trams and dusty trees)". Highbury is in Islington, north London; "Trams and dusty trees" is the daughter's own first line, 292, which opens her speech before the gloss's anchor. Say so.
- "the pleasure reaches upriver where Londoners went boating". Right in substance (Richmond and Kew are on the Thames above London); "pleasure reaches" is the annotator's.
- Draft: the leaf beginning "Highbury bore me" ran to nineteen lines for the first two daughters and was cut by ten (Rainey, <i>Revisiting The Waste Land</i>, as quoted in a web search; facsimile from memory, unverified).

**Readings passed off as fact**
- "Four lines carry the whole of a cheap seduction and the whole of Dante's line, and the girl does not even get a name." The fact inside (La Pia names herself; the daughter has no name) can be said plainly; the rest is a flourish.
- "has her grammar exactly": fair, but "keeps the shape of Dante's line" is plainer.

**Missing**
That her speech begins at 292; that the draft was longer.

**Cut**
The last sentence as worded.

**Proposed body**
Title — La Pia's line

<i>Purgatorio</i> V, 133–136. Among the late-repentant who died by violence, a woman gives Dante her name, her birthplace and the place of her death in one sentence, and points at her husband without naming him. The early commentators made her Pia de' Tolomei of Siena, killed on her husband's orders at his castle in the Maremma; nothing certain is known of her. The first Thames-daughter's speech (lines 292–295) keeps the shape of Dante's line, born in one place and undone in another: Highbury, in north London, "Trams and dusty trees"; Richmond and Kew, the reaches above London where Londoners went boating. She gives no name and no husband. In the draft the speech was longer and was cut to four lines.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Rewrite.

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- "asks Dante for remembrance in a single sentence" — counting flourish.
- "has her grammar exactly:" — a figure ("grammar") for the shape of the sentence, on a colon.
- "(a north London suburb of trams and dusty trees)" — the poem's own words handed back as description.
- "Four lines carry the whole of a cheap seduction and the whole of Dante's line, and the girl does not even get a name." — counting flourish; balanced "the whole of ... the whole of"; bow-tying last sentence.
- Title "La Pia's line, on a north London girl" — showy.

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- "the pleasure reaches upriver where Londoners went boating": I read "reaches" as a verb twice before seeing it meant stretches of river.
- The parallel is the whole point and the note leaves me to assemble it. Put the two sentences side by side.
- "murdered, it was said": a good hedge; the scholar may want to say by whom (the early commentators).

**Keep**
- Purgatorio V, 133–136; who La Pia was (Siena; murdered, it was said, by her husband in the Maremma).
- That the Thames-daughter's sentence is modelled on La Pia's.
- Highbury (north London suburb); Richmond and Kew (upriver; boating).
- That La Pia names herself and this speaker has no name, said plainly.

**Proposed body**
Plainer title — La Pia
<i>Purgatorio</i> V, 133–136. La Pia dei Tolomei of Siena, murdered, it was said, by her husband at his castle in the Maremma, asks Dante to remember her. The first Thames-daughter's sentence is modelled on hers: Siena made me, Maremma unmade me; Highbury bore me, Richmond and Kew undid me. Highbury is a suburb in north London. Richmond and Kew are on the Thames above London, where people went boating. La Pia gives her name; this speaker has none.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** La Pia as the scholar had her (the early commentators' identification, nothing certain known); the two sentences set side by side (reader); Highbury and the river placed plainly; "Trams and dusty trees" marked as the daughter's own first line; the counting close cut; the draft's longer version moved to the drafts record.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: the parallel is still described, not shown.

"The first Thames-daughter's speech (lines 292–295) keeps the shape of Dante's line, born in one place and undone in another: Highbury, in north London ("Trams and dusty trees" is her own first line); Richmond and Kew, on the river above London, where Londoners went boating." — a colon, a semicolon and a parenthesis in one sentence, and the reader is still left to lay the two lines side by side. Put instead: "The first Thames-daughter's speech (lines 292–295) keeps the shape of Dante's line: Siena made me, Maremma unmade me; Highbury bore me, Richmond and Kew undid me. Highbury is in north London, and "Trams and dusty trees" (line 292) is her own first line. Richmond and Kew are on the river above London, where Londoners went boating."

The rest accepted.

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's version, which lays the two lines side by side.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept (line 292 is "Trams and dusty trees").

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: La Pia's line
quote: “Ricorditi di me, che son la Pia;<br>Siena mi fé, disfecemi Maremma:<br>salsi colui che ’nnanellata pria<br>disposando m’avea con la sua gemma.”
body: <i>Purgatorio</i> V, 133–136. Among the late-repentant who died by violence, a woman gives Dante her name, her birthplace and the place of her death in one sentence, and points at her husband without naming him. The early commentators made her Pia de' Tolomei of Siena, killed on her husband's orders at his castle in the Maremma; nothing certain is known of her. The first Thames-daughter's speech (lines 292–295) keeps the shape of Dante's line: Siena made me, Maremma unmade me; Highbury bore me, Richmond and Kew undid me. Highbury is in north London, and "Trams and dusty trees" (line 292) is her own first line. Richmond and Kew are on the river above London, where Londoners went boating. She gives no name.

### moorgate (lines 296–299)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: The second daughter
body: Moorgate, in the City, where the clerks work. "After the event / He wept. He promised 'a new start.' / I made no comment. What should I resent?" The most terrible short speech in the poem, because it asks for nothing. He wept; she did not. It is the typist's "Well now that's done" without even the relief.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite

**Errors**
- "Moorgate, in the City, where the clerks work." Moorgate is a street and district on the north edge of the City, named from a gate in the old wall (from memory). "Where the clerks work" is an inference about her that the poem does not make.
- Nothing else in the note is checkable, because nothing else in it is a fact.

**Readings passed off as fact**
- "The most terrible short speech in the poem, because it asks for nothing." Uncheckable superlative.
- "He wept; she did not." The poem says "I made no comment," not that she did not weep.
- "It is the typist's "Well now that's done" without even the relief." Antithesis; a reading.

**Missing**
What "the event" and "a new start" are; that this is the second of the three speeches in Eliot's note.

**Cut**
Everything after the first sentence.

**Proposed body**
Title — The second daughter

The second of the three Thames-daughters of Eliot's note. Moorgate is a street and district on the north edge of the City, named from a gate in the old wall. "The event" is the seduction; "a new start" is his phrase, reported by her without comment. The poem gives nothing more about her.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Rewrite.

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- "The most terrible short speech in the poem, because it asks for nothing." — superlative nobody could check; fragment.
- "He wept; she did not." — balanced antithesis as a fragment.
- "It is the typist's 'Well now that's done' without even the relief." — bow-tying last sentence, "X without even Y".
- Three of the four lines quoted back at me.

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- Everything after the first sentence is the annotator's response to the lines, not help with them.
- "where the clerks work": the one fact, and it could be a shade fuller (the City of London, the business district).

**Keep**
- Moorgate, in the City, among the offices and the clerks.
- The likeness to the typist's line 252, as one plain cross-reference ("cf. line 252"), if the scholar thinks it is one; I did not need it.

**Proposed body**
Moorgate is a street and Underground station in the City of London, the business district, among the offices where the clerks worked. The second Thames-daughter speaks from there.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** Moorgate placed (scholar's gate in the wall, reader's business district); "the event" and "a new start" glossed; everything else, which was the annotator's response to the lines, cut.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Accept

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** accepted by both; unchanged from round 2.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: The second daughter
body: The second of the three Thames-daughters of Eliot's note speaks from Moorgate, a street and district on the north edge of the City of London, the business district, named from a gate in the old wall. "The event" is the seduction; "a new start" is his phrase, reported without comment.

### margate (lines 300–305)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: Where the poem was written
body: The third daughter speaks from Margate, the Kentish seaside resort at the mouth of the Thames estuary. Eliot went there in October 1921, on three months' sick leave from the bank, and stayed until mid-November at the Albemarle Hotel in Cliftonville; he sat in a public shelter on the front, the Nayland Rock shelter, which still stands, and wrote, he told Sydney Schiff on 4 November, "a rough draft of part of part III." "I can connect / Nothing with nothing" is the daughter's confession and the poet's. The "broken fingernails of dirty hands" and "My people humble people who expect / Nothing" are what he saw on the beach at the end of the season. The song runs down to "la la" and stops.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite

**Errors**
- Title "Where the poem was written". A tour-guide heading of the kind the owner objected to, and untrue as stated: Eliot drafted "part of part III" at Margate; the rest of the poem was written in London before and Lausanne after. Retitle.
- "Eliot went there in October 1921, on three months' sick leave from the bank, and stayed until mid-November". Right in outline. Lloyds gave him three months' leave from 12 October (Vivien, 13 October: he has "had to stop all work and go away for three months"); he was writing from the Albemarle Hotel, Cliftonville, by 26 October; on 17 November he wrote that he was "in transit between Margate ... and Lausanne"; Historic England says three weeks. I recall the dates as 22 October to 12 November (Letters vol. 1; Ricks and McCue's chronology), unverified. Sources: https://kentliterature.com/t-s-eliot-breaking-down-in-margate/; https://britishlistedbuildings.co.uk/101393490-nayland-rock-promenade-shelter-thanet-westbrook-ward.
- "he sat in a public shelter on the front, the Nayland Rock shelter, which still stands". The letter says "a shelter on the front"; the Nayland Rock shelter was listed Grade II on 9 October 2009 "as the likely location". Likely, not certain. Note that the listing text misquotes the letter as "a rough draft of part III", dropping "part of"; do not copy it.
- "wrote, he told Sydney Schiff on 4 November, "a rough draft of part of part III."" Right. What that part was: the pencil leaves of "The river sweats" (26 lines, lines 266–291), "Highbury bore me" (19 lines, cut to 292–299) and "On Margate Sands" (12 lines, 300–311), so lines 266–311. Rainey's account (<i>Revisiting The Waste Land</i>), as quoted in a web search; the leaves are undated, so "usually identified as".
- "The "broken fingernails of dirty hands" and "My people humble people who expect / Nothing" are what he saw on the beach at the end of the season." Unsupported, and the draft points the other way: the leaf's first version had the girl say she still felt "the pressure of dirty hand", which is her seducer's, and "my people" were "plain people". Source: the facsimile pp. 52–53 as quoted on the web (search snippet from explorationofthewasteland.weebly.com) and my memory of it; unverified against the facsimile itself.

**Readings passed off as fact**
- ""I can connect / Nothing with nothing" is the daughter's confession and the poet's." Lyndall Gordon's line of reading, offered as fact.
- The beach sentence above.
- "The song runs down to "la la" and stops." Decoration; "la la" is the refrain reduced, which can be said plainly if at all.

**Missing**
What part of Part III the Margate draft was; the hedge on the shelter; the draft's "dirty hand".

**Cut**
The last three sentences as worded.
