**Proposed body**
Title — Margate Sands

Margate, on the north Kent coast where the Thames estuary opens to the sea, is where Eliot went in the autumn of 1921, on three months' leave from Lloyds Bank after a breakdown. He and Vivien were at the Albemarle Hotel, Cliftonville, by late October, and he stayed about three weeks. On 4 November he told Sydney Schiff he had done "a rough draft of part of part III," written "while sitting in a shelter on the front"; the Nayland Rock shelter on the promenade, listed in 2009 as the likely one, still stands. The part is usually identified as the pencil drafts of lines 266–311, the Thames-daughters' song through to "burning." The third daughter's speech was changed in draft: in its first form she still felt "the pressure of dirty hand," and "my people" were "plain people." Lyndall Gordon and others have read "I can connect / Nothing with nothing" as Eliot's own confession as well as hers.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Rewrite.

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- Title "Where the poem was written" — a tour-guide heading of the "Where the poem is standing" kind; and part of Part III was drafted there, not the poem.
- "he sat in a public shelter on the front, the Nayland Rock shelter, which still stands, and wrote, he told Sydney Schiff on 4 November, 'a rough draft of part of part III.'" — clauses stacked on semicolons and commas.
- "is the daughter's confession and the poet's" — a reading dressed as fact; balanced pairing.
- "are what he saw on the beach at the end of the season" — a reading dressed as fact; nobody knows what he saw.
- "The song runs down to 'la la' and stops." — metaphor describing the poem; bow-tying last sentence.

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- "the Nayland Rock shelter": is the shelter Eliot's own detail (I believe the same letter says he wrote sitting in a shelter on the front), and is Nayland Rock a later local identification? The note runs them together as one fact, and I cannot tell how much to believe.
- "on three months' sick leave" against "stayed until mid-November": three months of leave, three weeks at Margate; say which is which.
- "at the Albemarle Hotel in Cliftonville": did not help me with the line.
- "My people humble people who expect / Nothing": I wanted to know who "my people" are and got what Eliot saw instead.

**Keep**
- Margate, Kent, at the mouth of the Thames estuary.
- Eliot there in October–November 1921 on sick leave from the bank; the letter to Sydney Schiff, 4 November 1921, "a rough draft of part of part III".
- The shelter on the front, if it is Eliot's own detail; that it still stands, in a clause.
- "I can connect / Nothing with nothing" as commonly read for the poet as well, offered as a reading.

**Proposed body**
Plainer title — Margate, 1921
Margate is a seaside resort in Kent at the mouth of the Thames estuary. Eliot spent three weeks there in October and November 1921, on sick leave from the bank, and on 4 November wrote to Sydney Schiff that he had done "a rough draft of part of part III," working, he said, in a shelter on the front; the Nayland Rock shelter, which still stands, is the one identified. "I can connect / Nothing with nothing" is often read as the poet's line as well as the third Thames-daughter's.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** title changed ("Where the poem was written" is the kind of heading the owner objected to, and untrue as stated); dates and the shelter as the scholar found them (the letter says "a shelter on the front"; Nayland Rock is the listed identification); the Schiff letter kept; which lines the Margate draft was, as usually identified; "Nothing with nothing" as Lyndall Gordon's reading; the beach sentence and "runs down to 'la la'" cut. The scholar's memory of the draft's "dirty hand" is left out as unverified.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept. The shelter sentence is in the same letter: the 4 November 1921 letter to Schiff runs "I have done a rough draft of part of part III, but I do not know whether it will do, and must wait for Vivien's opinion as to whether it is printable. I have done this while sitting in a shelter on the front – as I am out all day except when taking rest" (Letters I, as quoted by Kent Maps Online and the Historic England listing; the listing's own paraphrase drops "part of"). The "dirty hand" draft is now verified at second hand: two web accounts citing the facsimile, p. 53, give the first version as "I was to be grateful. On Margate sands / There were many others. I can connect / Nothing with nothing. He had / I still feel the pressure of dirty hand", revised on the leaf to "On Margate sands. / I can connect / Nothing wth nothing. / The broken fingernails of dirty hands. / My people are plain people, who expect / Nothing". For the drafts record rather than this gloss, cited as "facsimile p. 53, as reported"; neither account explains "plain" becoming "humble", and Rainey's statement that the twelve-line Margate leaf was unrevised sits oddly beside it, so the facsimile itself should settle the page before it is printed.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: the first two sentences.

"Margate, on the north Kent coast where the Thames estuary opens to the sea, is where Eliot went in the autumn of 1921, on three months' leave from Lloyds Bank after a breakdown; he stayed about three weeks, from late October to mid-November, at the Albemarle Hotel, Cliftonville." — "Margate ... is where Eliot went" is a frame, not a sentence a person would say; the hotel does not help with the line and should go. Put instead: "Margate is a resort on the north Kent coast, where the Thames estuary opens to the sea. Eliot went there in the autumn of 1921, on three months' leave from Lloyds Bank after a breakdown, and stayed about three weeks, from late October to mid-November."

"On 4 November he told Sydney Schiff he had done "a rough draft of part of part III", written sitting in a shelter on the front; the Nayland Rock shelter on the promenade, listed in 2009 as the likely one, still stands." — "listed" means nothing to a reader outside Britain, and the two halves are two facts. Put instead: "On 4 November he told Sydney Schiff he had done "a rough draft of part of part III", written sitting in a shelter on the front. That part is usually identified as the pencil drafts of lines 266–311, from "The river sweats" to "burning". The Nayland Rock shelter on the promenade, which still stands, was given listed-building protection in 2009 as the likely place." (this absorbs the third sentence; the Gordon sentence follows unchanged).

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's first two sentences (the hotel cut) and the shelter sentence taken. The Schiff letter's words, "a rough draft of part of part III", kept on the batch B scholar's citation (the Letters, as quoted by the Historic England listing); the batch C scholar doubts the wording, which is recorded as unsettled.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept. On the wording the batch C scholar doubts: "a rough draft of part of part III" is how the Letters give it, as quoted by Kent Maps Online, and how Valerie Eliot's introduction to the 1971 facsimile quotes the same letter, which goes on "But I have written only some fifty lines" (from memory of the facsimile introduction, unverified; the Historic England listing's "part III" is a paraphrase that drops "part of"). The fifty lines fit the identification with 266–311.

**Reader, round 3:** Accept. The sentence is mine; the wording of the letter is the scholars' citation, and the batch C doubt is rightly recorded as unsettled rather than hedged in the gloss.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Margate Sands
body: Margate is a resort on the north Kent coast, where the Thames estuary opens to the sea. Eliot went there in the autumn of 1921, on three months' leave from Lloyds Bank after a breakdown, and stayed about three weeks, from late October to mid-November. On 4 November he told Sydney Schiff he had done "a rough draft of part of part III", written sitting in a shelter on the front. That part is usually identified as the pencil drafts of lines 266–311, from "The river sweats" to "burning". The Nayland Rock shelter on the promenade, which still stands, was given listed-building protection in 2009 as the likely place. Lyndall Gordon and others have read "I can connect / Nothing with nothing" as Eliot's own confession as well as the third daughter's.

### carthage (lines 307–311)

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: Augustine and the Buddha, burning
quote: To Carthage I came, where there sang all around me in my ears a cauldron of unholy loves.
body: The opening of Book III of Augustine's <i>Confessions</i> (c. 400), given in Eliot's note in his own wording; E. B. Pusey's translation (1838), which is behind it, reads "To Carthage I came, where there sang all around me in my ears a cauldron of unholy loves." The Latin puns: Carthago, sartago, a frying pan. Augustine arrived in the city as a student and threw himself into love affairs and the theatre. Then the Buddha's Fire Sermon, which gives the section its title: "All things, O priests, are on fire . . . The eye, O priests, is on fire; forms are on fire . . . with the fire of passion, say I, with the fire of hatred, with the fire of infatuation." Eliot read it at Harvard in Henry Clarke Warren's <i>Buddhism in Translations</i> (1896). Then Augustine again, Book X: "I . . . entangle my steps with these outward beauties; but Thou pluckest me out, O Lord, Thou pluckest me out."

Eliot's note says that putting "these two representatives of eastern and western asceticism" together "as the culmination of this part of the poem, is not an accident." The lines fall apart as you read them: a sentence, a word four times, a prayer, a prayer with its end torn off, one word alone. The fire that burns here is lust. The fire at line 428 refines.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction

**Errors**
- Pusey III.1 and X.34: the quotations are accurate. Source: https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/3296/pg3296.txt ("To Carthage I came, where there sang all around me in my ears a cauldron of unholy loves"; "And I, though I speak and see this, entangle my steps with these outward beauties; but Thou pluckest me out, O Lord, Thou pluckest me out; because Thy loving-kindness is before my eyes"). Eliot's note does reword the first.
- "The Latin puns: Carthago, sartago, a frying pan." Right: "Veni Carthaginem, et circumstrepebat me undique sartago flagitiosorum amorum" (https://www.thelatinlibrary.com/augustine/conf3.shtml).
- "Augustine arrived in the city as a student and threw himself into love affairs and the theatre." Right: Book III.1–2, at about seventeen, to study rhetoric; "stage-plays also carried me away" (Pusey III.2).
- The Warren quotation is accurate. Source: https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Page:Buddhism_in_Translations_(1896).djvu/382. Warren's setting: the Buddha at Gayā Head with a thousand priests "who had all of them aforetime been monks with matted hair".
- "Eliot read it at Harvard in Henry Clarke Warren's Buddhism in Translations (1896)." Inference stated as fact. Known: Eliot studied Sanskrit and Pali under Charles Lanman at Harvard, 1911–13 (his own statement in <i>After Strange Gods</i>, 1934); Lanman edited the Harvard Oriental Series, in which Warren's book is vol. 3; Eliot's 1922 note sends readers to Warren and calls him "one of the great pioneers of Buddhist studies in the occident". Reported by Cleo McNelly Kearns (<i>T. S. Eliot and Indic Traditions</i>, 1987), from Eliot's surviving course notes: Lanman's Pali class read the Fire Sermon in Andersen's <i>Pali Reader</i> with Warren's translation recommended. From memory, unverified; the claim recurs in recent scholarship citing Kearns. Say "it is generally assumed he met the sermon there".
- "The fire at line 428 refines." Line 428 in the site's count is "Poi s'ascose nel foco che gli affina" (Eliot's note "427. V. Purgatorio, xxvi. 148"), Arnaut Daniel hiding in the refining fire. The connection is a critics' reading (Southam, Grover Smith, from memory) and can be given as one.

**Readings passed off as fact**
- "The lines fall apart as you read them: a sentence, a word four times, a prayer, a prayer with its end torn off, one word alone." The owner's own example.
- "The fire that burns here is lust. The fire at line 428 refines." The owner's own example.
- Title "Augustine and the Buddha, burning": the ", burning" is a flourish.

**Missing**
Eliot's note's comparison of the Fire Sermon to the Sermon on the Mount (optional); what is known versus assumed about Harvard.

**Cut**
The last three sentences.

**Proposed body**
Title — Augustine and the Buddha

Augustine, <i>Confessions</i> III.1, in Pusey's translation (1838): "To Carthage I came, where there sang all around me in my ears a cauldron of unholy loves." Eliot's note gives the sentence in his own rewording. The Latin has a pun, Carthago and sartago, a frying pan. Augustine had come to Carthage at about seventeen to study rhetoric, and Book III begins with his love affairs and the theatre. "Burning" is the Buddha's Fire Sermon, which gives the section its title: "All things, O priests, are on fire . . . The eye, O priests, is on fire; forms are on fire . . . with the fire of passion, say I, with the fire of hatred, with the fire of infatuation." Eliot's note sends readers to Henry Clarke Warren's <i>Buddhism in Translations</i> (1896), in the Harvard Oriental Series edited by Charles Lanman, under whom Eliot studied Sanskrit and Pali at Harvard in 1911–13; it is generally assumed he met the sermon there. Then Augustine again, Book X: "I . . . entangle my steps with these outward beauties; but Thou pluckest me out, O Lord, Thou pluckest me out." Eliot's note says that placing "these two representatives of eastern and western asceticism" together "as the culmination of this part of the poem, is not an accident." Critics have connected this burning with the refining fire of <i>Purgatorio</i> XXVI, quoted at line 428.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: Rewrite.

**Reads as a model wrote it**
- "The Latin puns: Carthago, sartago, a frying pan." — fragment on a colon.
- "Then the Buddha's Fire Sermon ..." / "Then Augustine again" — walk-through framing.
- "The lines fall apart as you read them: a sentence, a word four times, a prayer, a prayer with its end torn off, one word alone." — the owner's own example: counting flourish, second person, a figure for the poem.
- "The fire that burns here is lust. The fire at line 428 refines." — the owner's own example: the "X is Y. Z is W." epigram to close.
- Eliot's note quoted back ("these two representatives ... is not an accident") when it is printed beside the line.
- Title "Augustine and the Buddha, burning" — the tacked-on ", burning".

**Unclear or unhelpful**
- "given in Eliot's note in his own wording; E. B. Pusey's translation (1838), which is behind it, reads ...": the point (the poem's line and the note's are Eliot's wording, not Pusey's) took me two readings, and the quote field already gives Pusey.
- "threw himself into love affairs and the theatre": the annotator's phrase for what Augustine says himself in the pages that follow.
- "Eliot read it at Harvard": when, and as what? One clause (as a student, if the scholar confirms the years).
- "The fire at line 428 refines": I wanted the cross-reference plainly, what line 428 is and whose fire.

**Keep**
- Confessions, Book III, opening; that the poem's wording and the note's are Eliot's own; Pusey in the quote.
- The Carthago/sartago pun, which is Augustine's wit and explains "cauldron".
- The Fire Sermon as the source of "burning" and of the title; the Warren quotation; Warren (1896).
- Book X for "Thou pluckest me out", with the quotation.
- The cross-reference to line 428, as one plain sentence.

**Proposed body**
Plainer title — Augustine and the Buddha
The opening of Book III of Augustine's <i>Confessions</i> (c. 400). The line, like the version in Eliot's note, is Eliot's own wording; Pusey's translation (1838) is given above. Augustine puns on Carthago and sartago, a frying pan, which is Pusey's "cauldron." He had come to Carthage as a student, and Book III goes on to his love affairs and his passion for the theatre. "Burning" is from the Buddha's Fire Sermon, which gives this part of the poem its title, in Henry Clarke Warren's <i>Buddhism in Translations</i> (1896), which Eliot read as a student at Harvard: "All things, O priests, are on fire . . . The eye, O priests, is on fire; forms are on fire . . . with the fire of passion, say I, with the fire of hatred, with the fire of infatuation." "Thou pluckest me out" is Augustine again, Book X: "I . . . entangle my steps with these outward beauties; but Thou pluckest me out, O Lord, Thou pluckest me out." At line 428 the fire is Dante's, the fire of purgatory that refines.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** Pusey's sentence is in the quote field, so the body does not repeat it; the pun kept as Augustine's; the Harvard claim reduced to what is known (Lanman, 1911–13) and what is assumed; the Warren quotation kept, since a reader wants the words; Book X kept; the Dante fire at 428 given as a critics' connection; the counting sentence and the "lust / refines" close cut; title plainer.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept. The Harvard sentence says what is known and hedges the rest; Kearns's report from Eliot's course notes stays unverified, so "generally assumed" is right. One name, if wanted: Eliot's note calls the series the Harvard Oriental Series, which "the Harvard series" could give in full.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: three sentences.

"The Latin has a pun, Carthago and sartago, a frying pan." — the reader is not told this is why Pusey has "cauldron". Put instead: "The Latin has a pun, Carthago and sartago, a frying pan, which is Pusey's "cauldron"."

"Eliot's note sends readers to Henry Clarke Warren's <i>Buddhism in Translations</i> (1896), in the Harvard series edited by Charles Lanman, under whom Eliot studied Sanskrit and Pali in 1911–13; it is generally assumed he met the sermon there." — a chain of five links, and "there" could be the book, the series, the class or Harvard. Lanman and the series do not help with "burning"; I ask for them to go. Put instead: "Eliot's note sends readers to Henry Clarke Warren's <i>Buddhism in Translations</i> (1896). Eliot studied Sanskrit and Pali at Harvard in 1911–13, and it is generally assumed that he met the sermon there, in Warren's version." If the scholar needs Lanman as the ground for the assumption, one clause: "under Charles Lanman, who edited the series Warren's book is in".

"Eliot's note says that placing "these two representatives of eastern and western asceticism" together "as the culmination of this part of the poem, is not an accident."" — his note is printed beside line 309, inside this gloss's range; quoting it whole is repetition. Put instead: "Eliot's note at line 309 says the pairing of "eastern and western asceticism" here "is not an accident"."

Also "which gives the section its title" — put "which gives Part III its title". The rest accepted.

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's three rewordings taken; the series named as Eliot's note names it (Harvard Oriental Series), which the scholar offered; Eliot's note quoted in the reader's shorter form, with the note's own phrase for the two. Round 3: "in Warren's version" cut (the scholar: the one account of the Harvard course has the class reading the sermon in Pali, so it says more than is known).

**Scholar, round 3:** One correction, introduced by rewording. "it is generally assumed that he met the sermon there, in Warren's version": the one account of the Harvard course (Kearns, from Eliot's course notes) has the class reading the sermon in Pali in Andersen's reader with Warren's translation recommended, so "in Warren's version" says more than is known. Replace with "it is generally assumed that he met the sermon there." Eliot's note at 309 is quoted accurately in its shortened form; Pusey's "cauldron" for sartago is right.

**Reader, round 3:** Accept both. "(Harvard Oriental Series, 1896)" is the note's own name for the series, in a parenthesis, and costs nothing. "Eliot's note at line 309 says that putting "these two representatives of eastern and western asceticism" together here "is not an accident"." keeps Eliot's phrase and is shorter than the note.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Augustine and the Buddha
quote: To Carthage I came, where there sang all around me in my ears a cauldron of unholy loves.
body: The opening of Book III of Augustine's <i>Confessions</i> (c. 400), in Pusey's translation (1838), given above; the line, like Eliot's note, rewords it. The Latin has a pun, Carthago and sartago, a frying pan, which is Pusey's "cauldron". Augustine had come to Carthage at about seventeen to study rhetoric, and Book III begins with his love affairs and the theatre. "Burning" is from the Buddha's Fire Sermon, which gives Part III its title: "All things, O priests, are on fire . . . The eye, O priests, is on fire; forms are on fire . . . with the fire of passion, say I, with the fire of hatred, with the fire of infatuation." Eliot's note sends readers to Henry Clarke Warren's <i>Buddhism in Translations</i> (Harvard Oriental Series, 1896). Eliot studied Sanskrit and Pali at Harvard in 1911–13, and it is generally assumed that he met the sermon there. "O Lord Thou pluckest me out" is Augustine again, Book X: "I . . . entangle my steps with these outward beauties; but Thou pluckest me out, O Lord, Thou pluckest me out." Eliot's note at line 309 says that putting "these two representatives of eastern and western asceticism" together here "is not an accident". Critics have connected this burning with the refining fire of <i>Purgatorio</i> XXVI, quoted at line 428.

## The eleven ways in (batch C)

### Way: Follow the river

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: Follow the river
body: From the river's tent by Richmond to Greenwich Reach past the Isle of Dogs, by way of a canal behind a gasworks, the Cannon Street Hotel, the Strand, Lower Thames Street and St Magnus Martyr. The map has the real course of the Thames; the section moves down it with the tide.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.
Errors: "the river's tent by Richmond": lines 173–184 name no place; Richmond enters only at 293–294, in the first Thames-daughter's speech. "the section moves down it with the tide": the named places do not run downstream in order; after Greenwich reach and the Isle of Dogs (275–276) the daughters go back upstream to Richmond and Kew (293–294), then to Moorgate (296) and Margate (300) at the mouth of the estuary. The poem's word is "gashouse" (190), not "gasworks". The rest is right (Cannon Street Hotel 213, the Strand 258, Lower Thames Street 260, Magnus Martyr 264); checked against the site's text.
Readings passed off as fact: a single journey downriver.
Missing: nothing.
Cut: "moves down it with the tide".
Proposed text:
The places, in the order the section gives them: an unnamed stretch of the river (173–184), a canal behind a gashouse (189–190), the Cannon Street Hotel (213), the Strand, Queen Victoria Street and Lower Thames Street with St Magnus Martyr (258–265), Greenwich reach and the Isle of Dogs (275–276), and in the daughters' speeches Highbury, Richmond and Kew, Moorgate and Margate (293–300). The map has the real course of the Thames.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: "the section moves down it with the tide" — a metaphor for how the poem moves, standing in for a fact; and it is not so: after Greenwich (275) the first daughter goes back up to Richmond and Kew (293).
Unclear or unhelpful: "the river's tent by Richmond" — nothing at line 173 says Richmond; I cannot tell where that placing comes from. "gasworks" — the poem says "gashouse".
Keep: the string of named places; "The map has the real course of the Thames."
Proposed text: Follow the section's places along the Thames: a canal behind the gashouse, the Cannon Street Hotel, the Strand, Lower Thames Street and St Magnus Martyr, Greenwich Reach and the Isle of Dogs downstream, Richmond and Kew up the river. The map has the real course of the river.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** no single journey downriver (both); "gashouse" is the poem's word; the places given in the section's order with their lines.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: five bracketed line ranges in one sentence ("(189–190)", "(213)", "(258–265)", "(275–276)", "(292–300)") make it an index entry, not something a person says, and the Cities pathway already gives each place with its line. Proposed text: The section names, in order, a canal behind a gashouse, the Cannon Street Hotel, the Strand, Queen Victoria Street and Lower Thames Street with St Magnus Martyr, Greenwich Reach and the Isle of Dogs, and in the daughters' speeches Highbury, Richmond and Kew, Moorgate and Margate. The map has the real course of the Thames, with each of these on it.

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's text, without the bracketed line numbers.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Follow the river
body: The section names, in order, a canal behind a gashouse, the Cannon Street Hotel, the Strand, Queen Victoria Street and Lower Thames Street with St Magnus Martyr, Greenwich Reach and the Isle of Dogs, and in the daughters' speeches Highbury, Richmond and Kew, Moorgate and Margate. The map has the real course of the Thames, with each of these on it.

### Way: Spenser first, then the litter (now "Spenser's refrain")

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: Spenser first, then the litter
body: Read the first stanza of the Prothalamion with its refrain, then lines 173–184. Eliot keeps the refrain and takes away the wedding, the nymphs and the flowers, and lists instead what is not in the river.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs hedging (wording only).
Errors: none of fact. Line 176 is Spenser's refrain exactly ("Sweete Themmes runne softly, till I end my Song", which closes each of the ten stanzas); 183 repeats it and 184 varies it. "takes away the nymphs" is not quite right: Eliot keeps them and says twice that they "are departed" (175, 179). "lists what is not in the river" is right (177–179).
Readings passed off as fact: none.
Cut: the title's joke ("the litter"); "with its refrain".
Proposed title: Spenser's refrain
Proposed text:
Spenser's <i>Prothalamion</i> (1596) is a poem for a double wedding on the Thames, and its refrain, "Sweete Themmes runne softly, till I end my Song", closes each stanza. Eliot takes it at 176 and 183 and varies it at 184. Spenser's nymphs gather flowers by the river; Eliot's "are departed", and his river "bears no" bottles, papers or cigarette ends, a list of what is not there.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: keep. It tells me exactly what to read and what to look for, and what it says is on the page (177–179 list what is not in the river).

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** the reader would keep this; the scholar corrects "takes away the nymphs" (Eliot keeps them and says they are departed) and the title's joke. Merged, keeping the reader's "read this, then that".

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Accept

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** accepted by both; unchanged.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Spenser's refrain
body: Spenser's <i>Prothalamion</i> (1596) is a poem for a double wedding on the Thames, and its refrain, "Sweete Themmes runne softly, till I end my Song", closes each stanza. Read its first stanza, then lines 173–184. Eliot takes the refrain at 176 and 183 and varies it at 184; Spenser's nymphs gather flowers by the river, Eliot's "are departed", and his river "bears no" bottles, papers or cigarette ends.

### Way: See who speaks

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: See who speaks
body: Voices marks the fisher on the bank, a soldiers' song, Verlaine, a nightingale, Mr. Eugenides, Tiresias, the typist, the walker, three Thames-daughters, Augustine and the Buddha. It is the section with the most voices and the most quotation marks, and the one Eliot said Tiresias holds together.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.
Errors: "the section with the most quotation marks" is false. In the 1922 text (Gutenberg #1321, which like the 1922 edition opens a quotation mark on each quoted line) Part III has five (252, 257, 292, 296, 300), Part I ten, Part II fourteen. "the most voices" cannot be checked and depends on who counts. "the one Eliot said Tiresias holds together": the note on 218 says Tiresias is "the most important personage in the poem, uniting all the rest" and that what he sees "is the substance of the poem"; it is a claim about the poem, not about this section. In the list, "the fisher" (189) and "the walker" (259) are the annotator's names for an unnamed "I"; Verlaine (202) is quoted and Mr. Eugenides (212–214) is reported, not heard.
Readings passed off as fact: the two superlatives; the Tiresias claim as put.
Missing: nothing.
Cut: the second sentence.
Proposed text:
The Voices lens marks each speaker and each quoted voice: the "I" who fishes by the canal, the Mrs. Porter song, Verlaine's line, the nightingale, Mr. Eugenides (reported, not quoted), Tiresias, the typist, the "I" of Lower Thames Street, the three Thames-daughters, Augustine and the Buddha. Tiresias speaks only in this section; Eliot's note calls him "the most important personage in the poem, uniting all the rest".

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: "the section with the most voices and the most quotation marks" — a superlative and a count nobody will check.
Unclear or unhelpful: "the walker" — no one in the section is called that; I take it to mean the speaker of 259–265, but I am guessing. "the one Eliot said Tiresias holds together" — his note says Tiresias unites "all the rest" in the poem, not in this section. "a soldiers' song" — the note says only that the ballad was reported from Sydney; I cannot tell where "soldiers" comes from.
Keep: the roll-call, which is what the lens is for.
Proposed text: The Voices lens marks who is speaking: the fisher on the bank, the Mrs Porter ballad, a line of Verlaine, the nightingale, Mr Eugenides, Tiresias, the typist, the speaker beside the bar in Lower Thames Street, the three Thames-daughters, Augustine and the Buddha. Eliot's note at line 218 says Tiresias unites all the rest.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** the superlatives cut (the scholar counted: Part III has fewer quotation marks than I or II); the Tiresias claim given as what the note says; the unnamed speakers named as the "I" of each place.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Accept

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** accepted by both; unchanged.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: See who speaks
body: The Voices lens marks each speaker and each quoted voice: the "I" who fishes by the canal, the Mrs. Porter song, Verlaine's line, the nightingale, Mr. Eugenides (reported, not quoted), Tiresias, the typist, the "I" of Lower Thames Street, the three Thames-daughters, Augustine and the Buddha. Tiresias speaks only in this section; Eliot's note calls him "the most important personage in the poem, uniting all the rest".

### Way: The Fire Sermon itself

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: The Fire Sermon itself
body: Read the Buddha's sermon, in the translation Eliot used at Harvard, before the section, not after. Then everything in it, the river, the typist, the barges, the cities, is seen "on fire," and the five "burning"s at the end are the sermon's own refrain.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.
Errors: "the translation Eliot used at Harvard" is an inference, not a documented fact. Eliot's note names "the late Henry Clarke Warren's Buddhism in Translation" (the title is <i>Buddhism in Translations</i>, Harvard Oriental Series III, 1896). Eliot studied Sanskrit under Charles Lanman at Harvard from 1911 and Pali in 1912–13; Lanman edited the series and had been Warren's collaborator, so the book is the obvious text, but I know no document that says Eliot used it there (Crawford, <i>Young Eliot</i>; Kearns, <i>T. S. Eliot and Indic Traditions</i>; from memory, unverified). "the five 'burning's ... are the sermon's own refrain": five is right (four at 308, one at 311; counted), but Warren's repeated phrase is "is on fire" / "are on fire", never "burning" (Warren §73, pp. 351–52, https://archive.org/details/in.ernet.dli.2015.217548; checked). Eliot's note says only that "these words are taken" from the sermon. "everything in it ... is seen 'on fire'" is a reading.
Readings passed off as fact: the last sentence.
Missing: what the sermon says.
Cut: "before the section, not after".
Proposed text:
Eliot's note sends the reader to the Buddha's Fire Sermon in Henry Clarke Warren's <i>Buddhism in Translations</i> (1896) and calls it as important as the Sermon on the Mount. In Warren the Buddha says of the eye, the ear, the tongue, the body and the mind that each "is on fire", with the fire of passion, hatred and infatuation. Eliot had studied Sanskrit and Pali at Harvard under Charles Lanman, who edited the series the book appeared in, and is generally assumed to have read it there. The section's title is the sermon's; "burning", five times at 308–311, is the poem's word for the sermon's "on fire".

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: "before the section, not after" — an instruction for its own sake. "everything in it, the river, the typist, the barges, the cities, is seen 'on fire,'" — the rhythmic list of four, handing me a reading as if it followed by itself. "the five 'burning's at the end" — counting to sound clever.
Unclear or unhelpful: "the translation Eliot used at Harvard" — the note names Warren's book; the Harvard part I cannot check and do not need. "the sermon's own refrain" — I cannot tell whether the sermon says "burning" or "on fire"; the scholar should say which.
Keep: that the title and the last lines come from the sermon, that it is short, and that the note names it.
Proposed text: Eliot's note at line 308 names the translation of the Buddha's Fire Sermon he used, in Warren's <i>Buddhism in Translations</i>. It is short. In it the Buddha tells his monks that everything the senses meet is on fire. The section takes its title from it, and the word "burning" at lines 308 and 311.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** Harvard dropped here (it is in the carthage gloss, as an assumption); what the sermon says given; "burning" as the poem's word for Warren's "on fire" (scholar); "before the section, not after" and the list of four cut; the reader's "It is short" kept.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Accept

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** accepted by both; unchanged.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: The Fire Sermon itself
body: Eliot's note sends the reader to the Buddha's Fire Sermon in Henry Clarke Warren's <i>Buddhism in Translations</i> (1896) and calls it as important as the Sermon on the Mount. It is short. In it the Buddha says of the eye, the ear, the tongue, the body and the mind that each "is on fire", with the fire of passion, of hatred and of infatuation. The section's title is the sermon's, and "burning", five times at 308–311, is the poem's word for the sermon's "on fire".

### Way: Pound's pencil on the typist

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: Pound's pencil on the typist
body: The typist scene was longer, and Pound cut it hard, writing "verse not interesting enough as verse to warrant so much of it" in the margin. The drafts view shows his marks and the seventy lines of Popean couplets about Fresca that once opened the section.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs hedging.
Errors: none of fact as far as I know. Pound's "verse not interesting enough as verse to warrant so much of it" is on the typist typescript (facsimile; quoted by Ricks and McCue; from memory). "seventy lines" is the usual figure; "about seventy" is safer. "cut it hard": "heavily". Rights: the Pound sentence is eleven words; keep the core phrase and describe the rest. "The drafts view shows his marks": if the page reproduces the facsimile it breaks the site's own rule; if it describes them, say "describes".
Readings passed off as fact: none.
Missing: nothing.
Cut: nothing else.
Proposed text:
In the typescript the typist scene was longer, in regular quatrains, and Pound cut it heavily, writing in the margin that the verse was "not interesting enough as verse" for so much of it. The drafts view describes his marks, and the seventy or so lines of couplets in Pope's manner, about a woman called Fresca, that opened the section until he struck them out.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: nothing much; "cut it hard" is a person's phrase.
Unclear or unhelpful: "Popean couplets" — say couplets in imitation of Pope. "The drafts view shows his marks and the seventy lines" — does it? The drafts file says the facsimile can only be described and a phrase quoted, so "shows" promises what I will not get. Fresca is named without a word on who she is.
Keep: the quoted marginal comment; that the scene was longer; the pointer to the drafts.
Proposed text: The typist scene was longer in typescript, and Pound cut it, writing "verse not interesting enough as verse to warrant so much of it" in the margin. The drafts page describes his marks, and the seventy lines of couplets in imitation of Pope, about a fashionable woman called Fresca, that once opened the section.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** "describes", not "shows" (both); Fresca named as a woman; the Pound phrase shortened to the rights rule.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Accept

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** accepted by both; unchanged.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Pound's pencil on the typist
body: In the typescript the typist scene was longer, in regular quatrains, and Pound cut it heavily, writing in the margin that the verse was "not interesting enough as verse" for so much of it. The drafts page describes his marks, and the seventy or so lines of couplets in Pope's manner, about a fashionable woman called Fresca, that opened the section until he struck them out.

### Way: The Rhine-daughters, then the Thames-daughters

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: The Rhine-daughters, then the Thames-daughters
body: Wagner's three Rhine-daughters open the last act of Götterdämmerung singing "Weialala leia." Listen to the scene, then read lines 266–306 aloud. The short lines are a swimming rhythm, and the three modern daughters take their turns like the three in the opera.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction (cut the metaphor).
Errors: Act III of <i>Götterdämmerung</i> does open with the three Rhine-daughters ("Dritter Aufzug ... Die drei Rheintöchter"), and their refrain is "Weialala leia, wallala leialala" (libretto, projekt-gutenberg.org, Götterdämmerung, chapter 11; checked); Eliot's note gives III, i. "The short lines are a swimming rhythm" is the annotator's metaphor. "take their turns like the three in the opera" is a fair comparison but the annotator's; Eliot's note says only that his three "speak in turn".
Readings passed off as fact: the rhythm sentence.
Missing: where Eliot takes the refrain.
Cut: "Listen ... then read ... aloud"; "swimming rhythm".
Proposed text:
Eliot's note gives <i>Götterdämmerung</i>, Act III scene 1, which opens with the three Rhine-daughters swimming and singing for their stolen gold; "Weialala leia, wallala leialala" is their refrain, and the Thames-daughters take it at 277–278 and 290–291. The Listen lens has the scene [editor: confirm the site has a recording]. Eliot's note says the three daughters "speak in turn" from 292 to 306.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: "The short lines are a swimming rhythm" — a metaphor describing the verse instead of saying anything about it.
Unclear or unhelpful: nothing.
Keep: the opera scene, the refrain, listen then read (that is what a Listen way is for), the turns.
Proposed text: The three Rhine-daughters who open the last act of Wagner's <i>Götterdämmerung</i> sing the "Weialala leia" of lines 277 and 290. Listen to the scene, then read lines 266–306. Eliot's note says his three Thames-daughters speak in turn from line 292, as Wagner's three do.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** the site's listening room has the scene (the Solti recording, "Act III Scene 1"), so "listen, then read" stays, as the reader wanted for a Listen way; "swimming rhythm" cut; where Eliot takes the refrain added (scholar).

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "singing for their stolen gold" can be read as singing to get it. Proposed: "swimming and singing of their stolen gold". The rest as written.

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's "singing of".

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: The Rhine-daughters, then the Thames-daughters
body: Act III of Wagner's <i>Götterdämmerung</i> opens with the three Rhine-daughters swimming and singing of their stolen gold; "Weialala leia, wallala leialala" is their refrain, and the Thames-daughters take it at 277–278 and 290–291. Listen to the scene, then read lines 266–306. Eliot's note says his three daughters "speak in turn" from 292 to 306.

### Way: Margate

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: Margate
body: "On Margate Sands. / I can connect / Nothing with nothing." Eliot wrote much of this section in a public shelter on the Margate seafront in October 1921, on sick leave from the bank. The shelter is still there. The map has it.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.
Errors: "much of this section": the typist scene and the opening were in typescript before Margate. Eliot's own words from Margate were "a rough draft of part of part III" (to Sydney Schiff, 4 November 1921) and "some fifty lines" (letter from Margate, November 1921; both quoted in the Guardian, 11 July 2009, with Valerie Eliot's letter supporting the listing: https://www.theguardian.com/books/2009/jul/12/ts-eliot-margate-shrine; the "fifty lines" sentence is, as I recall, from the letter to Richard Aldington, unverified). "October 1921": he was there from about 22 October to about 12 November, and the letters that report the work are from November (Letters vol. 1, from memory). "a public shelter on the Margate seafront": Eliot wrote "a shelter on the front"; the identification with the Nayland Rock shelter is the council's and the listing's (Grade II, 2009), supported by Valerie Eliot, not Eliot's own. "The shelter is still there": the Nayland Rock shelter stands. "on sick leave from the bank": right (three months' leave from Lloyds).
Readings passed off as fact: which shelter; how much of the section.
Missing: nothing.
Cut: "much of".
Proposed text:
"On Margate Sands. / I can connect / Nothing with nothing." Eliot spent three weeks at Margate, from late October to mid-November 1921, on three months' sick leave from Lloyds Bank. In letters from there he said he had drafted "part of part III", "some fifty lines", sitting in a shelter on the front. The Nayland Rock shelter on the seafront was listed in 2009 as the place; it still stands, and the map has it.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: keep, with one change
Reads as a model wrote it: nothing.
Unclear or unhelpful: "much of this section" — the Eliot pathway stop at line 300 says fifty lines, about a third of the section; the two should agree. Whether he wrote them in the shelter or only at Margate I cannot tell; the scholar should say what the letter says.
Keep: the quotation, the date, "on sick leave from the bank", "The shelter is still there. The map has it." — exactly how a person talks.
Proposed text: "On Margate Sands. / I can connect / Nothing with nothing." Eliot wrote some fifty lines of this section at Margate in October and November 1921, on sick leave from the bank, sitting in a public shelter on the seafront. The shelter is still there. The map has it.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** "much of this section" corrected to Eliot's own "some fifty lines"; dates; the shelter as the listed identification (scholar); the reader's ending kept.

**Scholar, round 2:** Object: the two quoted phrases come from one letter, to Sydney Schiff, and its wording is not settled from here: the Guardian (11 July 2009, quoting Valerie Eliot) prints "I have done a rough draft of part III"; the Letters, as I recall, read "part of part III". Quote only "some fifty lines" and paraphrase the rest. Replacement for the third sentence: In a letter to Sydney Schiff written there in early November 1921 he said he had done a rough draft of part of Part III, "some fifty lines", sitting in a shelter on the front. The listing is right: the application was in hand in July 2009 and English Heritage's news item "Seaside shelter where TS Eliot wrote The Waste Land is listed" was archived by 4 January 2010 (cited in Wikipedia, "Cliftonville"; the archived page itself is behind a verification wall from here).

**Reader, round 2:** Object: 'he had drafted "part of part III", "some fifty lines", sitting' — two quoted scraps back to back; a reader stumbles, and the second says what the first does. Proposed sentence: In letters from there he said he had drafted "some fifty lines" of this part, sitting in a shelter on the front. The rest as written.

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's third sentence, which quotes only "some fifty lines" and so also meets the scholar's objection to quoting the Schiff letter's wording here. Round 3: the scholar corrected "letters" to one letter, to Sydney Schiff, 4 November 1921: the Guardian of 11 July 2009, quoting Valerie Eliot, gives the shelter and "some fifty lines" in one continuous quotation from it, and the batch B scholar recalls the same letter in the facsimile's introduction. The editor's memory that "some fifty lines" was in the letter to Aldington is withdrawn.

**Scholar, round 3:** Correct "In letters from there" to "In a letter from there, to Sydney Schiff,". The Guardian of 11 July 2009 (https://www.theguardian.com/books/2009/jul/12/ts-eliot-margate-shrine), quoting Valerie Eliot's letter in support of the listing, gives the shelter and "some fifty lines" in one continuous quotation from the letter to Schiff: "I have done this while sitting in a shelter on the front - as I am out all day except when taking rest. I have written only some fifty lines, and have read nothing, literally". That agrees with my memory of the Letters (Schiff, 4 November 1921); the Aldington letter I recall as saying only that he had been doing a little writing, unverified. The rest of the item is right.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Margate
body: "On Margate Sands. / I can connect / Nothing with nothing." Eliot spent three weeks at Margate, from late October to mid-November 1921, on three months' sick leave from Lloyds Bank. In a letter from there, to Sydney Schiff, he said he had drafted "some fifty lines" of this part, sitting in a shelter on the front. The Nayland Rock shelter on the seafront, listed in 2009 as the likely place, still stands. The map has it.

### Way: Fire

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: Fire
body: The Water lens has a third colour for fire. In this section it appears at the end only, and all at once. Watch the river run through the whole section and then burn.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite.
Errors: none of fact; "appears at the end only" leaves out the title.
Readings passed off as fact: none.
Missing: the title; Eliot's note on 309.
Cut: "Watch the river run through the whole section and then burn."
Proposed text:
The Water lens marks fire in a third colour. In this section it falls on the title and on the last four lines, where "burning" comes five times, from the Buddha's sermon, after Augustine's "To Carthage then I came". Eliot's note says the "collocation" of the two "is not an accident".

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: "Watch the river run through the whole section and then burn" — a pose, nothing to do. "at the end only, and all at once" — the brisk fragment.
Unclear or unhelpful: the first sentence tells me a colour before it tells me what the colour is for.
Keep: the true observation underneath, that fire comes only at the end.
Proposed text: The section is called The Fire Sermon, but nothing burns in it until line 308; the fire of the last four lines is the Buddha's, from the sermon of the title, and Augustine's. The Water lens marks it there in a third colour.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** "Watch the river ... and then burn" cut (both); the title and Eliot's note brought in (scholar); the reader's plain observation kept.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: 'where "burning" comes five times, from the Buddha's sermon of the title, after Augustine's' — three modifiers hung on one clause, and the count repeats "The Fire Sermon itself" two items above; 'the "collocation" of the two' leaves a reader to work out which two. Proposed text: The Water lens marks fire in a third colour. In this section it falls on the last four lines only, where "burning", from the Buddha's sermon of the title, follows Augustine's "To Carthage then I came". Eliot's note says the placing of these two together "is not an accident".

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's text.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Fire
body: The Water lens marks fire in a third colour. In this section it falls on the last four lines only, where "burning", from the Buddha's sermon of the title, follows Augustine's "To Carthage then I came". Eliot's note says the placing of these two together "is not an accident".

### Way: Every bird

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: Every bird
body: The nightingale, the gulls, the cock, the hermit-thrush, the swallow. The birds are the poem's musicians, and their songs are all complaints. A short walk with recordings where they exist.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.
Errors: "their songs are all complaints" is false: the hermit-thrush's is the "water-dripping song" Eliot's note (357) calls "justly celebrated"; the cock's "Co co rico" (392) is a crow; the swallow is in a wish (428, "Quando fiam uti chelidon"); the gulls (312) do not sing at all. "The birds are the poem's musicians" is the annotator's metaphor.
Readings passed off as fact: the second sentence.
Missing: nothing.
Cut: the second sentence.
Proposed text:
The nightingale (here and in Part II), the gulls of Part IV, the cock and the hermit-thrush of Part V, and the swallow at 428. The Birds pathway takes them in order, with recordings where they exist.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: "The birds are the poem's musicians, and their songs are all complaints" — the aphorism, and I do not believe it: the hermit-thrush's song (357) and the cock's crow (392) are not complaints.
Unclear or unhelpful: the list is out of the poem's order (the thrush comes before the cock), and only one of these birds is in Part III, which a way into Part III should say.
Keep: "A short walk with recordings where they exist" — tells me what I get.
Proposed text: The Birds pathway stops at each bird in the poem: the nightingale here at line 203 (and in Part II), then the gulls, the hermit-thrush, the cock and the swallow in Parts IV and V. A short walk, with recordings where they exist.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** "all complaints" is false (both) and cut; the birds given in the poem's order with their parts; the swallow is at 429 in the site's count (428 is Dante's refining fire).

**Scholar, round 2:** Object: the decision note says the poem's order, but the hermit-thrush (site 358) comes before the cock (site 393). Replacement: The nightingale (here at 203–206, and in Part II), the gulls of Part IV, the hermit-thrush and the cock of Part V, and the swallow at 429.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: the first sentence has no verb, and it gives "the cock and the hermit-thrush" the wrong way round for a list the next sentence says is "in order" (the thrush is at 357, the cock at 392). Proposed text: The Birds pathway stops at each bird in the poem in order: the nightingale (here at 203–206, and in Part II), the gulls of Part IV, the hermit-thrush and the cock of Part V, and the swallow at 429, with recordings where they exist.

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's text, which also carries the scholar's correction of the order (thrush before cock).

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Every bird
body: The Birds pathway stops at each bird in the poem in order: the nightingale (here at 203–206, and in Part II), the gulls of Part IV, the hermit-thrush and the cock of Part V, and the swallow at 429, with recordings where they exist.

### Way: Tiresias in Ovid

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: Tiresias in Ovid
body: Eliot's note quotes nineteen lines of Latin about a man who was a woman for seven years. Read them in English, then read the typist scene again as something seen by a person who has been both people on the divan.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs hedging.
Errors: nineteen lines is right (<i>Met.</i> III, 320–338; counted); "a woman for seven years" is Ovid's ("femina septem / egerat autumnos"). "seen by a person who has been both people on the divan" is a reading, put to the reader in the second person.
Readings passed off as fact: the last clause.
Missing: what the nineteen lines say.
Cut: "Read them in English, then read ... again".
Proposed text:
Eliot's note quotes nineteen lines of Ovid (<i>Metamorphoses</i> III, 320–338). Tiresias, who had lived seven years as a woman, is asked to settle Jupiter and Juno's dispute over which sex has the greater pleasure in love; Juno blinds him for his answer and Jupiter gives him foresight. The library has the Latin with an English version. Eliot's note says "the two sexes meet in Tiresias"; the typist and the clerk are what he watches.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: "nineteen lines of Latin" — counting. "a person who has been both people on the divan" — clever, a little.
Unclear or unhelpful: it says nothing of what the Latin is about beyond the change of sex; the quarrel between Jupiter and Juno that Tiresias settles is the part that bears on the typist scene.
Keep: "a man who was a woman for seven years" — plain and right; read it in English, then the scene.
Proposed text: Eliot's note at line 218 quotes Ovid's story of Tiresias, who lived seven years as a woman and was asked by Jupiter and Juno to settle which sex has the more pleasure in love. The source page gives it in English. Read it, then the typist scene, which Tiresias says he has "foresuffered."

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** what the Latin says given (both wanted it); "nineteen lines" kept, since it is a plain count of what the note contains and the scholar verified it; the second-person close cut.

**Scholar, round 2:** Object, one sentence: the library record (ovid-met3) gives only the eight lines about the serpents, 324–331, not the nineteen. Replacement: The library gives the lines about the serpents (324–331) in Latin and English.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: the first sentence runs a colon and a semicolon through three separate facts. Proposed text: Eliot's note at 218 quotes nineteen lines of Ovid (<i>Metamorphoses</i> III, 320–338). Tiresias, who had lived seven years as a woman, is asked to settle Jupiter and Juno's dispute over which sex has the greater pleasure in love. Juno blinds him for his answer and Jupiter gives him foresight. The library has the Latin with an English version. Read it, then the typist scene, which Tiresias says he has "foresuffered".

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's split; the scholar's correction of what the library holds (324–331 only), in the reader's round-3 wording.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept (the reworded library sentence is right: the serpents he struck, and the change of sex that followed).

**Reader, round 3:** Alternative: "the lines about the serpents" arrive with no serpents mentioned before them. One line: The library gives lines 324–331, on the serpents he struck and the change of sex that followed, in Latin and English. ("Read them, then the typist scene ..." stays.)

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Tiresias in Ovid
body: Eliot's note at 218 quotes nineteen lines of Ovid (<i>Metamorphoses</i> III, 320–338). Tiresias, who had lived seven years as a woman, is asked to settle Jupiter and Juno's dispute over which sex has the greater pleasure in love. Juno blinds him for his answer and Jupiter gives him foresight. The library gives lines 324–331, on the serpents he struck and the change of sex that followed, in Latin and English. Read them, then the typist scene, which Tiresias says he has "foresuffered".

### Way: Whose side is the poem on

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: Whose side is the poem on
body: The typist "smooths her hair with automatic hand, / And puts a record on the gramophone." Contempt, or the only mercy in the poem? Readers have argued for a century. Read lines 249–256 once each way.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction and rewrite.
Errors: the quotation must read "smoothes" (site text, 255; the 1922 spelling the site keeps; Gutenberg's transcription has the later "smooths", so do not check against it). "Readers have argued for a century" and "the only mercy in the poem" cannot be checked. "Read lines 249–256 once each way" is the banned instruction.
Readings passed off as fact: none, but the two readings should be given as readings.
Missing: Goldsmith, which is what the two readings turn on.
Cut: sentences two to four.
Proposed title: The typist
Proposed text:
The typist "smoothes her hair with automatic hand, / And puts a record on the gramophone" (255–256). The scene has been read as the poem's contempt for her and as its nearest approach to pity. Eliot's note gives Goldsmith's song, in which the woman who "stoops to folly" has nothing left but to die; the typist does not.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: "Contempt, or the only mercy in the poem?" — the balanced antithesis and a superlative nobody can check. "Readers have argued for a century" — who? "Read lines 249–256 once each way" — the tell the owner named.
Unclear or unhelpful: "smooths" — the 1922 text has "smoothes" (line 255). It never says what the two readings rest on, so I have nothing to read for.
Keep: the question in the title; the quotation, corrected.
Proposed text: In Goldsmith's song, which Eliot's note at line 253 points to, the woman who "stoops to folly" has nothing left but to die. Eliot's typist "smoothes her hair with automatic hand, / And puts a record on the gramophone." Contempt for her, or pity? Critics have read it both ways; those who read it as pity point to lines 243–246, where Tiresias says he has "foresuffered all."

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** "smoothes" (the 1922 spelling the site keeps); "argued for a century", "the only mercy" and "once each way" cut; Goldsmith brought in (scholar); the two readings given as readings with what each rests on (reader). The title kept, since it is a plain question and the way is an "ask".

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "the typist does not" is clipped to a riddle (does not die? does not stoop?); "The scene has been read as" hides who reads. Proposed text: The typist "smoothes her hair with automatic hand, / And puts a record on the gramophone" (255–256). Eliot's note gives Goldsmith's song, in which the woman who "stoops to folly" has nothing left but to die; Eliot's typist, instead, puts a record on. Critics have read the scene as contempt for her, and as the nearest the poem comes to pity; those who read it as pity point to Tiresias's "foresuffered all" (243).

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's text.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Whose side is the poem on
body: The typist "smoothes her hair with automatic hand, / And puts a record on the gramophone" (255–256). Eliot's note gives Goldsmith's song, in which the woman who "stoops to folly" has nothing left but to die; Eliot's typist, instead, puts a record on. Critics have read the scene as contempt for her, and as the nearest the poem comes to pity; those who read it as pity point to Tiresias's "foresuffered all" (243).

### Pathway stop: water, line 182

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

text: "By the waters of Leman I sat down and wept." Babylon's river, and Lake Geneva, where the poem was written.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction. "where the poem was written": Parts IV and V were written at Lausanne (November–December 1921) and the whole was put together there; Parts I–III were drafted before, in London and at Margate. The cities stop at 182 says "finished", which is right; make the two agree.
Proposed text:
"By the waters of Leman I sat down and wept." Psalm 137's rivers of Babylon, with Lake Geneva (Lac Léman) in their place; Eliot finished the poem at Lausanne, on the lake, in December 1921.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: "Babylon's river, and Lake Geneva, where the poem was written" — compressed to a riddle.
Unclear or unhelpful: "where the poem was written" — the Cities stop at the same line says "finished", the Eliot stop says Parts IV and V; the three should agree. Nothing tells a reader that Leman is Lake Geneva.
Proposed text: "By the waters of Leman I sat down and wept." Psalm 137 ("By the rivers of Babylon, there we sat down, yea, we wept"), with Lake Geneva put in for Babylon; Eliot was at Lausanne, on the lake, in late 1921, finishing the poem.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** see the text.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "Psalm 137's Babylon" gives a reader who does not know the psalm nothing to hear the line against; the psalm's words are short and free. Proposed text: "By the waters of Leman I sat down and wept." Psalm 137, "By the rivers of Babylon, there we sat down, yea, we wept", with Lake Geneva (Lac Léman) in Babylon's place. Eliot finished the poem at Lausanne, on the lake, in the winter of 1921–22.

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader wanted the psalm's words; given in the Prayer Book wording, which is the one Eliot's line follows (batch A scholar), not the King James the reader quoted.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept. The Prayer Book (Coverdale) Psalter reads "By the waters of Babylon we sat down and wept: when we remembered thee, O Sion", and Eliot's line follows it, not the King James.

**Reader, round 3:** Accept

**After** (the text now in the file):

text: "By the waters of Leman I sat down and wept." Psalm 137 in the Prayer Book wording, "By the waters of Babylon we sat down and wept", with Lake Geneva (Lac Léman) in Babylon's place. Eliot finished the poem at Lausanne, on the lake, in the winter of 1921–22.
