## The drafts records (batch C)

### Drafts: fresca

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: The seventy lines about Fresca
body: "The Fire Sermon" began, in typescript, with about seventy lines of heroic couplets describing a fashionable woman, Fresca, waking, breakfasting in bed, reading Richardson on the lavatory and writing a letter. It was a deliberate imitation of Pope's <i>Rape of the Lock</i> and Swift's dressing-room poems. Pound struck it all out. Eliot recalled his reason in 1946: "Pope has done this so well that you cannot do it better; and if you mean this as a burlesque, you had better suppress it, for you cannot parody Pope unless you can write better verse than Pope, and you can't." The section now begins with the river.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction (rights) and hedging.
Errors: the description is right as far as I know: about seventy lines of couplets on two typescript pages (Ricks and McCue's editorial composite; Rainey; from memory), covering her waking, breakfast in bed, the "needful stool" with Richardson, a letter, the bath and her reading. Pope's <i>Rape of the Lock</i> is Eliot's own account (the 1946 essay, and again in the 1959 <i>Paris Review</i> interview, "an imitation Rape of the Lock"); Swift's dressing-room poems are the critics' comparison and should be given as such. "Pound struck it all out": his pencil runs through the passage and it went; fine. The 1946 quotation is about forty-five words of Eliot's "Ezra Pound" (<i>Poetry</i>, September 1946); it matches my memory of the text (the Poetry Foundation archive is behind a challenge page from here, so unverified), but under the site's rule it is a passage, not a phrase, and must be paraphrased with at most a phrase kept.
Readings passed off as fact: Swift.
Missing: optional: some Fresca lines were reused in Vivien Eliot's "Letters of the Moment II" in the <i>Criterion</i>, April 1924 (Rainey; from memory).
Cut: the long quotation.
Proposed text:
In the typescript "The Fire Sermon" opened with some seventy lines of heroic couplets on a fashionable woman, Fresca: her waking, her breakfast in bed, her visit to the "needful stool" with a novel of Richardson, her letter, her bath, her reading. Eliot said later that it was written in imitation of Pope's <i>Rape of the Lock</i>; critics also hear Swift's dressing-room poems. Pound struck it out. Eliot recalled in 1946 that Pound's objection was that Pope had done this so well it could not be done better, and that one cannot parody Pope without writing better verse than Pope, "and you can't". The printed section begins with the river.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: little. "a deliberate imitation" — "deliberate" is padding.
Unclear or unhelpful: the 1946 quotation is two whole sentences of Eliot after 1929, which the rights rule says to describe or quote a phrase of. "reading Richardson" — a reader may not know he is a novelist.
Keep: the shape of the record: what the passage was, what it imitated, that Pound struck it out, why, how the section now begins.
Proposed text: In typescript the section opened with about seventy lines of heroic couplets on a fashionable woman, Fresca: she wakes, breakfasts in bed, reads a novel of Richardson's on the lavatory, writes a letter. It imitates Pope's <i>Rape of the Lock</i> and Swift's dressing-room poems. Pound struck it all out. Eliot recalled his reason in 1946: Pope had done this so well that it could not be done better, and "you cannot parody Pope unless you can write better verse than Pope." The section now begins with the river.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** the 1946 quotation reduced to a phrase (rights rule, both); Swift as the critics' comparison, Pope as Eliot's own account (scholar); Richardson named as a novelist (reader); Rainey's dating of the printed opening added here, where it belongs.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept. Rainey's dating stands as far as I know it, and the text bears it out: line 182 names Leman, where Eliot did not arrive until late November 1921, so the ten lines cannot belong to the London typescript that opened with Fresca; as I recall the facsimile, the London typescript ran from the Fresca couplets straight into the refrain lines that became 183–184, and 173–182 are on a separate leaf typed with the Paris fair copies. "On Rainey's dating" is the right hedge.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "critics also hear Swift's dressing-room poems" — "hear" is a critic's verb; and the last sentence joins the printed opening and Rainey's dating on a semicolon. Proposed: "critics also compare Swift's dressing-room poems." and, for the last sentence: The printed section begins with the river. On Rainey's dating of the drafts, its opening lines, 173–182, were the last lines written for the poem, in Paris in January 1922. The rest as written.

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's two changes ("compare"; the last sentence split).

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: The seventy lines about Fresca
body: In the typescript "The Fire Sermon" opened with some seventy lines of heroic couplets on a fashionable woman, Fresca: her waking, her breakfast in bed, her visit to the "needful stool" with a novel of Richardson's, her letter, her bath, her reading. Eliot said later that it was written in imitation of Pope's <i>Rape of the Lock</i>; critics also compare Swift's dressing-room poems. Pound struck it out. Eliot recalled in 1946 that Pound's objection was that Pope had done this so well it could not be done better, and that one cannot parody Pope without writing better verse than Pope, "and you can't". The printed section begins with the river. On Rainey's dating of the drafts, its opening lines, 173–182, were the last lines written for the poem, in Paris in January 1922.

### Drafts: typist-draft

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: Pound and the typist
body: The typist scene was longer, in quatrains throughout, with more circumstantial detail about the clerk (his hair, his evening, his "one bold stare" was there) and more hedging from Tiresias. Pound's pencil comments on these pages are the ones everyone quotes: "verse not interesting enough as verse to warrant so much of it"; "Perhaps be damned" against a line with "perhaps" in it; and, beside Tiresias's foretelling, "make up yr. mind / you Tiresias / if you know / know damn well / or / else / you / dont." The clerk's original exit had him urinating in the street; Pound cut it. A passage after the typist, beginning "London, the swarming life you kill and breed," addressed the city directly and went too.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.
Errors: "in quatrains throughout": right; the irregular lines of the 1922 text (215–218, 222–223) are where Pound cut half-lines out of regular quatrains (the draft's first stanza ended on "the horn or ivory gates"; from memory). "(his hair, his evening, his 'one bold stare' was there)": muddled. The draft's clerk had his age (twenty-one), his spotty face, hair "thick with grease, and thick with scurf", his table manners and his boast of an evening with the painter Nevinson; "one bold stare" is in the printed text (232), so it is not a cut detail. The typist also lost a stanza (a "bright kimono", a Japanese print bought in Oxford Street). "a line with 'perhaps' in it": the line is "Perhaps a cheap house agent's clerk", which became 232; say so. "beside Tiresias's foretelling": as I recall, "make up yr. mind ..." stands beside the stanza calling the clerk one of those loiterers "whom we say / We may have seen in any public place", the second hedge, not beside "foretold the rest" (229); unverified against the facsimile. "urinating in the street": the couplet had him stop "at the corner where the stable is" to urinate and spit; Pound's comment there, as I recall, was "probaly over the mark"; the couplet went and the dots at 248 fall where it stood. "A passage after the typist, beginning 'London, the swarming life you kill and breed'": before, not after, as I recall: in the typescript it stands between Mr. Eugenides (214) and "At the violet hour" (215), and Pound's comments on it include "B-ll-S" and "Palmer Cox's brownies" (against "Phantasmal gnomes"); from memory of the facsimile and of Ricks and McCue, unverified. Rights: three quotations of Pound's marginalia; keep the shortest forms.
Readings passed off as fact: none.
Missing: that the dots at 248 mark a cut.
Cut: "the ones everyone quotes".
Proposed text:
In the typescript the scene ran in regular quatrains and was longer. The clerk had his age, his spotty face, his greasy hair, his table manners and his boasts; the typist a kimono and a Japanese print; and Tiresias hedged: "Perhaps a cheap house agent's clerk", one of those "whom we say / We may have seen". Pound wrote that the verse was "not interesting enough as verse" for so much of it, "Perhaps be damned" against the first hedge, and beside the second told Tiresias to make up his mind. The clerk's exit, a couplet in which he stopped at a corner to urinate and spit, was marked by Pound and dropped; the dots at 248 fall where it stood. A passage before the scene addressed to London ("London, the swarming life you kill and breed") went too.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: "the ones everyone quotes" — the superlative.
Unclear or unhelpful: "(his hair, his evening, his "one bold stare" was there)" — I cannot parse it; I think it means the phrase survived from the draft. The eight-line marginal note is quoted whole, and so is a whole line of the draft, where the rule says a phrase. "'Perhaps be damned' against a line with 'perhaps' in it" — I cannot tell which line, and it does not matter.
Keep: the facts: longer, in quatrains, more about the clerk, Pound's verdict on the verse, the exit, the London passage.
Proposed text: The typist scene was longer in typescript, in rhymed quatrains throughout, with more about the clerk (his hair, how he spends his evenings) and a more hesitant Tiresias. Pound's pencil is all over these pages. Beside the quatrains he wrote that the verse was "not interesting enough as verse" to warrant so much of it; against a "perhaps" he wrote "Perhaps be damned"; and beside Tiresias's foretelling he told Eliot to make up his mind whether Tiresias knows or not. The clerk's exit, in which he stopped in the street to urinate, went, and so did a passage after the scene that addressed London directly.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** the scholar's account of what the draft had (the clerk's age, face, hair, manners, boasts; the typist's kimono and print), which agrees with the editor's own memory of the facsimile; "Perhaps be damned" placed against "Perhaps a cheap house agent's clerk"; Pound's Tiresias comment described, not quoted at length, and not placed by line; the London passage stood before the scene, not after; the dots at 248; "the ones everyone quotes" cut. Quotation kept to phrases.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept. Nothing in it is doubtful enough to cut: the age ("A youth of twentyone"), the spotted face, the greasy hair, the table manners (the cigarette ash on the mat), the Nevinson boast, the kimono and the Japanese print from Oxford Street, "Perhaps a cheap house agent's clerk", the loiterers "whom we say / We may have seen in any public place", the London passage before "At the violet hour", the urinate-and-spit couplet with "probaly over the mark": my memory of the facsimile agrees on every point, and none of the quoted phrases exceeds the rights rule.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "The clerk had his age, his spotty face ... ; the typist a kimono and a Japanese print; and Tiresias hedged" — "had his age" is not English for what the draft gave him, the typist's clause has lost its verb, and the semicolons stack three things; "beside the loiterers" is beside lines, not people; "A passage before the scene addressed to London" can be read as the scene being addressed to London. Proposed text: In the typescript the scene ran in regular quatrains and was longer. The draft gave the clerk his age, a spotty face, greasy hair, table manners and boasts, and gave the typist a kimono and a Japanese print. Tiresias hedged with "Perhaps a cheap house agent's clerk", one of those loiterers who might have been seen anywhere. Pound wrote that the verse was "not interesting enough as verse" for so much of it. Against the hedge he wrote "Perhaps be damned", and beside the loiterers' lines he told Tiresias to make up his mind whether he knew or not. The clerk's exit, a couplet in which he stopped at a corner to urinate and spit, was marked by Pound and dropped; the dots at 248 fall where it stood. A passage that stood before the scene, addressed to London ("London, the swarming life you kill and breed"), went too.

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's text; the scholar confirmed every detail in it. Round 3: where Pound's "make up yr. mind" stands is given as the batch A scholar placed it in round 3 (beside the typist's "may pass" line, facsimile p. 47, borne out by the printed change to "allows"), not beside the loiterers as this batch's scholar recalled; recorded as a difference between the two.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: Pound and the typist
body: In the typescript the scene ran in regular quatrains and was longer. The draft gave the clerk his age, a spotty face, greasy hair, table manners and boasts, and gave the typist a kimono and a Japanese print. Tiresias hedged with "Perhaps a cheap house agent's clerk", one of those loiterers who might have been seen anywhere. Pound wrote that the verse was "not interesting enough as verse" for so much of it. Against the hedge he wrote "Perhaps be damned", and beside the typist's "one half-formed thought may pass" (printed at 251 as "allows one half-formed thought to pass") he told Tiresias to make up his mind whether he knew or not. The clerk's exit, a couplet in which he stopped at a corner to urinate and spit, was marked by Pound and dropped; the dots at 248 fall where it stood. A passage that stood before the scene, addressed to London ("London, the swarming life you kill and breed"), went too.

### Drafts: thames-draft

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

title: The Thames-daughters, lengthened and shortened
body: The river song and the three daughters' speeches survive much as drafted. Pound's marks here are approving; he wrote "OK" and "echt" against the short-lined river passage. Eliot's note in the printed edition, numbering the daughters' turns, was written later, for the book.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction and hedging.
Errors: "survive much as drafted": true of the river song, the Elizabeth stanza and the second and third daughters, as I recall; the first daughter's speech was reworked by Eliot himself from a longer manuscript version (unverified). "he wrote 'OK' and 'echt' against the short-lined river passage": I recall both words on these pages but cannot place them by line; unverified; keep the words only if the editor can check the facsimile, and drop the location. "Eliot's note ... numbering the daughters' turns, was written later, for the book": the note does not number them; it says "(three)" and that they "speak in turn" from 292 to 306; and all the notes were added for the Boni and Liveright book (December 1922), the Criterion and Dial printings having none, so the sentence implies a distinction that does not exist. Title: "lengthened and shortened" describes nothing.
Readings passed off as fact: the placing of Pound's words.
Missing: that this stretch is the part of the poem generally taken to be the "some fifty lines" Eliot drafted at Margate in November 1921, the one fact a reader of the drafts page most needs here.
Cut: the last sentence as written.
Proposed title: The Thames-daughters in draft
Proposed text:
The river song, the Elizabeth stanza and the three daughters' speeches are the part of the poem generally taken to be the "some fifty lines" Eliot drafted at Margate in November 1921, and they reached print with few marks from Pound; the words he wrote here, "OK" and "echt" (genuine), approve. Eliot's own revision shortened the first daughter's speech. His note identifying three daughters who "speak in turn" from 292 to 306 was added, like all the notes, for the book edition of December 1922.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: the title, "lengthened and shortened" — an antithesis, and the body says the passage survived much as drafted, so which is it?
Unclear or unhelpful: "survive much as drafted" — I cannot tell whether to believe it; the scholar should say whether the daughters' speeches were cut (I recall the first daughter's being longer in draft, unverified). "Eliot's note ... was written later, for the book" — true of every note, so why say it here? The point worth making is that the poem itself never says there are three daughters or where their turns fall; only the note does.
Keep: Pound's "OK" and "echt".
Proposed text (title): The Thames-daughters in draft
Proposed text (body): Pound left the river song and the daughters' speeches largely alone; his marks here are approving, "OK" and "echt" against the short-lined river passage. Nothing in the text says there are three daughters, or where each one's turn begins; that is in Eliot's note, added for the book edition.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** title changed ("lengthened and shortened" describes nothing); the Margate identification brought in (scholar); Pound's "OK" and "echt" kept without a placing by line; the first daughter's speech shortened by Eliot (both scholars, from memory; stated without figures); the note "added, like all the notes" (scholar).

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept. Keep the sentence: three memories agree (mine, batch B's, the reader's) that the first daughter's speech was longer in manuscript; what I recall is a first version running on about Highbury's trees and park, which "Trams and dusty trees" compresses, and "At Kew we had tea" and "a perilous canoe" among the variants. If the editor wants a verb that does not rest on memory of length, "reworked" is safe; "shortened" is what I believe.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: the first sentence runs to a semicolon and ends 'the words he wrote here, "OK" and "echt" (genuine), approve', where "approve" hangs without an object; "the Elizabeth stanza" calls 279–289 a stanza. Proposed text: The river song, the Elizabeth and Leicester lines and the three daughters' speeches are generally taken to be the "some fifty lines" Eliot drafted at Margate in November 1921. They reached print with few marks from Pound, and the words he did write here, "OK" and "echt" (genuine), are approving. Eliot's own revision shortened the first daughter's speech. His note, which identifies three daughters who "speak in turn" from 292 to 306, was added, like all the notes, for the book edition of December 1922.

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's text; "shortened" kept on the scholar's word (three memories of the facsimile agree).

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**After** (the text now in the file):

title: The Thames-daughters in draft
body: The river song, the Elizabeth and Leicester lines and the three daughters' speeches are generally taken to be the "some fifty lines" Eliot drafted at Margate in November 1921. They reached print with few marks from Pound, and the words he did write here, "OK" and "echt" (genuine), are approving. Eliot's own revision shortened the first daughter's speech. His note, which identifies three daughters who "speak in turn" from 292 to 306, was added, like all the notes, for the book edition of December 1922.

## The library records for Part III's sources (batch D, proposals to the site thread)

### Library: dante-purg5

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

what: La Pia's "Siena mi fé, disfecemi Maremma" behind "Highbury bore me. Richmond and Kew / Undid me."
date: c. 1315
note: Among the late-repentant who died by violence, a woman speaks seven lines and gives her name, her birthplace, the place of her death and, by implication, her murderer, her husband. Early commentators named her Pia de' Tolomei of Siena and her husband Nello dei Pannocchieschi; nothing certain is known of her. Eliot's first Thames-daughter borrows the grammar and keeps the anonymity.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.
Errors: Passage and translation are accurate (Purg. V.130–136, Petrocchi text, it.wikisource Canto V). Title, author, date fine. "keeps the anonymity" is wrong: Pia names herself ("son la Pia"); it is Eliot's Thames-daughter who gives no name. "borrows the grammar" is loose; what is borrowed is the shape of the sentence (place made me, place unmade me). Eliot's note gives "Purgatorio, V, 133" and prints "fe'".
Readings passed off as fact: none.
Missing: that Eliot's note at 293 cites the passage.
Cut: "keeps the anonymity".
Proposed note: In Ante-Purgatory, among those who died by violence and repented at the last, a woman speaks seven lines: her name, her birthplace, the place of her death and, without naming him, the husband who put a ring on her and, the line implies, had her killed. Early commentators identified her as Pia de' Tolomei of Siena and the husband as Nello dei Pannocchieschi; nothing certain is known of her. Eliot's note at 293 cites the passage. His first Thames-daughter has the shape of Pia's sentence, birthplace and undoing, and no name.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: "speaks seven lines and gives her name, her birthplace, the place of her death and, by implication, her murderer, her husband" (counting, then the rhythmic list); "borrows the grammar and keeps the anonymity" (bow-tying last sentence; a metaphor where an explanation should be).
Unclear or unhelpful: "keeps the anonymity" contradicts the note's own "gives her name": la Pia names herself, the Thames-daughter does not. "Among the late-repentant who died by violence" is Dante's category in Dante's shorthand. "Maremma" is not explained.
Keep: what she tells Dante; the early commentators' identification; "nothing certain is known of her".
Proposed note: The souls in this canto repented only as they died, by violence. The last to speak is a woman who names herself la Pia, says Siena made her and the Maremma (the marsh country on the Tuscan coast) unmade her, and says that the husband who put his ring on her knows how she died. Early commentators named her Pia de' Tolomei of Siena and her husband Nello dei Pannocchieschi; nothing certain is known of her. Eliot's first Thames-daughter says where she was born and where she was undone in the same shape of sentence.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** see the text.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "In Ante-Purgatory, among those who died by violence and repented at the last, a woman speaks seven lines: her name, her birthplace, the place of her death and, without naming him, the husband who put a ring on her and, the line implies, had her killed." The colon-list with two interruptions; a reader loses the thread, and "the line implies" states the commentators' reading as the line's. Put: "In Ante-Purgatory, among those who died by violence and repented at the last, a woman speaks seven lines. She names herself, says that Siena made her and the Maremma unmade her, and says that the husband who put his ring on her knows how she died; she does not name him, and the words are taken to mean that he had her killed." And "his first Thames-daughter has the shape of Pia's sentence, birthplace and undoing, and gives no name" is cryptic; put: "his first Thames-daughter says where she was born and where she was undone in the shape of Pia's sentence, and gives no name."

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's two rewordings.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**Proposed** (for the site thread):

note:
In Ante-Purgatory, among those who died by violence and repented at the last, a woman speaks seven lines. She names herself, says that Siena made her and the Maremma unmade her, and says that the husband who put his ring on her knows how she died; she does not name him, and the words are taken to mean that he had her killed. The Maremma is the marsh country on the Tuscan coast. Early commentators identified her as Pia de' Tolomei of Siena and the husband as Nello dei Pannocchieschi; nothing certain is known of her. Eliot's note at 293 cites the passage; his first Thames-daughter says where she was born and where she was undone in the shape of Pia's sentence, and gives no name.

### Library: gotterdammerung

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

what: "Weialala leia / Wallala leialala": the Rhine-daughters' refrain.
date: 1876 (first performed Bayreuth)
note: The last opera of the <i>Ring</i> opens its third act with the three Rhine-daughters swimming and singing for the gold that Alberich stole in the first scene of the first opera, and asking Siegfried to give back the ring. Their refrain is the "la la" that Eliot's Thames-daughters sing over oil and tar. Eliot's note is precise about the transfer: "The Song of the (three) Thames-daughters begins here. . . . V. <i>Götterdämmerung</i>, III, i: the Rhine-daughters."

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs hedging.
Errors: Summary of III.i is right; date right (Bayreuth, 17 August 1876). The passage is the sung text as vocal scores and modern librettos print it ("des Vaters Gold noch in ihr glänzte", "Wie hell du einstens strahltest", then the refrain). Wagner's printed poem has no vocalise at all and reads "des Vaters Gold in ihr glänzte!" and "wie hell strahltest du einst" (Schott librettos of 1874 and 1882, archive.org 11185940bsb and gtterdmmerun01wagn); the full-score text gives the refrain in a longer form ("Weialala, weialala heia leia wallala la...", murashev.com). The two-line form in the record is the libretto's, and the one the commentaries print (Southam; Ricks and McCue, from memory, unverified). Not an error, but the note should not imply the refrain is in Wagner's poem as he printed it. Translation: "hehr" is noble or august, not holy ("heil und hehr" = whole and noble; "hehrer Stern der Tiefe" = noble star of the deep). "Their refrain is the 'la la' that Eliot's Thames-daughters sing over oil and tar": the daughters' refrain is "Weialala leia / Wallala leialala" (277–278, 290–291); "la la" is the third daughter's ending at 306.
Readings passed off as fact: "Eliot's note is precise about the transfer" is the annotator's gloss on a note that is simply quoted.
Missing: nothing.
Cut: "the 'la la' that ... over oil and tar"; "is precise about the transfer".
Proposed trans (two words): "whole and noble" for "heil und hehr"; "noble star of the deep".
Proposed note: The last of the four <i>Ring</i> operas opens its third act with the three Rhine-daughters swimming in the river and singing to the sun for the gold Alberich stole in the first scene of <i>Das Rheingold</i>; Siegfried comes by, and they ask him for the ring. Their wordless refrain, as the vocal scores and librettos print it, is the one Eliot's Thames-daughters sing at 277–278 and 290–291. Eliot's note at 266: "The Song of the (three) Thames-daughters begins here. . . . V. <i>Götterdämmerung</i>, III, i: the Rhine-daughters."

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: "Eliot's note is precise about the transfer" (annotator admiring Eliot; "transfer" is a metaphor); "the 'la la' that Eliot's Thames-daughters sing over oil and tar" (cute compression).
Unclear or unhelpful: quoting Eliot's note back is redundant; the site prints it at line 266. "Their refrain is the 'la la'" muddles: the refrain is the two Weialala lines, and "la la" at 306 is its tail.
Keep: the plot in one sentence: third act, three Rhine-daughters, the gold Alberich stole at the start of the first opera, asking Siegfried for the ring.
Proposed note: The last of the four <i>Ring</i> operas opens its third act with the three Rhine-daughters swimming and singing of the gold Alberich stole from them at the start of the first, <i>Das Rheingold</i>; Siegfried comes to the bank and they ask him for the ring. Eliot's note on line 266 sends the reader here for the song of the Thames-daughters, whose refrain is the Rhine-daughters' own.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** see the text.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "Their wordless refrain, as the vocal scores and librettos print it, is the one Eliot's Thames-daughters sing at 277–278 and 290–291". "As the vocal scores and librettos print it" is a hedge a reader cannot use: as opposed to what? Put: "Their refrain is sung syllables without meaning, and in the two-line form the librettos give it is the one Eliot's Thames-daughters sing at 277–278 and 290–291." If the scholar needs the hedge kept, say the thing plainly: that Wagner's printed poem has no refrain and the two-line form is the librettos'. Also "singing to the sun for the gold" reads as "in exchange for"; put "singing to the sun of the gold".

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's "of the gold" and the plain statement of the refrain's form. Round 3: the scholar corrected "the two-line form the librettos give it" (no printing gives Eliot's two lines as a unit; the printed poem has no vocalise, the scores spread a longer string among the three voices).

**Scholar, round 3:** Correction: "the two-line form the librettos give it" is wrong; Wagner's printed poem (Schott librettos, 1874 and 1882) has no vocalise at all, and the vocal scores (Klindworth 1900, Schirmer 1904, checked on archive.org) spread a longer string of syllables among the three voices, so no printing gives Eliot's two lines as a unit. Replace "and in the two-line form the librettos give it is the one Eliot's Thames-daughters sing at 277–278 and 290–291" with "which Wagner's printed poem leaves out and the scores supply at length; Eliot's Thames-daughters sing two lines of it at 277–278 and 290–291".

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**Proposed** (for the site thread):

trans (two changes): "whole and noble" for "heil und hehr"; "noble star of the deep" for "hehrer Stern der Tiefe".
note:
The last of the four <i>Ring</i> operas opens its third act with the three Rhine-daughters swimming and singing to the sun of the gold Alberich stole in the first scene of <i>Das Rheingold</i>; Siegfried comes by, and they ask him for the ring. Their refrain is sung syllables without meaning, which Wagner's printed poem leaves out and the scores supply at length. Eliot's Thames-daughters sing two lines of it at 277–278 and 290–291, and Eliot's note at 266 sends the reader to the scene.

### Library: shakespeare-tempest

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

what: Ariel's song, "Those are pearls that were his eyes"; Ferdinand on the bank; "This music crept by me upon the waters."
date: c. 1611
note: Ferdinand, washed ashore after the shipwreck, believes his father Alonso drowned; Ariel's song tells him so, beautifully and falsely. Alonso is alive, and by the end of the play everyone has suffered a sea-change for the better. Eliot quotes the song twice and Ferdinand's speech twice, and never allows the change. The drowned father with pearls for eyes becomes the drowned Phoenician and Phlebas; Ferdinand weeping on the bank becomes the Fisher King by the gasworks.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite.
Errors: Passage accurate (I.ii; Gutenberg #23042). "Eliot quotes the song twice and Ferdinand's speech twice": the song is quoted at 48 and 125; Ferdinand's speech is quoted once (257) and adapted at 191–192, where "Weeping again the king my father's wreck" becomes a brother's wreck and a father's death. "by the end of the play everyone has suffered a sea-change for the better" is not so (Antonio and Sebastian are unrepentant); the phrase is Ariel's, about the drowned man. "gasworks": the poem says "gashouse".
Readings passed off as fact: "beautifully and falsely"; "never allows the change"; "becomes the drowned Phoenician and Phlebas"; "becomes the Fisher King" (the usual reading, via Weston and Eliot's note to 46, not a fact).
Missing: Eliot's notes at 192 and 257.
Cut: all of the above.
Proposed note: Ferdinand, cast ashore, believes his father Alonso drowned in the wreck; Ariel's song tells him so, though Alonso is alive. The poem takes the song's line about the pearls twice (48, 125) and Ferdinand's "This music crept by me upon the waters" once (257), and at 191–192 turns his "Weeping again the king my father's wreck" into a musing on a brother's wreck and a father's death. Eliot's notes at 192 and 257 point to the scene. The song's next lines, the "sea-change / Into something rich and strange", are not quoted.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: "beautifully and falsely" (balanced antithesis); "everyone has suffered a sea-change for the better" (annotator's wit on the source's word); "Eliot quotes the song twice and Ferdinand's speech twice, and never allows the change" (counting, then the bow); "The drowned father ... becomes ...; Ferdinand weeping on the bank becomes ..." (matched clauses on a semicolon).
Unclear or unhelpful: "never allows the change" is a reading stated as fact. "the drowned Phoenician and Phlebas" reads as two people; they are one. "becomes the Fisher King" is a reading I cannot check from anything the note gives me, and the poem says gashouse, not gasworks. In `what`, "Ferdinand on the bank" does not tell me which words are Ferdinand's.
Keep: the plot: Ferdinand believes his father drowned, Ariel's song tells him so, Alonso is alive. That is what I need at 191 and 257.
Proposed what: Ariel's song, "Those are pearls that were his eyes"; Ferdinand's "the king my father's wreck" and "This music crept by me upon the waters."
Proposed note: Ferdinand, cast ashore by the storm, believes his father Alonso drowned in the wreck, and Ariel's song tells him so. Alonso is in fact alive, and the play ends with father and son reunited. Eliot takes Ariel's pearls for Madame Sosostris's drowned sailor (line 48) and for the memory in Part II (125), and gives Ferdinand's words to the fisher in Part III (191) and to the walker in the City (257). Critics have noted that the poem keeps the drowning and the music and leaves out the play's restoration.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** see the text.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Accept

**Proposed** (for the site thread):

what: Ariel's song, "Those are pearls that were his eyes"; Ferdinand's "the king my father's wreck" and "This music crept by me upon the waters."
note:
Ferdinand, cast ashore, believes his father Alonso drowned in the wreck; Ariel's song tells him so, though Alonso is alive. The poem takes the song's line about the pearls twice (48, 125) and Ferdinand's "This music crept by me upon the waters" once (257), and at 191–192 turns his "Weeping again the king my father's wreck" into a musing on a brother's wreck and a father's death. Eliot's notes at 192 and 257 point to the scene. The song's next lines, the "sea-change / Into something rich and strange", are not quoted.

### Library: ovid-met6

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

what: "The change of Philomel."
date: AD 8
note: Tereus of Thrace, escorting his wife's sister Philomela to visit Procne, rapes her in a hut in the woods and cuts out her tongue; she weaves the crime into a cloth and sends it to her sister; Procne kills her son Itys and serves him to his father; Tereus, pursuing the sisters with a sword, and the sisters, fleeing, all become birds. Ovid does not say which sister became the nightingale; later tradition made Philomela the nightingale and Procne the swallow, and Eliot follows it. The story sits above the mantel in Part II, breaks into birdsong in Part III, and ends the poem as a swallow. Arthur Golding's 1567 translation, the one Shakespeare read, is the English to go to.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs hedging (minor); trim.

"later tradition": the Latin poets (Virgil's "philomela" is the nightingale) and after; the Greek versions make Procne the nightingale. The swallow at 429 (site count) is from the Pervigilium Veneris, and Eliot's note there says "Cf. Philomela in Parts II and III", so "ends the poem as a swallow" is Eliot's own link. "the one Shakespeare read, is the English to go to" is chat, and the Shakespeare fact does not help with these lines.
Readings passed off as fact: none.

Ovid does not say which sister became the nightingale (one "seeks the woods, the other goes under the roof"); the Latin poets and the later tradition made her Philomela, and Eliot follows them. The story is on the wall in Part II (99–103); the bird's cry, "jug jug" and "Tereu" in the Elizabethan spelling, breaks in at 203–206 around "So rudely forc'd" from line 100; and Eliot's note to the swallow of 429 refers back to "Philomela in Parts II and III".

He pursues the sisters with a sword, and all three are turned into birds. Ovid does not say which sister became the nightingale; later tradition made Philomela the nightingale and Procne the swallow, and Eliot follows it. trans: As they fly from him you would think that the bodies of the two Athenians were poised on wings: they were poised on wings! One flies to the woods, the other rises to the roof. Tereus, swift in pursuit because of his grief and eager desire for vengeance, is himself changed into a bird. trans: As they fly from him you would think that the bodies of the two Athenians were poised on wings: they were poised on wings! One flies to the woods, the other rises to the roof. ### Library: ovid-met3

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

what: The man who had been a woman, and who sees the future.
date: AD 8
note: Lines 324–331 of the passage Eliot's note quotes at length, calling it "of great anthropological interest." Jupiter and Juno, disputing which sex takes more pleasure in love, consult the one person who has been both; his answer costs him his sight and earns him prophecy. Eliot's note makes him the poem's uniting consciousness. Ovid makes him a man who lost an argument with a goddess.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.
Errors: The passage is Met. III.324–331; Eliot's note quotes III.320–338 (checked at thelatinlibrary.com/ovid/ovid.met3.shtml). "Lines 324–331 of the passage Eliot's note quotes" is true but unclear; give book and lines. Passage and translation accurate. "Ovid makes him a man who lost an argument with a goddess" is false: Tiresias argued with no one; asked to judge, he sided with Jupiter and Juno blinded him for it. It is also the bow-tying antithesis the standard forbids.
Readings passed off as fact: "Eliot's note makes him the poem's uniting consciousness" paraphrases the note in the critics' word; quote the note instead.
Missing: nothing.
Cut: last sentence.
Proposed note: <i>Metamorphoses</i> III.324–331; Eliot's note quotes III.320–338 and calls the passage "of great anthropological interest". Jupiter and Juno, disputing which sex has the greater pleasure in love, refer it to Tiresias, who had been both: he struck two coupling snakes with his staff and was a woman for seven years, then struck them again and was a man. He sides with Jupiter; Juno blinds him; Jupiter, who cannot undo what a god has done, gives him knowledge of the future. Eliot's note makes him "the most important personage in the poem, uniting all the rest".

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: `what`, "The man who had been a woman, and who sees the future" (a riddle for a label); "Eliot's note makes him the poem's uniting consciousness. Ovid makes him a man who lost an argument with a goddess." (the balanced pair, the epigram to close).
Unclear or unhelpful: "Lines 324–331 of the passage" opens on a fragment and does not say whose lines. The note never says what Tiresias answered, which is the point of the story. "lost an argument with a goddess" is not what the passage says: he judged the argument and was punished for his verdict. "uniting consciousness" is critic-speak for Eliot's plain "uniting all the rest".
Keep: "consult the one person who has been both"; "his answer costs him his sight and earns him prophecy"; the pointer to Eliot's "of great anthropological interest".
Proposed what: Tiresias, who lived seven years as a woman, and how he came to see the future.
Proposed note: Jupiter and Juno, arguing which sex has the greater pleasure in love, put the question to Tiresias, who had known both. These lines tell how: he had struck two coupling snakes with his staff and been turned into a woman, and in the eighth year struck them again and became a man. He sided with Jupiter. Juno blinded him for it, and Jupiter, who could not undo what another god had done, gave him knowledge of the future instead. The passage here is <i>Metamorphoses</i> III.324–331, the middle of the passage Eliot's note quotes in full, calling it "of great anthropological interest" and Tiresias "the most important personage in the poem, uniting all the rest".

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** see the text.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "<i>Metamorphoses</i> III.324–331; Eliot's note quotes III.320–338 and calls the passage "of great anthropological interest"." A bare citation for a first sentence. And "He sides with Jupiter; Juno blinds him; Jupiter, who cannot undo what a god has done, gives him knowledge of the future." Three clauses on semicolons in the historic present, the brisk tell. Put: "The passage is <i>Metamorphoses</i> III.324–331, the middle of the longer stretch (III.320–338) that Eliot's note quotes and calls "of great anthropological interest". Jupiter and Juno, disputing which sex has the greater pleasure in love, put the question to Tiresias, who had been both: he had struck two coupling snakes with his staff and lived seven years as a woman, then struck them again and become a man. He sided with Jupiter. Juno blinded him for it, and Jupiter, who could not undo what another god had done, gave him knowledge of the future instead. Eliot's note calls him "the most important personage in the poem, uniting all the rest"." ("Calls him", not "makes him": that is what a note does.)

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's text.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**Proposed** (for the site thread):

what: Tiresias, who lived seven years as a woman, and how he came to see the future.
note:
The passage is <i>Metamorphoses</i> III.324–331, the middle of the longer stretch (III.320–338) that Eliot's note quotes and calls "of great anthropological interest". Jupiter and Juno, disputing which sex has the greater pleasure in love, put the question to Tiresias, who had been both: he had struck two coupling snakes with his staff and lived seven years as a woman, then struck them again and become a man. He sided with Jupiter. Juno blinded him for it, and Jupiter, who could not undo what another god had done, gave him knowledge of the future instead. Eliot's note calls him "the most important personage in the poem, uniting all the rest".

what: "By the waters of Leman I sat down and wept."
date: 6th century BC; translation 1611
note: The exiles' psalm, which is also a psalm about being asked to write a poem when you cannot. Eliot, in exile from the bank on the shore of Lake Geneva, took its first line and put his lake in it.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite.
Errors: Passage accurate (KJV 137:1–4, Wikisource). "6th century BC" is the exile; fine as an approximation. "a psalm about being asked to write a poem when you cannot" is a second-person reading; "in exile from the bank" is a joke about Lloyds; "put his lake in it" a flourish. Eliot was at Lausanne, on sick leave from Lloyds Bank, under Vittoz, from about 22 November to the end of December 1921; that this line was written there is not established (he had drafted part of Part III at Margate by 4 November, as he told Sydney Schiff: from memory of the Letters, unverified).
Readings passed off as fact: the "poem you cannot write" reading.
Missing: "leman", the old word for a lover or mistress, which Southam and Ricks and McCue hear in the name (from memory, unverified).
Cut: everything after the first clause.
Proposed note: The psalm of the exiles in Babylon, asked by their captors for "one of the songs of Zion". Leman is Lac Léman, Lake Geneva: Eliot was at Lausanne, on leave from the bank for treatment, in the last weeks of 1921, when the poem was finished. The old word "leman", a lover or mistress, has been heard in the name.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: "which is also a psalm about being asked to write a poem when you cannot" (reframing wit, second person, a reading as fact); "in exile from the bank" (a joke that needs you to know Eliot worked at Lloyds); "put his lake in it" (cute).
Unclear or unhelpful: the note never says what Leman is. "Exile" overstates a sick leave.
Keep: the exiles' psalm; Eliot on Lake Geneva.
Proposed note: The psalm of the Jews in captivity in Babylon, asked by their captors to sing them a song of Zion. Eliot was at Lausanne, on Lake Geneva, Lac Léman in French, in the last weeks of 1921, working on the poem, and puts the lake's name where the psalm has Babylon.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** see the text.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept. Confirmed: the Prayer Book psalter is the Great Bible's (1539), and that text reads "By the waters of Babilon we sat downe and weapte, when we remembred Syon" (textusreceptusbibles.com/Great/19/137; Coverdale 1535 the same but for spelling); the King James has "By the rivers of Babylon, there we sat down, yea, we wept". Eliot's "By the waters of Leman I sat down and wept" follows the Prayer Book. Since the record names the source Eliot's words follow, the passage should carry the Prayer Book verse the editor gives, "By the waters of Babylon we sat down and wept: when we remembered thee, O Sion", and the author field should say "Prayer Book psalter (Coverdale)" rather than "King James Version"; if those fields stay, the note's sentence covers it.

**Reader, round 2:** Accept

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the text below.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

The Part III gloss "leman" now quotes the Prayer Book wording.
note:
The psalm of the exiles in Babylon, asked by their captors for "one of the songs of Zion". Eliot's line follows the Prayer Book wording, "By the waters of Babylon we sat down and wept", not the King James "rivers" given here. Leman is Lac Léman, Lake Geneva: Eliot was at Lausanne, on the lake, on leave from the bank for treatment, in the last weeks of 1921, when the poem was finished. The old word "leman", a lover or mistress, has been heard in the name.

date: written c. 1650; published 1681
note: The great English argument for going to bed now, because later there is only the grave. **Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction (voice).
Errors: Passage is the modernized text; the 1681 Miscellaneous Poems (Wikisource) reads "alwaies", "Desarts" and so on; fine. The quotation from the 1921 essay is verified in Homage to John Dryden (1924; archive.org homagetojohndryd00elio): "it is, what we have designated tentatively as wit, a tough reasonableness beneath the slight lyric grace." The essay appeared in the Times Literary Supplement for 31 March 1921 (from memory, the tercentenary; the 1924 preface says only "several years ago"). "written months before the poem": the poem was drafted through 1921; say "in the year of the poem". "The great English argument for going to bed now" is a superlative and chat; "the turn of the argument" a metaphor.
Readings passed off as fact: none.
Missing: line numbers.
Cut: first sentence; "the turn of the argument".
Proposed note: Marvell's poem argues that time is short and the lady should yield now. Eliot twice quotes "But at my back I always hear" (185, 196) and each time gives it a new object: the rattle of the bones, the horns and motors. His essay "Andrew Marvell" (<i>Times Literary Supplement</i>, March 1921) found in Marvell "a tough reasonableness beneath the slight lyric grace".

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: "The great English argument for going to bed now, because later there is only the grave" (aphoristic opening; uncheckable superlative); "gives it a rattle of bones the first time and motor horns the second" (the tidy pairing).
Unclear or unhelpful: "the turn of the argument" is precious; say which line.
Keep: the sense of Marvell's poem in a sentence; the 1921 essay and its phrase, which is Eliot's wit, not ours.
Proposed note: Marvell's speaker urges his mistress to yield now, because time is short and the grave waits. Eliot uses the opening of the lines twice, and what the speaker hears at his back is first the rattle of bones and a chuckle (185–186), then the horns and motors that bring Sweeney to Mrs Porter (196–198). Earlier in 1921 Eliot had written his essay on Marvell, praising "a tough reasonableness beneath the slight lyric grace".

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** see the text.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "Eliot twice takes "But at my back I always hear" (185, 196) and each time gives it a new object: the rattle of the bones, the horns and motors." He does not take the line whole (his lines drop "always"), and "object" will be read as purpose, not grammar. Put: "Eliot twice adapts "But at my back I always hear" (185, 196), and what is heard is first the rattle of the bones (186), then the horns and motors (197)."

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's sentence ("adapts"; what is heard, by line).

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

### Library: day-parliament-of-bees

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

what: Actaeon and Diana, turned into Sweeney and Mrs Porter.
date: printed 1641 (written c. 1608–16)
note: A masque-like sequence of "characters" among the bees; the passage Eliot's note quotes describes a spring hunt bringing Actaeon to the goddess bathing. In Ovid the hunter who sees Diana naked is turned into a stag and torn apart by his own dogs. Eliot brings Sweeney to Mrs Porter in a motor car.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.
Errors: Date field "written c. 1608–16" is unsupported. Bullen (Works of John Day, 1881, archive.org worksofjohndayno00dayjrich) says the 1641 quarto is the only edition extant; a quarto of 1607 was reported by Langbaine's editors and by Lamb but has never been found; the work shares whole scenes with Dekker's Wonder of a Kingdom (1636) and Rowley's Noble Spanish Soldier (1634); a manuscript is in the British Library (Lansdowne 725). The passage is in Character 3, spoken by Polypragmus, "the Plush Bee", describing the palace he will build, with a roof of forests where at a "noise of horns and hunting" Actaeon will come upon Diana and be torn by his hounds; Bullen's text: "When of the sudaine, listning, you shall heare / A noise of Hornes and hunting, which shall bring / Actaeon to Diana in the spring, / Where all shall see her naked skin; and there / Actaeons hounds shall their owne Master teare". Eliot's note modernizes; fine. "a spring hunt" misreads Day: "the spring, / Where all shall see her naked skin" is the pool where Diana bathes; it is Eliot's line that makes it the season (Southam notes the play on the word, as I recall). "in a motor car" is the annotator's; the poem has "horns and motors". "masque-like sequence of 'characters'" is fair.
Readings passed off as fact: "a spring hunt".
Missing: who speaks and where.
Cut: "in a motor car".
Proposed date: printed 1641; date of writing unknown.
Proposed note: Twelve verse "characters" of bees. In the third, the ambitious Polypragmus describes the palace he will build, with a roof of forests where, at a sudden "noise of horns and hunting", Actaeon will come upon Diana bathing and be torn by his own hounds (Ovid, <i>Metamorphoses</i> III). In Day "the spring" is the pool where "all shall see her naked skin"; in Eliot's line it is the season. Eliot's note at 197 quotes the four lines. The only edition is the quarto of 1641; an edition of 1607 was reported by early cataloguers but has never been found, and the work shares scenes with plays printed in 1634 and 1636, so when Day wrote it is not known.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: "Eliot brings Sweeney to Mrs Porter in a motor car" (the joke to close; the `what` has already said it).
Unclear or unhelpful: "A masque-like sequence of 'characters' among the bees" does not tell me what the work is; say that the bees stand for kinds of men.
Keep: the Ovid sentence on Actaeon, which is the thing I need; "a spring hunt bringing Actaeon to the goddess bathing".
Proposed note: A sequence of verse "characters", satirical portraits in which bees stand for kinds of men. The lines Eliot's note quotes tell how a noise of horns and hunting will bring Actaeon upon Diana bathing. In Ovid the hunter who sees Diana naked is turned into a stag and torn apart by his own hounds. Eliot keeps the horns, adds motors, and the hunter and goddess become Sweeney and Mrs Porter.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** see the text.

**Scholar, round 2:** Object, twice. (1) "may be" is too weak. In Day the words that follow fix the sense: "Actaeon to Diana in the spring, / Where all shall see her naked skin; and there / Actaeons hounds shall their owne Master teare" (Bullen, p. 24); "where" and "there" are a place, and Ovid's Diana bathes at a spring, "fons sonat a dextra tenui perlucidus unda" (III.161). Replace the sentence with: In Day "the spring" is the water Diana bathes in, as in Ovid ("Where all shall see her naked skin"); in Eliot's line it is the season. (2) "The only edition is the quarto of 1641" overstates: Bullen said so in 1881, but the Early English Books microfilms list an issue dated 1643 (archive.org bim_early-english-books-1641-1700_the-parliament-of-bees-_day-john-1574-1640_1643), which I could not examine; the 1697 "Parliament of Bees. A Fable" is a different work. Replace with: The earliest edition that survives is the quarto of 1641; an edition of 1607 was reported by early cataloguers but has never been found, so when Day wrote it is not known.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "describes the palace he will build, with a roof of forests where, at a sudden "noise of horns and hunting", Actaeon will come upon Diana bathing". "A roof of forests" is baffling without a word of explanation, and the palace is not what the reader needs. Put: "describes the palace he will build, where, at a sudden "noise of horns and hunting", Actaeon will come upon Diana bathing and be torn by his own hounds (Ovid, <i>Metamorphoses</i> III)." And "The only edition is the quarto of 1641; an edition of 1607 was reported by early cataloguers but has never been found, so when Day wrote it is not known." The phantom 1607 edition is more than a library note needs; the date field already says the date of writing is unknown. Put: "The only edition is the quarto of 1641; when Day wrote it is not known." The rest, including the two senses of "spring", is clear and I accept it.

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's palace sentence (the roof of forests cut); the scholar's sentence on "the spring" (the scholar has the last word on what Day's line means); on the editions, the scholar's "earliest that survives" with the reader's cut of the phantom 1607 edition, which the date field already covers.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** Accept both. "In Day 'the spring' is the water Diana bathes in, as in Ovid ('Where all shall see her naked skin'); in Eliot's line it is the season" is clear and gives its evidence; "The earliest edition that survives is the quarto of 1641; when Day wrote it is not known" is plain.

**Proposed** (for the site thread):

date: printed 1641; date of writing unknown
note:
Twelve verse "characters" of bees, satirical portraits in which bees stand for kinds of men. In the third, the ambitious Polypragmus describes the palace he will build, where, at a sudden "noise of horns and hunting", Actaeon will come upon Diana bathing and be torn by his own hounds (Ovid, <i>Metamorphoses</i> III). In Day "the spring" is the water Diana bathes in, as in Ovid ("Where all shall see her naked skin"); in Eliot's line it is the season. Eliot's note at 197 quotes the four lines. The earliest edition that survives is the quarto of 1641; when Day wrote it is not known.

### Library: verlaine-parsifal

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

what: "Et O ces voix d'enfants, chantant dans la coupole!"
date: 1886
note: Verlaine's sonnet on Wagner's last opera, written for the <i>Revue wagnérienne</i>, compresses the whole plot into thirteen lines and then, in the fourteenth, simply reports the sound of the boys' choir in the Grail temple. Eliot places that line after a barrack-room song about a brothel, so that the temptation and the temple stand one line apart. Parsifal is Weston's Grail knight; his healing of the king is the thing the poem never manages.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.
Errors: Passage, line 7: "rentre sous la tente", not "sa tente" (Œuvres complètes, Vanier, vol. II, on fr.wikisource; also poesie-francaise.fr); trans accordingly "returns to the tent". First printed in the Revue wagnérienne, no. XII, 8 January 1886, in the set "Hommage à Wagner", sonnets by Mallarmé, Verlaine, Ghil, Merrill, Morice, Vignier, Wyzewa and Dujardin (archive.org, Revue wagnérienne vol. 1 index); collected in Amour (1888). Date 1886 right. Eliot's line differs from Verlaine's in a capital O and a comma after "enfants". "a barrack-room song about a brothel": Eliot's note calls the Mrs Porter lines a ballad "reported to me from Sydney, Australia"; that the song was sung by Australian troops and that fuller versions concern a brothel-keeper is Southam's and later editors' information (from memory), not the poem's. "Parsifal is Weston's Grail knight" is loose: Weston's book is on the Grail romances (Wolfram's Parzival among them), not on Wagner.
Readings passed off as fact: "so that the temptation and the temple stand one line apart"; "his healing of the king is the thing the poem never manages".
Missing: the first printing.
Cut: the last two sentences.
Proposed note: Verlaine's sonnet on Wagner's last opera, printed in the <i>Revue wagnérienne</i> of 8 January 1886 among a set of sonnets in homage to Wagner, and collected in <i>Amour</i> (1888). Thirteen lines give the story: Parsifal resists the Flower Maidens and Kundry, wins back the spear, heals King Amfortas and becomes king and priest of the Grail; the last line is the sound of the boys' choir singing in the dome of the Grail temple. Eliot's note at 202 cites the poem; he prints the line with a capital O and a comma.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: "compresses the whole plot into thirteen lines and then, in the fourteenth, simply reports" (counting; "simply" admiring); "so that the temptation and the temple stand one line apart" (alliterated antithesis; intention as fact); "his healing of the king is the thing the poem never manages" (bow-tying; reading as fact).
Unclear or unhelpful: "a barrack-room song about a brothel": nothing in lines 199–201 tells me it is about a brothel, so I cannot tell whether to believe it. "Weston's Grail knight" assumes I know who Weston is. The note never says what happens in the opera, which the sonnet assumes.
Keep: written for the <i>Revue wagnérienne</i>; the last line reports the boys' choir in the Grail temple; Eliot quotes it straight after the Mrs Porter song.
Proposed note: Verlaine's sonnet on Wagner's <i>Parsifal</i>, written for the <i>Revue wagnérienne</i>, runs through the opera in thirteen lines: the knight resists the flower-maidens and Kundry, heals the wounded king with the spear and becomes keeper of the Grail. The last line is the children's voices singing in the dome of the Grail temple, and it is the one Eliot quotes, straight after the soldiers' song about Mrs Porter. The knight who heals the wounded king is the Grail story Eliot's headnote says the poem draws on, through Jessie Weston's <i>From Ritual to Romance</i>.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** see the text.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "Verlaine's sonnet on Wagner's last opera, printed in the <i>Revue wagnérienne</i> of 8 January 1886 among a set of sonnets in homage to Wagner, and collected in <i>Amour</i> (1888)." The day of the month and the 1888 collection do not help with line 202. Put: "Verlaine's sonnet on Wagner's last opera, one of a set of sonnets in homage to Wagner printed in the <i>Revue wagnérienne</i> in January 1886." And "he prints the line with a capital O and a comma after "enfants", and sets it straight after the song about Mrs Porter" itemises punctuation a reader can see for himself in the passage. Put: "Eliot's note at 202 cites the poem; his line differs from Verlaine's only in punctuation and a capital O, and he sets it straight after the song about Mrs Porter."

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's cuts (the day of the month, the 1888 collection, the itemised punctuation).

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**Proposed** (for the site thread):

passage, line 7: "rentre sous la tente" (not "sa tente"); trans: "returns to the tent".
note:
Verlaine's sonnet on Wagner's last opera, one of a set of sonnets in homage to Wagner printed in the <i>Revue wagnérienne</i> in January 1886. Thirteen lines give the story: Parsifal resists the Flower Maidens and Kundry, wins back the spear, heals King Amfortas and becomes king and priest of the Grail; the last line is the sound of the boys' choir singing in the dome of the Grail temple. Eliot's note at 202 cites the poem; his line differs from Verlaine's only in punctuation and a capital O, and he sets it straight after the song about Mrs Porter.

### Library: lyly-campaspe

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

what: "Jug jug" and "Tereu."
date: 1584
note: The Elizabethan convention that the nightingale sings "jug jug" and calls "tereu," the vocative of Tereus, is at its plainest in this song from Lyly's court comedy; the songs in Lyly's plays were first printed in the collected edition of 1632, and whether he wrote them is disputed. Eliot's "Jug Jug to dirty ears" and the four-line burst of birdsong at lines 203–206 are Lyly's bird, with the pretty convention turned back into what it describes.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.
Errors: Date field "1584" is the play's (two quartos of 1584); the song, like all the songs in Lyly's plays, is not in the quartos and was first printed in Blount's Sixe Court Comedies (1632): Bond, Complete Works of John Lyly (1902), vol. II, pp. 264–265 (archive.org completeworksofj02lylyuoft). The song is in Campaspe V.i, sung by Trico; the 1632 text reads "What Bird so sings, yet so dos wayle? / O t'is the rauish'd Nightingale. / Iug, Iug, Iug, Iug, tereu, shee cryes, / And still her woes at Midnight rise." The record's modernized text is fine. Bond defended Lyly's authorship; W. W. Greg argued in 1905 that the songs are not his (from memory, unverified); "disputed" is right. "Tereu" the vocative of Tereus: right. "the four-line burst of birdsong at lines 203–206 are Lyly's bird": 205, "So rudely forc'd", is Eliot's own line 100, not the bird's.
Readings passed off as fact: "with the pretty convention turned back into what it describes".
Missing: act, scene, singer; the 1632 printing in the date field.
Cut: last clause.
Proposed date: play printed 1584; song first printed 1632.
Proposed note: The Elizabethan nightingale sings "jug jug" and cries "tereu", the vocative of Tereus; the convention is at its plainest in this song from Lyly's court comedy (V.i, sung by Trico). The songs in Lyly's plays are not in the quartos and were first printed in Blount's <i>Sixe Court Comedies</i> of 1632; whether Lyly wrote them has been disputed since W. W. Greg questioned it in 1905. Eliot's "'Jug Jug' to dirty ears" (103) and the cry at 203–206 use the convention's words; "So rudely forc'd" (205) repeats line 100.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: "is at its plainest in this song" (uncheckable superlative); "the four-line burst of birdsong" (counting, metaphor); "are Lyly's bird, with the pretty convention turned back into what it describes" (epigram to close).
Unclear or unhelpful: the first sentence is two unrelated facts on a semicolon; the 1632 printing and the doubt about authorship deserve their own plain sentence or none. "turned back into what it describes" made me stop and work out that it means the rape.
Keep: "tereu, the vocative of Tereus", which tells me what the bird is saying; the doubt about authorship, stated as doubt; the pointer to line 103.
Proposed note: In this song from Lyly's court comedy the "ravish'd" nightingale cries "jug, jug" and "tereu", the vocative of Tereus, as if calling the name of the man who raped her. The song may not be Lyly's: the songs in his plays were first printed in 1632, long after his death. Eliot uses the same bird-notes at line 103 ("Jug Jug" to dirty ears) and at lines 203–206, where they are set beside "So rudely forc'd".

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** see the text.

**Scholar, round 2:** Object: "has been disputed since W. W. Greg questioned it in 1905" makes Greg the first doubter, and my round-1 wording is to blame. Bond's edition of 1902 already records that the songs' absence from the quartos "has cast some doubt upon Lyly's authorship" (vol. II, p. 265), and Fleay had proposed Dekker before that; Greg's article ("The Authorship of the Songs in Lyly's Plays", Modern Language Review 1, 1905–6, pp. 43–52; archive.org modernlanguagere01modeuoft) argued that they were gathered for Blount's edition from other hands, Dekker's among them. Replace with: whether Lyly wrote them has long been doubted; W. W. Greg argued in 1905 that they are not his.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "Eliot's "'Jug Jug' to dirty ears" (103) and the cry at 203–206 use the convention's words". No convention has been named in this note (the scholar's opening sentence, which named it, was dropped), so "the convention's words" points at nothing. Put: "Eliot's "'Jug Jug' to dirty ears" (103) and the cry at 203–206 take the song's "jug jug" and "tereu"; "So rudely forc'd" (205) repeats line 100." And "The songs in Lyly's plays are not in the quartos and were first printed in Blount's <i>Sixe Court Comedies</i> of 1632, long after his death": "the quartos" means nothing to a reader, and the publisher and title are more than the note needs. Put: "The songs in Lyly's plays are missing from the early printings and first appeared in a collected edition of 1632, long after his death; whether he wrote them has been disputed since W. W. Greg questioned it in 1905." (If the scholar wants the 1632 title kept for checking, put it in brackets after "1632".)

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's two sentences, with the scholar's correction that Greg was not the first to doubt the songs. Round 3: "take the song's" corrected to "use the same", since Eliot names no source for the words and other Elizabethan poems have them (Barnfield, 1598); Lyly is the editors' candidate.

**Scholar, round 3:** Correction: "take the song's 'jug jug' and 'tereu'" makes this song Eliot's source, which Eliot nowhere says (his notes give none for these words) and which the same words in other Elizabethan poems (Barnfield's "Tereu, tereu! by and by", 1598, among them) leave open; Lyly is the editors' candidate. Replace "take the song's 'jug jug' and 'tereu'" with "use the same 'jug jug' and 'tereu'".

**Reader, round 3:** Accept. "Whether he wrote them has long been doubted; W. W. Greg argued in 1905 that they are not his" says how old the doubt is and names one doubter, which is what a reader can use.

**Proposed** (for the site thread):

date: play printed 1584; song first printed 1632
note:
In this song from Lyly's court comedy (Act V scene i) the "ravish'd" nightingale cries "jug, jug" and "tereu", the vocative of Tereus, as if calling the name of the man who raped her. The songs in Lyly's plays are missing from the early printings and first appeared in a collected edition of 1632, long after his death. Whether he wrote them has long been doubted; W. W. Greg argued in 1905 that they are not his. Eliot's "'Jug Jug' to dirty ears" (103) and the cry at 203–206 use the same "jug jug" and "tereu"; "So rudely forc'd" (205) repeats line 100.

### Library: sappho-104

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

what: The evening hour that "brings the sailor home from sea."
date: c. 600 BC
note: Two lines that survive because a critic, Demetrius in his treatise <i>On Style</i>, quoted them. Eliot's note says his lines "may not appear as exact as Sappho's" and that he was thinking of the "longshore" fisherman coming in at nightfall. Byron and Stevenson ("Home is the sailor, home from sea") are the English relay stations between Sappho and the typist's teatime.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.
Errors: "Two lines that survive because ... Demetrius ... quoted them" is inaccurate. Demetrius, On Style 141, quotes "Ἕσπερε, πάντα φέρεις ... φέρεις ὄϊν, φέρεις αἶγα, φέρεις ματέρι παῖδα" as an example of anaphora (Roberts's edition, 1902, archive.org demetriusonstyle01deme, Greek text and p. 139); the words "ὄσα φαίνολις ἐσκέδασ' Αὔως", "all that shining Dawn scattered", are preserved by the Etymologicum Magnum, a Byzantine lexicon, which cites the line for the Aeolic word αὔως, dawn (Wharton, Sappho, fr. 95, archive.org sapphomemoirtex00bunngoog). The text as printed is the editors' combination (Bergk's, with φέρων; Lobel-Page and Voigt print φέρηις). Byron, Don Juan III.107, "O, Hesperus! thou bringest all good things" (Gutenberg #21700), is an imitation of the fragment, noted as such by Wharton. Stevenson's "Requiem" (1887), "Home is the sailor, home from sea", is the usual parallel for 221 (Ricks and McCue, from memory, unverified). "relay stations" is a banned metaphor. The paraphrase of Eliot's note is right.
Readings passed off as fact: none beyond the Demetrius claim.
Missing: the Etymologicum Magnum; where Byron's lines are.
Cut: "relay stations ... teatime".
Proposed note: Two lines pieced together by editors from two quotations: Demetrius, <i>On Style</i> 141, gives the address to the Evening Star with its three "you bring" as an example of graceful repetition, and the Byzantine lexicon <i>Etymologicum Magnum</i> preserves the words "all that shining Dawn scattered". Byron imitated the lines in <i>Don Juan</i> III.107 ("O, Hesperus! thou bringest all good things"). Eliot's note at 221 says his lines "may not appear as exact as Sappho's lines" and that he had in mind the "longshore" or "dory" fisherman coming in at nightfall; "brings the sailor home from sea" also echoes Stevenson's "Requiem", "Home is the sailor, home from sea".

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: "relay stations" (named in the standard); "the typist's teatime" (cute).
Unclear or unhelpful: "Byron" with no title or line; I cannot tell what is meant or whether to believe it. The note never says what the two lines say; the `trans` does, but the note should point at it.
Keep: survival through Demetrius; Eliot's note on the "longshore" fisherman; Stevenson's line, quoted, which the poem's words plainly echo.
Proposed note: Sappho's lines to the evening star, which brings home everything the dawn scattered. Only these two survive, quoted by the critic Demetrius in <i>On Style</i>. Eliot's note allows that his lines "may not appear as exact as Sappho's" and says he had in mind the inshore fisherman who comes back at nightfall. "Brings the sailor home from sea" also echoes Stevenson's "Requiem", "Home is the sailor, home from sea".

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** see the text.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "Two lines pieced together by editors from two quotations: Demetrius, <i>On Style</i> 141, gives the address to the Evening Star with its three "you bring" as an example of graceful repetition, and the Byzantine lexicon <i>Etymologicum Magnum</i> preserves the words "all that shining Dawn scattered"." The note now opens with the textual history and never says in plain words what Sappho says; a reader wants that first, and the sentence is too long to carry both. Put: "Sappho's address to the evening star, which brings back all that the dawn scattered. The two lines survive only as quotations, and editors have joined them: Demetrius, <i>On Style</i> 141, quotes the star and its three "you bring" as an example of graceful repetition, and a Byzantine dictionary, the <i>Etymologicum Magnum</i>, preserves "all that shining Dawn scattered"." The Byron sentence does not help with line 221 (nothing in it is echoed in Eliot's words); cut it, or keep it only if the scholar can tie it to "home". The last sentence joins Eliot's note and Stevenson on a semicolon; put a full stop after "nightfall" and begin: ""Brings the sailor home from sea" also echoes Stevenson's "Requiem", "Home is the sailor, home from sea"."

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's order (what Sappho says first, then the textual history); Byron cut at the reader's asking, since nothing in Eliot's words echoes him; the last sentence split.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** Accept.

**Proposed** (for the site thread):

note:
Sappho's address to the evening star, which brings back all that the dawn scattered. The two lines survive only as quotations, and editors have joined them: Demetrius, <i>On Style</i> 141, quotes the star and its three "you bring" as an example of graceful repetition, and a Byzantine dictionary, the <i>Etymologicum Magnum</i>, preserves "all that shining Dawn scattered". Eliot's note at 221 says his lines "may not appear as exact as Sappho's lines" and that he had in mind the "longshore" or "dory" fisherman coming in at nightfall. "Brings the sailor home from sea" also echoes Stevenson's "Requiem", "Home is the sailor, home from sea".

date: 1766

"in the same meter" is wrong: Goldsmith's stanza is in tetrameters, Eliot's quatrain (253–256) in pentameters; what Eliot keeps is the first line and the alternate rhyme. "the poem's one act of mercy toward a woman" is an uncheckable superlative; "she is allowed not to die" a bow.

Eliot's note at 253 cites the song; he takes its first line and rhymes it into a pentameter quatrain in which the typist, instead of dying, puts on a record. The lines have been read both as contempt for her and as pity.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: "as contempt for her and as the poem's one act of mercy toward a woman: she is allowed not to die" (antithesis; an uncheckable "one act"; the epigram after the colon).
Unclear or unhelpful: "in the same meter, puts on a record" is compressed to the point of a joke; say that Eliot's line is Goldsmith's line continued. Whose readings?
Keep: the first sentence, which is exactly what I need; "has been read as", the right way to offer a reading.
Proposed note: Olivia Primrose, seduced and abandoned by the squire, sings this to her family after she has been brought home. Eliot's line 253 is Goldsmith's first line with "and" added, and the sentence runs on into the typist smoothing her hair and putting on a record, where Goldsmith's woman has nothing left but to die. The comparison has been read as contempt for the typist and as sympathy with her.

Olivia Primrose, seduced by Squire Thornhill and abandoned, sings this at her mother's request, on the spot where she first met him.
