### Library: froude-elizabeth

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

what: Elizabeth and Leicester in the barge.
date: 1863
note: Froude quotes a letter of 1561 from the Spanish ambassador, Álvaro de la Quadra, to Philip II. The marriage never happened; Dudley's wife had died the year before in circumstances that made it impossible. Eliot's Thames pageant of red and gold is this afternoon, seen from the water, with nothing coming of it.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.
Errors: The passage reproduces Eliot's note, not Froude. Froude reads "She was alone with the Lord Robert and myself on the poop" (Everyman's Library edition, vol. I, ch. IV, p. 244; archive.org reignofelizabeth01frouuoft); Eliot supplied "(The queen)" and dropped "the". The letter is headed "De Quadra to Philip, London, June 30" and the year is 1561 (the surrounding narrative). Eliot's "Vol. I, ch. iv" is the Everyman edition, where the letter falls in ch. IV; the original History (vol. VII, 1863) numbers differently (not checked). Álvaro de la Quadra, Bishop of Aquila, ambassador 1559–64 (from memory). Amy Robsart died at Cumnor Place on 8 September 1560 (from memory; well established). "in circumstances that made it impossible" is a historians' judgment; say what happened. "Eliot's Thames pageant ... is this afternoon, seen from the water, with nothing coming of it" is a reading as fact, with a flourish.
Readings passed off as fact: the last sentence.
Missing: the letter's date; the edition Eliot used.
Cut: "in circumstances that made it impossible"; the last sentence.
Proposed passage: In the afternoon we were in a barge, watching the games on the river. She was alone with the Lord Robert and myself on the poop, when they began to talk nonsense, and went so far that Lord Robert at last said, as I was on the spot there was no reason why they should not be married if the queen pleased.
Proposed note: Froude prints, in his own translation, a letter of 30 June 1561 from the Spanish ambassador, Álvaro de la Quadra, to Philip II, reporting an afternoon on the river with the queen and Lord Robert Dudley. Eliot's note at 279 quotes it, with "(The queen)" for Froude's "She"; his "Vol. I, ch. iv" is the Everyman's Library edition, where the letter is on p. 244. Dudley's wife, Amy Robsart, had been found dead at the foot of a staircase in September 1560, and the marriage never took place.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: rewrite
Reads as a model wrote it: "Eliot's Thames pageant of red and gold is this afternoon, seen from the water, with nothing coming of it" (bow-tying; metaphor; a reading as fact).
Unclear or unhelpful: "Lord Robert" in the passage is never connected to "Dudley" in the note or to Leicester in the poem. "in circumstances that made it impossible" withholds the fact it alludes to; either say it or leave it out.
Keep: who wrote the letter, to whom, when; that the marriage never happened.
Proposed note: Froude's history quotes a letter of 1561 from the Spanish ambassador, De Quadra, to Philip II, about an afternoon on the river when the queen and Lord Robert Dudley, later Earl of Leicester, talked of marrying. They never did. Dudley's wife had been found dead at the foot of a staircase the year before, and the talk that followed made the marriage impossible. Eliot's note points to the letter for lines 279–289.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** see the text.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Accept

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the text below.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**Proposed** (for the site thread):

passage: In the afternoon we were in a barge, watching the games on the river. She was alone with the Lord Robert and myself on the poop, when they began to talk nonsense, and went so far that Lord Robert at last said, as I was on the spot there was no reason why they should not be married if the queen pleased.
(Froude's words, checked against the 1911 Everyman page; the present passage is Eliot's note, with "(The queen)" for "She" and "the" dropped. The Part III gloss "elizabeth-leicester" now quotes Froude.)
note:
Froude prints, in his own translation, a letter of 30 June 1561 from the Spanish ambassador, Álvaro de la Quadra, to Philip II, reporting an afternoon on the river with the queen and Lord Robert Dudley, later Earl of Leicester. Eliot's note at 279 quotes it, with "(The queen)" for Froude's "She"; his "Vol. I, ch. iv" is the Everyman's Library edition, where the letter is on p. 244. Dudley's wife, Amy Robsart, had been found dead at the foot of a staircase in September 1560, and the marriage never took place.

### Library: augustine-confessions

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

what: "To Carthage then I came"; "O Lord Thou pluckest me out."
date: c. 400; translation 1838
note: Augustine's account of his student years in Carthage, "a cauldron of unholy loves," and, six books later, of the pull of beautiful things on a man trying to look at God. The Latin of the first sentence puns Carthago with sartago, a frying pan, which is why the Fire Sermon ends with it. Eliot uses Pusey's Victorian translation, the one in Everyman's Library, and rewrites its first sentence in his note as "to Carthage then I came, where a cauldron of unholy loves sang all about mine ears."

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.
Errors: Both passages are Pusey's words (Gutenberg #3296: Book III opens "To Carthage I came, where there sang all around me in my ears a cauldron of unholy loves"; the X.34 sentence is verbatim; the Gutenberg text carries no section numbers, but X.34 is the standard reference). Pusey 1838 right; the Everyman's Library reprint is 1907 (from memory). The Latin is "Veni Carthaginem, et circumstrepebat me undique sartago flagitiosorum amorum"; the play on Carthago and sartago (frying-pan) is real and noted by editors of Augustine (from memory). "which is why the Fire Sermon ends with it" is unwarranted: the part ends with "burning", and Eliot's own reason, in the note at 309, is the "collocation of these two representatives of eastern and western asceticism". "rewrites its first sentence in his note" is fair: Eliot's wording is not Pusey's. "a man trying to look at God" is loose for X.34, which is on the lust of the eyes.
Readings passed off as fact: the "which is why" clause.
Missing: Eliot's note at 309.
Cut: "which is why the Fire Sermon ends with it"; "Victorian".
Proposed note: Book III opens with Augustine's arrival as a student in Carthage, "where there sang all around me in my ears a cauldron of unholy loves" (Pusey); the Latin plays on Carthago and sartago, a frying-pan. Book X.34, on the lust of the eyes, confesses that visible beauty still catches him and that God plucks him out. Eliot's notes at 307 and 309 cite both; the wording of his first quotation is his own, not Pusey's, and his second note says that placing Augustine beside the Buddha, "these two representatives of eastern and western asceticism", "is not an accident". Pusey's translation of 1838 was the one in Everyman's Library.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: "which is why the Fire Sermon ends with it" (Eliot's motive stated as fact); "rewrites its first sentence in his note" (a deliberate act I cannot check; it may be misquotation from memory).
Unclear or unhelpful: "the pull of beautiful things on a man trying to look at God" is nearly lyric, though it does explain X.34. "six books later" makes me count.
Keep: student years in Carthage; Book X and the plucking out; Pusey and Everyman; the Carthago/sartago pun, which is real and helps.
Proposed note: Book III opens with Augustine's arrival as a student in Carthage, where "a cauldron of unholy loves" sang around him. In Book X, long after, he says that beautiful things still catch at him and that God plucks him out. The translation is Pusey's, the one in Everyman's Library; Eliot's note quotes the Carthage sentence in slightly different words. The Latin plays on Carthago and sartago, a frying pan, a pun critics have connected with the burning in the lines that follow.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** see the text.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "Book X.34, on the lust of the eyes, confesses that visible beauty still catches him and that God plucks him out." A book does not confess. Put: "In Book X.34, on the lust of the eyes, he confesses that visible beauty still catches him and that God plucks him out." And the stranded last sentence, "Pusey's translation of 1838 was the one in Everyman's Library", belongs with the first quotation; put, in the first sentence: ""where there sang all around me in my ears a cauldron of unholy loves", in Pusey's translation of 1838, the one in Everyman's Library; the Latin plays on Carthago and sartago, a frying-pan." Then split the long third sentence at the first semicolon: "Eliot's notes at 307 and 309 cite both. The wording of his first quotation is his own, not Pusey's, and his second note says that placing Augustine beside the Buddha, "these two representatives of eastern and western asceticism", "is not an accident"."

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's three changes.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**Proposed** (for the site thread):

note:
Book III opens with Augustine's arrival as a student in Carthage, "where there sang all around me in my ears a cauldron of unholy loves", in Pusey's translation of 1838, the one in Everyman's Library; the Latin plays on Carthago and sartago, a frying-pan. In Book X.34, on the lust of the eyes, he confesses that visible beauty still catches him and that God plucks him out. Eliot's notes at 307 and 309 cite both. The wording of his first quotation is his own, not Pusey's, and his second note says that placing Augustine beside the Buddha, "these two representatives of eastern and western asceticism", "is not an accident".

### Library: buddha-fire-sermon

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

what: The title of Part III and "Burning burning burning burning."
date: 5th century BC; translation 1896
note: Preached, the tradition says, to a thousand fire-worshipping ascetics on Gayā Head shortly after the enlightenment. Eliot read it at Harvard in Warren's <i>Buddhism in Translations</i> and his note calls it as important as the Sermon on the Mount. It is the text behind the whole third section, in which everything, from the river to the typist, is seen as burning.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs hedging.
Errors: Passage verbatim from Warren, Buddhism in Translations (1896), §73 "The Fire-Sermon", pp. 351–353, "Translated from the Mahā-Vagga (i.21¹)" (archive.org buddhismintransl00warr). Warren's heading has the Buddha preach on Gayā Head to "a thousand priests, who had all of them aforetime been monks with matted hair"; that they were fire-worshippers comes from the Mahāvagga's preceding story of the Kassapa brothers' fire-cult (from memory), not from this text, so "the tradition says" is right and the wording should show where it comes from. The Pali title Ādittapariyāya Sutta is the Saṃyutta Nikāya's (35.28); Warren's text is the Vinaya's; fine as a title. Eliot's note prints "Buddhism in Translation" without the s. "Eliot read it at Harvard" is presumed from his Indic studies (Sanskrit under Lanman 1911–12, Indian philosophy under Woods 1912–13), not recorded; hedge.
Readings passed off as fact: "the text behind the whole third section, in which everything ... is seen as burning".
Missing: Warren's section and page; the Harvard Oriental Series, which Eliot's note names.
Cut: the last sentence.
Proposed note: Preached, in the Mahāvagga's narrative, on Gayā Head to a thousand monks who had been matted-hair ascetics, tenders of the sacred fire, soon after the enlightenment: every sense and its objects are on fire with passion, hatred and infatuation. Warren's <i>Buddhism in Translations</i> (Harvard Oriental Series, 1896; §73, pp. 351–353), which Eliot's note names, was the standard English anthology of the Pali texts when Eliot studied Sanskrit and Indian philosophy at Harvard in 1911–13. His note says the sermon "corresponds in importance to the Sermon on the Mount"; it gives Part III its title and, at 308 and 311, its "burning".

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: "It is the text behind the whole third section, in which everything, from the river to the typist, is seen as burning" (a reading as fact, with the sweeping "everything, from X to Y").
Unclear or unhelpful: the note never says what the sermon says; the passage does, and one clause of it would help.
Keep: "Preached, the tradition says, to a thousand fire-worshipping ascetics"; Warren; Eliot's comparison with the Sermon on the Mount, which is his and rightly credited.
Proposed note: The Buddha's sermon, preached, the tradition says, to a thousand fire-worshipping ascetics on Gayā Head soon after his enlightenment: everything the senses meet is on fire, with the fire of passion, of hatred, of infatuation. Eliot read it at Harvard in Warren's <i>Buddhism in Translations</i>, the book his note recommends, and his note calls the sermon as important as the Sermon on the Mount. Part III takes its title from it, and "burning" at lines 308 and 311 is its word.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** see the text.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept. "Generally assumed" is the right strength: nothing in Eliot's own words places his reading of Warren at Harvard. If the site wants the ground for the assumption, one checkable fact supplies it: Warren's book is volume III of the Harvard Oriental Series, "edited ... by Charles Rockwell Lanman, Professor of Sanskrit in Harvard University" (title page, archive.org buddhismintransl00warr), and Lanman was Eliot's Sanskrit teacher in 1911–13.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "Preached, in the Mahāvagga's narrative, on Gayā Head to a thousand monks who had been matted-hair ascetics and tenders of the sacred fire, soon after the enlightenment: every sense and its objects are on fire with passion, hatred and infatuation." A fragment with three interruptions and a colon, and "the Mahāvagga's narrative" is unexplained. Put: "According to the Mahāvagga, the Pali text Warren translates it from, the Buddha preached it soon after his enlightenment, on Gayā Head, to a thousand monks who had been matted-hair ascetics and tenders of the sacred fire. It says that every sense and everything it meets is on fire, with passion, hatred and infatuation." The second sentence is too long and the bracket too heavy; put: "Eliot's note names Warren's <i>Buddhism in Translations</i> (Harvard Oriental Series, 1896), where the sermon is §73, pp. 351–353. It was the standard English anthology of the Pali texts when Eliot studied Sanskrit and Indian philosophy at Harvard in 1911–13, and it is generally assumed he met the sermon there." In the last sentence "it gives Part III its title" has an ambiguous "it" (the note or the sermon); put: "The sermon gives Part III its title and, at 308 and 311, its "burning"; Eliot's note says it "corresponds in importance to the Sermon on the Mount"."

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's three changes; the ground for the Harvard assumption added from the scholar's title-page check (Lanman edited the series and taught Eliot Sanskrit), in the reader's round-3 order.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** Alternative. As added, "it is generally assumed he met the sermon there; the series was edited by Charles Lanman, who taught Eliot Sanskrit" hangs the ground after the assumption on a semicolon, so a reader has to work out that the second clause explains the first. Put the ground first and split: "It was the standard English anthology of the Pali texts when Eliot studied Sanskrit and Indian philosophy at Harvard in 1911–13, and the series it appeared in was edited by Charles Lanman, who taught him Sanskrit. It is generally assumed he met the sermon there."

**Proposed** (for the site thread):

note:
According to the Mahāvagga, the Pali text Warren translates it from, the Buddha preached it soon after his enlightenment, on Gayā Head, to a thousand monks who had been matted-hair ascetics and tenders of the sacred fire. It says that every sense and everything it meets is on fire, with passion, hatred and infatuation. Eliot's note names Warren's <i>Buddhism in Translations</i> (Harvard Oriental Series, 1896), where the sermon is §73, pp. 351–353. It was the standard English anthology of the Pali texts when Eliot studied Sanskrit and Indian philosophy at Harvard in 1911–13, and the series it appeared in was edited by Charles Lanman, who taught him Sanskrit. It is generally assumed he met the sermon there. The sermon gives Part III its title and, at 308 and 311, its "burning"; Eliot's note says it "corresponds in importance to the Sermon on the Mount".

## The Voices lens notes for Part III's speakers (batch D, proposals to the site thread)

### Voice: fisher

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

label: The fisher on the bank
note: Spenser's poet, Ferdinand and the Fisher King in one voice: lines 173–198.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs hedging.
Errors: Range 173–198 holds: the "I" sings Spenser's refrain (176, 183–184), fishes (189), hears (185, 196); 173–175 is description. Spenser's poet and Ferdinand are Eliot's own notes (176, 192); "the Fisher King" is the usual reading, resting on Weston and Eliot's notes to the title and to 46, not something Eliot says of these lines. The words at 191–192 are adapted from Ferdinand, not quoted.
Proposed note: Lines 173–198: the "I" who sings Spenser's refrain (Eliot's note at 176), fishes in the canal, and muses, in words adapted from Ferdinand, on "the king my father's death" (note at 192). Usually taken as the Fisher King of Weston's book, whom Eliot's notes to the title and to 46 invoke.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: trim
Reads as a model wrote it: "in one voice" (the composite as a flourish).
Unclear or unhelpful: three names and nothing to hang them on; the Fisher King is a reading and should be marked as one.
Keep: the three identities; the line range.
Proposed note: Lines 173–198: the "I" on the river bank, who speaks Spenser's refrain and Ferdinand's words about his father's wreck, and fishes in the canal. He is usually taken as the Fisher King of the Grail legend.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** see the text.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "the "I" who sings Spenser's refrain": he says "I speak not loud or long"; put "speaks Spenser's refrain". And "Usually taken as the Fisher King of Weston's book, whom Eliot's notes to the title and to 46 invoke" sends a reader to three places for a one-line key. Put: "Usually taken as the Fisher King of the Grail legend, whom Eliot's note to line 46 names."

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's text.
Lines 173–198: the "I" who speaks Spenser's refrain (Eliot's note at 176), fishes in the canal, and muses, in words adapted from Ferdinand, on "the king my father's death" (note at 192). Usually taken as the Fisher King of the Grail legend, whom Eliot's note to line 46 names.

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept (Eliot's note to 46 does name "the Fisher King himself"; checked in the Gutenberg text).

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**Proposed** (for the site thread):

note: Lines 173–198: the "I" who speaks Spenser's refrain (Eliot's note at 176), fishes in the canal, and muses, in words adapted from Ferdinand, on "the king my father's death" (note at 192). Usually taken as the Fisher King of the Grail legend, whom Eliot's note to line 46 names.

### Voice: walker

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

label: The walker in the City
note: The 'I' who crosses London Bridge in the fog, cries out to Stetson, meets Mr. Eugenides, and walks from the Strand to Lower Thames Street. Lines 60–76, 207–211, 257–265.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs correction.
Errors: Lines check: 60–76 ("Unreal City" to "mon frère!"), 207–211, 257–265. "crosses London Bridge": the poem has the crowd flowing over the bridge and the "I" watching it and stopping Stetson. "walks from the Strand to Lower Thames Street": it is the music that goes "along the Strand, up Queen Victoria Street"; the "I" "can sometimes hear" beside a bar in Lower Thames Street. Both are inferences stated as fact. 257 is Ferdinand's line (Eliot's note: "The Tempest, as above"), which the fisher's voice had adapted at 191–192; the lens gives it to the walker, which should be said.
Proposed note: The "I" of the City passages: sees the crowd flow over London Bridge in the winter dawn and stops Stetson (60–76); is accosted by Mr. Eugenides in the winter noon (207–211); hears the music along the Strand and up Queen Victoria Street, and the mandoline by the bar in Lower Thames Street (257–265). At 257 he speaks Ferdinand's line (Eliot's note: "The Tempest, as above"), which the fisher had used at 191–192.

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: keep. Plain, clear, and tells me where the same "I" turns up.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** see the text.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: the whole note has grown from one sentence to a colon and three semicolon-clauses with brackets, the stacked-clause tell, plus a second sentence quoting a note the site already prints at 257. "Accosted" is also stronger than the poem, which says "Asked me". Put: "The "I" who watches the crowd flow over London Bridge and stops Stetson (60–76), is invited to luncheon by Mr. Eugenides (207–211), and hears the music along the Strand and the mandoline in Lower Thames Street (257–265). At 257 he speaks Ferdinand's line from <i>The Tempest</i>, which the fisher had adapted at 191–192."

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's text. Round 3: the scholar's correction, the invitation is at 212–214, which the lens gives to Mr. Eugenides.
The "I" who watches the crowd flow over London Bridge and stops Stetson (60–76), meets Mr. Eugenides (207–211), whose invitation follows at 212–214, and hears the music along the Strand and the mandoline in Lower Thames Street (257–265). At 257 he speaks Ferdinand's line from <i>The Tempest</i>, which the fisher had adapted at 191–192.

**Scholar, round 3:** Correction: the invitation is at 212–214, which the lens gives to Mr. Eugenides; the walker's 207–211 describe him. Replace "is invited to luncheon by Mr. Eugenides (207–211)" with "meets Mr. Eugenides (207–211), whose invitation follows at 212–214".

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**Proposed** (for the site thread):

note: The "I" who watches the crowd flow over London Bridge and stops Stetson (60–76), meets Mr. Eugenides (207–211), whose invitation follows at 212–214, and hears the music along the Strand and the mandoline in Lower Thames Street (257–265). At 257 he speaks Ferdinand's line from <i>The Tempest</i>, which the fisher had adapted at 191–192.

### Voice: tiresias

**Before** (the text on the site, as exported on 20 September):

label: Tiresias
note: Lines 215–251 and 253–256: the blind seer who has been both sexes and watches the typist's evening.

**Scholar, round 1:** Verdict: needs hedging; the note should keep Eliot's note and the lines apart, as the standard's test case asks.
Errors: Lines right (215–251, 253–256; 252 is the typist's). "watches": he is blind and says he "can see"; use the poem's words. "the blind seer who has been both sexes" is Ovid's story, which Eliot's note at 218 quotes.
Proposed note: Lines 215–251 and 253–256. Ovid's Theban seer (Eliot's note at 218 quotes the passage): blind, once a woman for seven years, he says he "can see" the typist's evening and has "foresuffered all". The same note calls him "a mere spectator and not indeed a 'character'" and yet "the most important personage in the poem, uniting all the rest".

**Reader, round 1:** Verdict: keep. Says who he is and what he watches.

**Editor, round 2 (decisions):** see the text.

**Scholar, round 2:** Accept.

**Reader, round 2:** Object: "Lines 215–251 and 253–256. Ovid's Theban seer (Eliot's note at 218 quotes the passage): blind, once a woman for seven years, he says he "can see" the typist's evening and has "foresuffered all"." A bare range for a sentence, then a bracket breaking the apposition before the colon. Put: "Lines 215–251 and 253–256: the blind seer of Thebes from Ovid, once a woman for seven years, who says he "can see" the typist's evening and has "foresuffered all". Eliot's note at 218, which quotes Ovid, calls him "a mere spectator and not indeed a 'character'" and yet "the most important personage in the poem, uniting all the rest"." The second sentence is worth keeping in a Voices key, since it is Eliot saying that the voice the lens marks is not a character.

**Editor, round 3 (decisions):** the reader's text.
Lines 215–251 and 253–256: the blind seer of Thebes from Ovid, once a woman for seven years, who says he "can see" the typist's evening and has "foresuffered all". Eliot's note at 218, which quotes Ovid, calls him "a mere spectator and not indeed a 'character'" and yet "the most important personage in the poem, uniting all the rest".

**Scholar, round 3:** Accept.

**Reader, round 3:** not asked: nothing of this item was altered after the reader's round-2 wording.

**Proposed** (for the site thread):

note: Lines 215–251 and 253–256: the blind seer of Thebes from Ovid, once a woman for seven years, who says he "can see" the typist's evening and has "foresuffered all". ## The standard given to both sides

### The standard for every note in Part III (read this first)

#### What this is
The annotated Waste Land at https://the-waste-land.netlify.app carries commentary beside every line. Its owner, Ethan, has read it and objected that the commentary is "written in the voice of an LLM". His two examples of what must go:

  1. a tour-guide heading: "Where the poem is standing"
  2. this sentence: "The poem's argument is a weather report: rain that is unwelcome, rivers that are dirty, a sea that drowns, and then twenty-nine lines of rock before three words of rain."

He asked for Eliot-scholar agents and reader agents to debate every section "to make sure it is clear, good in its wording, and real". You are one side of that debate for Part III, The Fire Sermon (lines 173 to 311 in the site's count). An editor runs the debate, decides, and applies the rewrites. Three rounds at most per note. On substance the scholar has the last word; on wording the reader has it. What is not settled is written down.

#### What "the voice of an LLM" means here, from Ethan's examples
- Tour-guide framing and headings ("Where the poem is standing", "Notice that", "Decide whether", "read it once each way").
- The aphoristic "X is a Y:" opening followed by a rhythmic list of three or four ("The poem's argument is a weather report: rain that..., rivers that..., a sea that...").
- The tidy flourish that counts things to sound clever ("twenty-nine lines of rock before three words of rain"; "a sentence, a word four times, a prayer, a prayer with its end torn off, one word alone"; "Four lines, three birds' worth of noise, then the name").
- Metaphors that describe the poem instead of explaining it ("a water-rhythm", "the river's own noise takes over", "relay stations", "the rhymes are doing the judging").
- The last sentence that ties a bow: the epigram, the paradox, the "X is not Y. It is Z." ("The nightingale's song in this poem is never lovely. It is evidence."; "The fire that burns here is lust. The fire at line 428 refines.")
- Second-person flourishes that make the reader the subject ("a question the poem asks about you"; "decides how you read the poem"; "a season you can feel on your skin").
- Superlatives nobody could check ("the most argued-over scene in the poem", "the most terrible short speech in the poem", "the most violent cut in the poem", "the poem's newest object", "its most modern word", "the poem's hour").
- Balanced antitheses for their own sake ("the poem's most private moment and its most public"; "Spenser's river is a procession; Eliot's is the morning after"; "skip the seduction and keep the dying").
- Fragments and stacked clauses strung with colons and semicolons to sound brisk.

#### What to aim at instead
The plainness of a good scholarly editor writing for readers: Ricks and McCue's commentary in the 2015 Poems, Southam's Guide, Rainey's Annotated Waste Land. Say what the line refers to, what is known about it and how it is known, what is disputed, and, when a reading is offered, whose it is or that it is one. Then stop. A note may be one sentence. Short sentences are fine; fragments and stacked clauses are not. A fact that does not help a reader with this line is cut, however good. Wit is allowed when it is Eliot's or a source's, not the annotator's. Plain words: "says", "quotes", "means", "was", "is usually taken as", "Southam suggests", "Eliot's note gives". No "notice", "watch", "decide", "read it once each way".

Readings may be offered, but as readings: "has been read as", "one reading is", "critics have taken this as", or with the critic named. What Eliot said (in a note, a letter, a lecture), what critics made of it, and what the lines do are kept distinct. Eliot's note calling Tiresias "the most important personage in the poem" is the test case.

#### Facts and sources
Every fact on the site was checked against primary sources by the builder. That does not make it right. The scholar checks again, and a change of fact needs a source. Reachable from here (WebFetch or curl through the proxy): the 1922 text on Gutenberg (https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/1321/pg1321.txt; also saved at the path given in your brief); Wikisource (Prothalamion, Marvell, Psalms KJV, Verlaine's Parsifal, Purgatorio V); Gutenberg for Augustine's Confessions in Pusey (#3296) and The Vicar of Wakefield (#2667); Perseus for Ovid Met. III; archive.org for Froude's Elizabeth (reignofelizabeth01frouuoft, p. 244) and Bullen's Day; projekt-gutenberg.org for the Götterdämmerung libretto; Warren's Buddhism in Translations is on archive.org (search inside for "Fire-Sermon"). Lyly's Campaspe songs are on Wikisource or Bartleby. The 1971 facsimile of the drafts (Valerie Eliot), Ricks and McCue (2015), Southam, Rainey, Grover Smith, Kenner, Gardner, Gordon and Crawford are not online in full; use what you know of them and say so ("Southam gives", "Ricks and McCue note", "as I recall it, unverified").

Rights rules of the site, which the notes must keep to: sources before 1930 may be quoted freely. Eliot's own texts after 1929 (letters, the 1946 essay on Pound, the 1956 lecture, the drafts as published in 1971) and Pound's marginalia: describe, or quote a phrase or two, never a passage. The 1922 text's spellings are kept (forc'd, smoothes, to-night).

#### Line numbers
The site counts every line 1 to 434. Eliot's 1922 marginal numbers run one behind from line 346. In Part III the site's numbers match the 1922 margin. Eliot's notes are printed with their 1922 numbers even where they slip; the commentary uses the site's numbers.

#### The files
Records are `key: value` blocks separated by `---`. Fixed fields (id, line, to, anchor, kind, source, image, lang, path, part) must not change. Editable: title, body, text, and quote/trans only for accuracy. Plain text with <i>…</i>, <a href>, <br> allowed; curly quotes where they already are; no Markdown. A body line must not begin with `word:`.
